A Torus-Node Model for Emergent Spacetime,
Gravity, and Gauge Structure: D4 Moves, Level-3
Commensurability, the S4 Ordered-History Algebra,
and a Poisson-Source Interface

Youngik (Tom) Lee

 E-mail: youngik_lee@alumni.brown.edu

Abstract: We construct a model in which low-energy 3 + 1-dimensional spacetime,
gravity, gauge fields and chiral matter all arise from flat two-torus node data through
a single conditional chain. The construction combines a declared finite completion of
bounded geometry with D4 spatial moves, a level-three cusp action, determinant-two Hecke
correspondences and an S4 ordered-history algebra, and leads from the node data to four-
dimensional kinematics, the metric, gauge and matter blocks, and a low-energy effective
action. Each premise and each source-to-continuum condition is stated separately; deriving
the finite completion from an unrestricted Poisson torus source remains open.
     The D4 lattice model selects four dimensions and one oriented temporal direction; with
the stated Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction assumptions, this gives Lorentz signature
(−, +, +, +). The ordered-history commutant supplies the finite gauge factors, which re-
duce after the cutoff-scale central U (1) combinations are removed to SU (3)c × SU (2)L ×
U (1)Y . The resulting chiral module has quantised charges and cancels the local gauge,
mixed gauge–gravitational and global SU (2) anomalies; the choice of three generations is
vacuum data. The canonical finite model yields a conditional Einstein–Hilbert term, while
the induced finite action supplies the gauge-kinetic and matter structures under their stated
assumptions. In the static limit the sector actions give Newton’s and Coulomb’s laws, the
short-range weak force with its Fermi coupling, and the asymptotically free force between
quarks at short distances. For the Wilson action, confinement follows from the decay of
the vortex free energy, which a renormalisation flow through the intermediate couplings
would supply; the mass gap is open in the same range of couplings.
     An observationally constrained specialisation uses measured couplings and declared
matching, threshold and overlap inputs. Its matching equations admit exact gauge-coupling
solutions and, with the stated heavy-state threshold, give the conditional bound mt ≲
173.0–173.9 GeV. These are input-dependent outputs, not parameter-free predictions of
the canonical finite model. Photon masslessness for the integer Wilson action, the non-
perturbative electroweak gap, the physical SU (3) Yang–Mills gap and reflection positivity
of the unrestricted Poisson source remain open, as does the source-to-continuum identifi-
cation itself.

1     Introduction

1.1    The question
The Standard Model and general relativity begin from structures whose empirical success is
not in question but whose origin remains unexplained. Spacetime has four dimensions and
one time direction; the gauge group is SU (3) × SU (2) × U (1); matter occurs in three chiral
generations with anomaly-free charges; the photon, and in general relativity the graviton,
are massless. The question pursued here is whether several of these facts can arise from
one simpler microscopic object rather than being imposed separately.
     That object is a node carrying the data of a flat two-dimensional torus. The nodes are
not placed in a spacetime supplied beforehand; their position data are used to define the
spacetime on which the effective theory is to live. This reversal of viewpoint makes the
hierarchy of assumptions important. Axioms I and D define the minimal model. Axiom M
is added only when the metric is to be dynamical, and the finite source and its completion
are stated as constructions or hypotheses in their own right. The distinction is not cosmetic:
the finite model determines a number of algebraic structures, but it does not determine
the observed couplings, masses or cosmological constant. Those quantities still depend on
vacuum data, continuous moduli and matching or amplitude prescriptions.
     The proposal should therefore be read as a route to a low-energy 3 + 1-dimensional
spacetime with gauge fields and gravity, not as a unique completion of fundamental physics.
Its intended contribution is the manner in which torus data, a Poisson source and a finite
history algebra are joined, together with a sharp account of where that construction stops.
Whenever a continuum or physical conclusion needs more than the finite model provides,
the additional premise is stated at the point of use.

1.2    The physical frameworks in historical context
Each of the four interactions has an established theory of its own, with its own history;
the programmes that try to connect these theories are a separate matter and are described
afterwards.
     Gravity was described by Newtonian mechanics from the seventeenth century until
Einstein’s general relativity (1915–16) replaced it at relativistic scales. General relativity
is a classical theory of spacetime geometry. The present model asks whether a metric and
its gravitational action can emerge from node data; it does not quantise general relativity
by assumption.
     Electromagnetism was formulated classically by Maxwell as a U (1) gauge theory and
completed by quantum electrodynamics in the 1940s. The photon is the massless gauge
boson of the unbroken electromagnetic subgroup. In the present model the Maxwell form
is derived in the infrared, while the statements about the Wilson action and the continuum
remain conditional.
     The weak interaction was initially described by Fermi’s four-fermion theory in the
1930s. It was replaced by a Yang–Mills description and the Glashow–Weinberg–Salam
electroweak theory during 1961–68. The Higgs mechanism and the observed W and Z
bosons established the Standard Model during the 1970s and 1980s. This paper derives a




                                            –3–
candidate finite gauge structure and treats the electroweak mass-gap condition separately
from the established Standard Model phenomenology.
     The strong interaction developed from the colour idea of the 1960s into quantum
chromodynamics in the 1970s. Asymptotic freedom was found in 1973, and Wilson’s lattice
formulation made confinement a nonperturbative question in 1974 [1]. The SU (3)c sector
and its Yang–Mills gap are treated here as a source-to-Wilson problem, not as an already-
proved continuum theorem.
     Electromagnetism and the weak interaction were unified at the electroweak scale in
the 1960s. Adding quantum chromodynamics gives the Standard Model gauge description,
but this is not the same as a verified grand-unified theory: proposals based on a larger
simple group appeared in the 1970s, and proton decay or a definitive high-scale coupling
unification has not been observed.
     Quantum gravity addresses the incompatibility between the classical metric of
general relativity and quantum field theory. Perturbative quantisation led to the non-
renormalisability problem in the 1970s; canonical and loop approaches, asymptotic-safety
programmes and discrete path-integral constructions subsequently explored different
nonperturbative completions. String theory began as a dual-resonance model in 1968,
became a supersymmetric ten-dimensional theory in the 1970s, and received a major
consistency result from anomaly cancellation in 1984. Compactification, branes and
dualities then supplied a broad unification framework, but no unique vacuum or experi-
mental confirmation has been established. The present paper takes a related but distinct
approach: it declares a specific torus source, Poisson placement and finite history algebra,
and analyses them explicitly, labelling every source-to-continuum and mass-gap bridge as
proved, conditional or open.
     Against this background, the torus-node construction is an attempt to obtain from
one microscopic object the spacetime, gravitational, gauge and matter structures that
these theories describe separately.

1.3   Relation to prior work and use of references
The arithmetic language used here has a precise but limited provenance. Connes and
Marcolli’s theory of Q-lattices supplies the commensurability and determinant language
[2]; Diamond–Shurman supplies standard modular-curve and Hecke terminology [3]; and
Serre supplies the finite-group representation facts used when the level-three cusp action
is decomposed [4]. These references provide background definitions and standard results
only. The determinant-two shell, the four-cusp permutation calculation, and the distinc-
tion between the A3 reflection sector and the D4 source are calculated in this paper, not
imported from those works.
     The gauge and gravity discussion is related to noncommutative-geometric models, but
it is not an application of their spectral action. Connes–Lott introduced particle mod-
els from finite noncommutative geometry [5]; Chamseddine–Connes formulated the spec-
tral action [6]; and Chamseddine–Connes–Marcolli developed a gravity–Standard-Model
construction with neutrino mixing [7]. In the present paper the history commutant, the
finite matter bimodule, and all numerical coeﬀicients are derived or declared separately;




                                           –4–
no spectral-action coeﬀicient is silently transferred. The geometric-phase and holonomy
vocabulary is standard differential geometry [8] and Berry phase theory [9]; the scalar
determinant weight in this model is explicitly a character, not an aﬀine cocycle.
    Wilson’s lattice formulation [1], Mondal’s conditional mass-gap proposal [10], and the
constructive references listed in Appendix D are used to delimit the Yang–Mills programme.
Neither reference provides a four-dimensional SU (3) mass-gap theorem, so the source-to-
Wilson bridge and the intermediate-window estimate are the two steps that still have to
be proved.

The model is organised as a sequence of declared inputs, finite constructions, continuum
interfaces, and conclusions whose assumptions are stated at the point of use. Figure 1 gives
this sequence before the detailed dependency diagram.

1.5   Logical structure of the model
Figure 2 displays the order of the main argument and the supplementary results attached
to it.

Main argument. The argument begins with Axioms I and D. The dynamical-metric
specialisation adds Axiom M, while the finite source and completion are stated separately.
The sequence has eight steps:

1. Axioms and declared inputs (§2). Spacetime is node data and the node quantum state is
   fermionic; Axiom M and the finite source/completion choices are only included on the
   branches where they are explicitly declared.

2. Source (§3). Nodes form a Poisson process, so the number of nodes in a region is its
   volume. The elementary moves of the source are the 24 roots of the lattice D4 , weighted
   by how they change the torus area.

3. Spacetime (§4). Node data have four real dimensions. The area weight singles out one
   axis, which becomes time and carries an arrow. Reflection positivity of the D4 lattice
   model and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction along this axis give signature (1, 3).
   Averaging over Poisson configurations gives exact Euclidean O(4) invariance after the

Figure 2: Logical structure of the model. The orange boxes form the main argument of Part I; their
arrows show the order of derivation or construction and do not by themselves assert unconditional

   common quadratic form is normalised; Lorentz invariance of the reconstructed physi-
   cal theory additionally requires Hypothesis 4 and the remaining Osterwalder–Schrader
   assumptions.

4. Gravity (§5). Couplings depend only on distances and counts, so the induced action is
   invariant under all diffeomorphisms. In four dimensions with fixed volume element its




                                                    –6–
   local low-energy form is unimodular Einstein–Hilbert, and a graviton mass is forbidden.
   Its static limit is Newton’s law.

5. Gauge structure (§6). The spatial moves act on the four cusps of a tetrahedron and
   generate the permutation group S4 . The unitary group of the commutant of the right
   regular action on the resulting algebra of spatial-move sequences, C[S4 ], is U (3)×U (2)×
   U (1) × U (1)′ after a matter-free factor drops out; the four-cusp permutation module
   alone gives only U (1) × U (1). The fermion of Axiom D on wrapped cycles supplies the
   generation bookkeeping; the number of generations is vacuum data.

6. Electromagnetism (§8). The infrared action is Maxwell’s; for charges at rest it gives
   Coulomb’s law. The photon is massless in three comparison models; for the physical
   Wilson action this is Hypothesis 1.

7. Weak interaction (§9). A Higgs doublet breaks SU (2) × U (1) to exactly U (1)em , with
   mW , mZ > 0 and mγ = 0. The massive bosons carry a short-range force, which at low
   energy is Fermi’s interaction.

8. Strong interaction (§10). The factor U (3) contains SU (3)c . With the derived matter
   content, SU (3)c is asymptotically free, and the force between quarks has the Coulomb
   form at short distances. Confinement at long distances is derived from a condition on
   the vortex free energy (§17.5).

Gravity comes before the gauge forces because it uses only the source and spacetime, not
the gauge group.

Supplementary results and specialisations. The following statements attach to par-
ticular steps without becoming premises of the later steps:

• Proved boundaries (from the source). The values of couplings, masses and the cosmo-
  logical constant, and the sizes of the scale hierarchies, are set by moduli or initial data
  and are proved not to be fixed by the axioms (§14).

• Averaged reflection positivity (from spacetime). This is the unitarity of the physical,
  Poisson-averaged theory (Hypothesis 4, §11).

• Dynamical metric (from gravity). With Axiom M the metric becomes dynamical. This
  gives cosmological expansion; a singularity bounce and a Page curve are numerical
  illustrations, and the black-hole entropy ratio is a numerical consistency check (§5.3).

• Matter, vacua and node dynamics (from gauge). Chirality, charge quantisation, anomaly
  cancellation for three-generation content and 76 consistent vacua (§6.5). The waves,
  rotor and resonance on the node torus, and what they can and cannot do (§7).

• Wilson-action photon (from electromagnetism). Hypothesis 1, together with the per-
  sample homogenisation (§8.5, Appendix A).




                                           –7–
• Electroweak gap and Higgs stability (from weak). Hypothesis 2 (§9.3). Stability tests the
  fixed reference inputs (§9.6); continuous observational restrictions and their unresolved
  joint constraints are described in §13.7.

• Yang–Mills gap (from strong). The open physical Yang–Mills gap hypothesis is delimited
  in Section 17 and developed in the follow-up paper together with qualitative one-loop
  universality, the intermediate-window covariance estimate, and the source-to-Wilson
  universality bridge.

Section 12 gives the dependency analysis and verifies, implication by implication, that none
of the four hypotheses is used in the later steps of the main argument.

1.6   How statements are marked
Logical consistency and rigour take precedence over agreement with observation: a result
must first follow from its stated premises, and only then is it compared with data. Observed
facts are kept separate from their interpretation, and numerical evidence is never presented
as a proof. The environment in which a statement appears indicates its standing:

• Theorem, Proposition, Lemma, Corollary: proved; any hypothesis on which the proof
  depends is part of the statement. If the proof uses a computer, the statement says
  computer-assisted, and the computation uses exact integer or rational arithmetic or
  outward-rounded interval arithmetic. If the proof uses a published theorem, the state-
  ment names it.

• Hypothesis: an unproved mathematical statement, used only where it is named.

• Construction: an explicit law that we declare and then analyse. A construction is a
  choice, not a consequence of the axioms.

• Numerical result: floating-point evidence, not a proof.

• No-go result: a proof that a proposed mechanism cannot work.

The algebraic core of the main argument is formalised in Lean 4 [11]. Appendix B explains
how the formal statements correspond to the text and where the numerical results come
from; the detailed tables are in Appendices F–H and in the separately distributed Verifi-
cation Supplement. Each computation used in the text is described by what it computes,
the inputs it starts from, and the bound or obstruction it yields.
     Summaries and tables use six words for the standing of a statement or a number:
derived (proved in the finite model from the axioms and the declared data), conditional
(proved under premises stated with it), input-dependent (a number that depends on de-
clared matching, threshold or overlap inputs), interval-certified (a numerical bound proved
with outward-rounded interval or exact arithmetic), diagnostic (floating-point evidence
without a proof) and open (a named hypothesis or a step not yet proved).

guished from conclusions of the minimal model.


2.1       The node
Definition 2.1 (Torus node). 1 A node is a tuple X = (A, τ, ϕ, ψ, w) with

• A > 0, the area of the torus, and ρ := log(A/A0 ) ∈ R its dimensionless logarithmic area
  relative to a fixed reference area A0 > 0;

• τ ∈ H = {τ ∈ C : Im τ > 0}, the shape (modulus) of the torus;

• ϕ ∈ R/2πZ, the rotor phase;

• ψ, the quantum state of the node;

• w ∈ Z2 , the winding numbers.

The node carries the flat torus Tτ2 = C/(Z + τ Z) rescaled to area A = A0 eρ . Two shapes
τ and γτ = (aτ + b)/(cτ + d) with γ ∈ SL2 (Z) describe the same torus. The symmetry
group of the data (A, τ ) is GL2 (R) acting on the lattice Z + τ Z (det rescales the area, SL2
changes the shape).

    The unwound phase ϕ̃ ∈ R is the lift of ϕ to the universal cover. The triple (ϕ̃, ρ, τ ) is
the position data of a node; ψ and w are internal data.

2.2       The axioms
Each named axiom below is a self-contained model statement: its symbols and quantifiers
are specified in the statement itself, and no theorem, lemma or external result is part of the
axiom. References in the explanatory text after an axiom are consequences or clarifications,
not additional content of that axiom.
                                                                  f = Rφ̃ ×Rρ ×Hτ , with
Axiom I (Identification). The marked position data form the cover M
physical area A = A0 e . Physical spacetime is the modular quotient M = M/P
                      ρ                                                    f SL2 (Z),
where the group acts only on τ by fractional linear transformations. Thus a point is an

spacetime region is a set of such equivalence classes.

Axiom D (Fermionic node state). Let ID be a fixed finite component set, including the
independent barred generators whenever a Dirac action is used. For every node x the
variables ψα (x), α ∈ ID , denote all these Grassmann generators and satisfy ψα (x)ψβ (y) =
−ψβ (y)ψα (x). For a finite node set X, fix an ordering of X × ID and define the finite-

If the source supplies an infinite locally finite configuration Π, take an exhaustion by finite-

The infinite-volume fermionic state is defined only when limn→∞ hOin exists for every
cylinder observable and is independent of the chosen exhaustion. No infinite Berezin
product, existence or uniqueness of this limit, or spin–statistics theorem is postulated by
this axiom.
     Axiom D is a definition. We tested whether it could be replaced by a weaker statement
(that ψ carries half-integer spin under frame rotations, with the spin–statistics theorem
giving Fermi statistics). The answer is no: the only candidate source of spinors in the D4
structure is triality, and triality is a geometric rotation in W (F4 ) ⊂ O(4), so it does not
produce a spinor transformation law (§7, No-go 7.11). Axioms I and D are the whole input
of the minimal model; Axiom M is an additional, explicitly separated dynamical-metric
branch.
Axiom M (Dynamical metric). Let M     f = R×R×H and let M = M/P     f SL2 (Z) be the four-
real-dimensional modular orbifold of Axiom I. Fix a positive orbifold four-form ν on M,
equivalently a modular-invariant positive four-form on the cover. The admissible metrics
are positive orbifold Riemannian metrics g satisfying dvolg = ν, equivalently invariant
metrics on the cover, and every ν-preserving orbifold relabelling is a gauge symmetry. For
each admissible g, nodes are sampled from a Poisson process of intensity λν; all links
and node-field couplings depend on g only through geodesic distances. If ZΠ,g denotes the
resulting node-field partition function, the metric is integrated with weight e−Γ[g] , where
one of the two explicitly defined choices

                     Γq [g] = EΠ [− log ZΠ,g ],    Γa [g] = − log EΠ [ZΠ,g ]              (2.3)

is fixed as part of the model. No Einstein–Hilbert form, field equation or low-energy
approximation is included in this axiom.




                                             – 12 –
    Axiom M is the standard starting point of general relativity and of most non-string
approaches to quantum gravity. It is not a consequence of Axioms I and D (Proposition 5.7).
We call the model with Axioms I, D and M the canonical finite model. The main argument
in Fig. 2 applies to both models; this model adds the specialisation of §5.3.

2.3   The four hypotheses
The following statements are not proved. Each is a precise mathematical statement, and
each is used only where it is named. They are not axioms: each is true or false as stated,
and the model does not choose.

Hypothesis 1 (Photon masslessness for the Wilson action). For the integer-flux represen-
tation of the physical (Wilson-induced) U (1) action on the model complex, the variance of
every linear flux observable is bounded below by (1 − c′ ) times the Gaussian comparison
variance, for some c′ < 1 (§8.5).

Hypothesis 2 (Electroweak gap at fixed background). Fix an electromagnetic background
A with field strength |FA | ≤ δ, where δ < m2W /(2e). Then the connected correlations of
the heavy fields W ± , Z, h, conditional on A, decay exponentially, uniformly in A and in
the volume (§9.3).

Hypothesis 3 (Yang–Mills mass gap). Four-dimensional lattice SU (3) Yang–Mills theory
at the physical bare coupling has a volume-uniform spectral gap of its transfer matrix (the
open problem delimited in Section 17 and the follow-up paper).

Hypothesis 4 (Averaged reflection positivity). In the physical (Poisson) model, the
Schwinger functions of relational observables, averaged over node configurations, are
reflection positive (§11).

     The Yang–Mills hypothesis separates three logically different obligations. The first is
qualitative one-loop universality inside the Wilson weak-coupling chain. The second is the
source-to-Wilson universality bridge: the positive closed-walk action induced by the torus
model at the decoupling scale must be shown to lie in the same continuum universality
class as the Wilson action. The third is a volume-uniform cross-link covariance bound in
the intermediate window. The first is an input to the Wilson chain, whereas the second is
model-specific and is not proved by that chain; the third is the remaining Wilson-window
estimate. None of these is an extra axiom, and none is silently transferred from the SU (2)
Wilson problem to the SU (3) theory generated by the microscopic torus source.

Conditional source-to-continuum theorem. The finite argument should not be read
as an unconditional theorem about an infinite-volume torus source. The conditional source-
to-continuum theorem uses one and the same microscopic family
                                 −1 −SΛ,a (X)
                    µΛ,a (dX) = ZΛ,a e        νΛ,a (dX),     Λ ⋐ aZ4 ,                 (2.4)

By a state–field map we mean the map that assigns a lattice or continuum field observable
to each microscopic source state; it is the interface through which source variables enter




                                          – 13 –
correlation functions. The relation domain and temporal transfer are also part of X. Its
eight source-to-continuum conditions are: an explicit microscopic source; uniform stability
and moments; physical reflection positivity; an exact same-measure RG map; retention
of the spatial-move sequence, coframe and gauge sectors or a summable Feshbach com-
plement; exact projected Ward identities with summable remainders; continuum tightness
and equicontinuity; and pole/vertex identification with Maxwell/YM, coframe/Einstein
and chiral-matter tensors. Here a Feshbach complement is the Schur-complement effective
operator obtained after eliminating a discarded sector; summability controls the cumulative
effect of those eliminated modes. If these eight conditions hold for one common source, a
subsequence has a reflection-positive OS continuum state containing the retained sectors,
with the Ward limits and identifications specified by the fifth through eighth conditions.
These are conditions of a theorem, not extra axioms of the minimal model.
     The unrestricted Poisson–torus source does not verify these eight conditions. In partic-
ular, the relation skeleton does not select a unique transport Ug , state–field map, measure,
interaction coeﬀicients or source-identical RG block. Two explicit transports with the same
skeleton have different Hessians, and invariant positive quartic terms preserve the tested
finite symmetries while changing nonlinear vertices. Thus the theorem yields its continuum
conclusion only after a microscopic selection rule is supplied.

Mathematical scope of the named hypotheses. None of the four hypotheses has
been proved in full, and none has been weakened merely to obtain a proof. Their finite
sub-statements are proved in the sections cited: the electroweak covariance input is interval-
certified and the declared finite electroweak realisation passes its quantitative tests (§9.4);
reflection positivity holds for the declared finite source of the canonical finite model and
for the explicit comparison sources (§11); the photon is massless in the comparison models
(§8); and uniqueness with exponential decay holds for Wilson SU (N ) lattice measures at
strong coupling (Section 17). What remains open in each case is the transfer to the unre-
stricted physical object: the signed transfer to the integer Wilson action (Hypothesis 1);
the interacting gauge–Higgs polymer norm, uniqueness, cutoff removal and the 53-step
composition (Hypothesis 2); the four-dimensional SU (3) gap together with the source-to-
Wilson bridge (Hypothesis 3); and reflection positivity of the unrestricted Poisson torus
measure (Hypothesis 4).
     Two further statements have a different logical role.

• The Higgs-stability condition (§9.6) requires the Higgs potential to be bounded below
  at all scales up to the cutoff. It is not a hypothesis about mathematics. It is a physical
  requirement that a vacuum either meets or fails, and we compute which vacua meet it.

• The upper bound in per-sample homogenisation (Theorem A.5) is proved conditionally
  on four published percolation theorems. It is not used in the main argument.

2.5   Three readings of the axioms
Three interpretations follow from the axioms and recur in the paper.

1. Spacetime is counted. Since node count equals volume, a four-volume is a number of
   nodes. The metric is therefore a relational object: two configurations related by a
   diffeomorphism have the same probability law (Theorem 5.1).

2. Forces arise from source-move symmetries. Gauge groups are not put in by hand. They
   are the unitary groups of the commutant of the right regular action on the algebra
   of ordered spatial-move sequences, restricted to the blocks that carry charged matter
   (Theorem 6.4).

3. Values are vacuum data. The axioms force structure (dimensions, groups, represen-
   tations) but leave numbers (couplings) as moduli of a vacuum. This split is itself a
   theorem (Theorem 6.17).

Figure 5: Torus-node geometry at three levels. (a) A schematic view of node locations; the Poisson

    The Poisson law specifies an ensemble rather than one deterministic placement. A
particular configuration is one sample Π from that law; the model’s statements about
density and independence refer to the ensemble, while all subsequent finite constructions
are applied to a selected realisation or to the corresponding Poisson average.

3.1      Poisson placement and count = volume
Definition 3.1 (Source configuration). 2 Let M be the space of position data with a volume
measure vol. The source is a Poisson point process Π on M with intensity λ vol. For disjoint
regions A1 , . . . , Ak the counts N (Ai ) = #(Π ∩ Ai ) are independent, and N (A) is Poisson
with mean λ vol(A).

Proposition 3.2 (Poisson law from void probabilities). Let Π be a simple point process
on M whose void probabilities are P (N (A) = 0) = e−λ vol(A) for all bounded Borel sets A.
    2
   Lean 4 counterpart of Definition              3.1     (Source   configuration):       TorusModel.Poisson.
finitePoissonMeasure; see Appendix F.




                                                      – 16 –
Then Π is the Poisson process of Definition 3.1. Thus the source law is fixed once count
= volume is imposed on empty regions; the proposition characterises the Poisson law but
does not derive the void-probability input.

Proof. By Rényi’s theorem [12] (§D.1.7), the law of a simple point process is determined
by its void probabilities. The Poisson process with intensity λ vol has exactly these void
probabilities.

    The phrase count = volume means EN (A) = λ vol(A), with relative fluctuations
N (A)−1/2 . We use two standard facts about Poisson processes throughout.

Lemma 3.3 (Mapping theorem). 3 Let ψ : M → M ′ be measurable and suppose the image
measure ψ∗ (λ vol) has no atoms. Then ψ(Π) is a Poisson process with intensity ψ∗ (λ vol).
In particular, if ψ is a diffeomorphism between Riemannian manifolds (M, ψ ∗ g) and (M, g),
then ψ maps a Poisson process of intensity λ dvolψ∗ g to one of intensity λ dvolg .

Proof. Put µ = λ vol and ν = ψ∗ µ. The image point measure is, by definition,
                                          X

                                                x∈Π

For pairwise disjoint bounded Borel sets B1 , . . . , Bk ⊂ M ′ , its counts satisfy

                                    ψ(Π)(Bi ) = Π(ψ −1 Bi ).                              (3.2)

The sets ψ −1 Bi are pairwise disjoint. The defining property of a Poisson process therefore
gives independence, and gives
                                                      
                     Π(ψ −1 Bi ) ∼ Poisson µ(ψ −1 Bi ) = Poisson(ν(Bi )) .                (3.3)

These joint count laws are exactly those of a Poisson process with intensity ν, so they
establish the claimed push-forward law.
    It remains to check simplicity, because different source points could in principle have
the same image. Let B ⊂ M ′ have finite ν(B). If ν(B) = 0, the image process has no
points in B almost surely and there is nothing to prove. Assume henceforth that ν(B) > 0
and condition on the event that the image contains n points in B. The n source points
contributing to this count have independent locations, and their image locations have the
normalised measure ν|B /ν(B). Since ν has no atoms, for every pair i 6= j,

There are only finitely many pairs, so the union bound gives probability zero of any collision
in B. Exhausting M ′ by countably many finite-ν sets proves that ψ(Π) is simple almost
surely.

Lemma 3.4 (Spatial independence). 4 If M = M− t M+ , the restrictions Π ∩ M− and
Π ∩ M+ are independent Poisson processes.
Proof. This is the independence of counts in disjoint sets, applied to all finite families of
subsets of M− and M+ simultaneously.

3.2   The elementary moves
Two node tori are related if one lattice is an index-2 sublattice or superlattice of the
other, up to rotation. Writing a lattice basis change as a quaternion, the index-2 relations
are the Hurwitz integers of norm 2. There are 24 of them, and in suitable coordinates
x = (x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 ) on the four-dimensional data space they are the 24 roots of D4 :

                               R = {±ei ± ej : 1 ≤ i < j ≤ 4}.                             (3.6)

The unit group of the Hurwitz order (the binary tetrahedral group, of order 24) acts
transitively on R.

and w = 12 on the six roots +(ei + ej ).

Remark 3.8 (On the value β = 1). The value β = 1 is part of the definition of the source.
It is specified by the source kernel and is not derived. Counting the index-n sublattices

coincidence is suggestive, but we do not use it. The value is therefore an input, not a

3.3   Arithmetic provenance: GL2 , Q-lattices and Hecke moves
There is a useful arithmetic interpretation of the preceding source kernel. A Q-lattice is
a pair (Λ, φ) consisting of a rank-two lattice Λ ⊂ R2 and a marking φ : Q2 /Z2 → QΛ/Λ.
Commensurable marked lattices are related by rational matrices, and the resulting com-
mensurability group is described by GL+  2 (Q) (with the two orientations giving GL2 (Q)).
The determinant records the covolume change, while the upper/lower half-plane coordinate
records the shape. This is the Q-lattice framework of Connes and Marcolli [2].
    In the present model this language supplies provenance and a dictionary, not a new
axiom. The index-two Hurwitz relations share the determinant two of the determinant-two
Hecke correspondences, but the 24 relations are not in bijection with the three determinant-
two Hecke cosets (Proposition 13.2); the Hecke reading supplies the determinant character
and, at level three, the S4 monodromy, while the D4 moves remain a separately declared
source. Here ρ = log(A/A0 ) is the area coordinate and a relation of determinant d changes
  6
     Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 3.6 (Compatibility of area and D4 valuations): TorusModel.D4.d4_
chart_compatibility_full; see Appendix F.
   7
     Lean 4 counterpart of Definition 3.7 (Determinant valuation and source weight): TorusModel.D4.
source_weight_definition_exact; see Appendix F.




                                                – 19 –
it by ∆ρ = log d; the source factor 2−βt(r) is the corresponding multiplicative determinant
character at the defining value β = 1. It is a scalar weight, not an aﬀine cocycle: no vector-
valued translation part or aﬀine group action is introduced here [8]. The shape coordinate
is acted on by the finite A3 /S4 sector described above. The conjugate unwound phase ϕ̃
is a clock coordinate; identifying it with ρ would be an additional dictionary choice, not a
consequence of the Q-lattice notation.
     We do not restrict Poisson nodes to CM points, identify physical adjacency with all
Hecke correspondences, or import Bost–Connes equilibrium and Galois statements. In
particular, the specified source does not prove an edge–root/Hecke dictionary, a uniform
Hecke-compatible renormalisation map ΦRG , or uniqueness of the microscopic measure from
this arithmetic description. Source-identifiability and completion analyses sharpen this
boundary: the relation skeleton admits inequivalent transports and invariant interaction
completions with different Hessians and higher vertices. These are explicit source-level
obligations rather than hidden inputs to the infrared claims.

drift. The three remaining directions are equivalent under S4 .
Proof. Proposition 3.9(c) gives the second-moment operator C = 13P⊥ + 15P0 and the
drift b = − 29 1. Because P0 and P⊥ are complementary orthogonal projections, Cv = 15v
for v ∈ R1 and Cv = 13v for v ∈ 1⊥ . Their ranks are respectively one and three, so
there are no further eigenspaces. Moreover P⊥ b = 0 and P0 b = b, so the drift has no
spatial component. Finally the coordinate-permutation representation of S4 fixes 1 and
acts irreducibly on 1⊥ ; hence it cannot distinguish any line inside the latter subspace. The
eigenvalue ratio is 15/13.

Remark 3.11 (Which data direction is time). The distinguished direction is the one along
which t(r), i.e. the logarithm of the torus area, changes. The generator of the distinguished
flow is therefore log det, acting in the area direction ρ. Its conjugate clock reading is the
unwound phase ϕ̃. The infrared conclusions below do not depend on how the remaining
three data coordinates are labelled, because the statistical symmetry acting on them is the
full S4 .

distinguished axis is the coordinate axis y0 ; the twelve roots orthogonal to it are ±ei ± ej
with 1 ≤ i < j ≤ 3 (the image of the difference roots), and the twelve roots with a time
component are ±e0 ± ei (the image of ±(ei + ej )). This frame is used for the dispersion
(Lemma 4.10) and for the reflection of Theorem 4.5.

3.4   The literal source kernel and the completion data
The relation kernel of the unrestricted source is, for g ∈ GL+
                                                             2 (Q) relating node i to node j,

             Wij (g) = (det g)−β e−µ dH (τj ,gτi ) e−ν(ρj −ρi −log det g) ,

The finite D4 source used above keeps only the determinant-two Hecke shell. The next
theorem shows that this restriction, or something equivalent to it, is necessary.

   Lean 4 counterpart of Corollary 3.10 (A distinguished axis): TorusModel.beta_one_exact; see Ap-

Theorem 3.12 (The unrestricted kernel does not define a source). 10
                                P
 (a) For every β, µ, ν, the sum g∈GL+ (Q) Wij (g) diverges for coincident endpoint data
                                       2
     τi = τj = i and ρi = ρj . Consequently the proposed kernel cannot be normalised on
     the full endpoint space.

 (b) For β 6= 0 the kernel is not inverse-even: Wij (H)/Wji (H −1 ) = 2−2β for H =
     diag(2, 1) at equal endpoint data.

 (c) The determinant part of the kernel is the scalar character g 7→ (det g)−β , so de-
     terminant weights alone have commuting image. A non-abelian S4 sector therefore
     requires an additional finite quotient or ordered-history transport; it is not supplied
     by the scalar kernel.

 (d) The kernel does not determine a state measure. Two state measures with the same
     geometric kernel give different push-forwards to the history sector.

Consequently any completion of the source must specify (i) a finite or summable relation
domain, (ii) a reversible (inverse-even) measure, (iii) an ordered-history transport, and
(iv) uniform tail control of the history renormalisation. None of these follows from the
kernel.

     (b) With det H = 2 and equal endpoint data, the two exponential factors are equal

     (c) The determinant contribution is χβ (g) = (det g)−β and satisfies χβ (gh) =

The non-abelian finite history sector must therefore be supplied by an additional quotient
or ordered-history transport; the level–three Hecke action described in Proposition 13.2 is
one such finite source of the permutation group.

    (d) The displayed kernel depends only on (τi , ρi , τj , ρj ) and not on the state variable.
Fix two distinct state values ψ0 6= ψ1 and take η0 = δψ0 and η1 = δψ1 on the same geometric
configuration space. The two products Wij (g)η0 and Wij (g)η1 have identical geometric
kernels but different state push-forwards. Thus the kernel alone cannot determine the state
measure. The two explicit completions in §12.3 provide a second, Hessian-level witness.

     The finite source of Definition 3.7 is one completion. It satisfies (i) by keeping the
determinant-two shell, and (iii) through the history algebra of §6. In the declared fi-
nite source of the canonical finite model, (ii) is supplied by its inverse-symmetric product
measure and (iv) by the finite history contraction (Proposition 6.8); for the unrestricted
Poisson source the corresponding statements remain part of Hypothesis 4 and of the open
source-to-continuum conditions. Appendix E, §E.2, makes the finite dictionary explicit:
twelve directed spatial D4 roots map two-to-one to the six transpositions, the 49 elements
of D∗ = H∗ × E∗ are one-step history–clock marks, and path multiplication gives the 24-
state S4 history fibre (Appendix E.2). The seven clock marks are declared completion data
and are not derived from the other twelve D4 roots. The source-to-continuum statement
of the model is therefore conditional on this choice of completion, as summarised in §12.1.

3.5   The cuboctahedron is the spatial section
Figure 6 displays the roots in the three determinant-valuation layers. The twelve difference

Figure 6: The 24 elementary moves separated by t(r) = 12 i ri . Each panel is an orthographic
view of the corresponding roots projected onto 1⊥ , using the orthonormal coordinates (r1 + r2 −

                             √
roots ±(ei − ej ) have length 2 and lie in the three-dimensional space 1⊥ . They are the
vertices of a cuboctahedron, the root polytope of A3 . The 24 roots of D4 are the vertices
of the 24-cell. The cuboctahedron is therefore exactly the spatial section of the 24-cell, i.e.
the roots orthogonal to the distinguished axis. It enters the model in three places:
1. as the spatial part of the dispersion (Lemma 4.10). The cuboctahedron alone is a
                                                         P 4
   spherical 3-design, so it leaves a quartic anisotropy  i ki ; together with the twelve
   remaining roots the 24-cell (a 5-design) cancels it;




                                              – 23 –
2. as the generator of S4 , and hence of the gauge group (Theorem 6.4);

3. as the nearest-neighbour shell of the face-centred cubic time slice used in the black-hole
   entanglement computation (Proposition 5.10).

A standalone 12-vertex cuboctahedral graph is not part of the model. The construction
uses the full D4 root system; the cuboctahedron appearing here is only its A3 spatial
section, as stated in Appendix C.

3.6   The canonical finite model
The finite theorems in the rest of the paper are proved for one fixed model, which this
subsection specifies. It completes the torus relation data by declaration; Theorem 3.12
shows that the unrestricted kernel cannot supply such a model by itself. Several of its
ingredients—the ordered-history algebra and its commutant, the blocking operators and
the history fibre—are only specified here. Their analysis comes later, in §6 and Appendix E,
and the terms are summarised in Table 1.
     The paper uses this model together with a few related constructions, and it helps to see
them side by side. The minimal model of §2 keeps Axioms I and D on a fixed background
metric. The canonical finite model adds Axiom M, the level-three Hecke domain, the
declared finite D4 source and the completion package. Three kinds of construction are
built from it: the finite certification lattice, a single 256-site candidate on which the finite
checks are carried out; the factorised control and projected coupled models, which add
metric couplings for comparison; and the observationally constrained specialisation of §13.7,
which fixes the free moduli by data. The source-measure completion proposal is the literal
reading of the unrestricted Poisson source, and its identification with the canonical finite
model is open. Figure 24 and Table 7 show the complete arrangement.

These entries are fixed inputs, not consequences of the raw torus relation. Reflection Θ
is the lattice reflection in the y0 plane; it reverses oriented links, sends group variables to
their inverses, reflects the coframe and Higgs fields, and applies the standard conjugate-
linear fermion reflection. The crossing terms are required to use the Gram factorisation
stated in Chapter 11. This requirement is part of the finite-model definition and is what
distinguishes it from the unrestricted Poisson source.
Proposition 3.16 (Well-defined finite model). For every finite L, the bosonic partition
function is finite and strictly positive, and the Berezin functional of every polynomial
cylinder observable is finite. If ZL,a 6= 0, the corresponding normalised chiral correlators
are therefore defined. The history blocking operators
                                   1X
                              P =        L(ij) ,   Mt = et(P −I)                      (3.27)
                                   6
                                         i<j

form a same-space Markov semigroup and commute with the right S4 action. They map
probability laws on the 24-state cumulative history fibre (whose relation to the seven one-
step marks is set out in Appendix E.2) to laws on that same fibre; they do not leave fixed
the one-step history law µH on the seven elements of H∗ .
Proof. The group and history factors are compact and have unit total mass. On the pinned
clock space the quadratic form has smallest eigenvalue κt 4 sin2 (π/L) > 0. The coframe
quadratic form is strictly positive because m2e > 0, and SH → +∞ quartically as kHk → ∞.
Their integrals and all polynomial moments are therefore finite. A finite Berezin integral is
algebraic, although it need not be positive or nonzero. Finally Mt Ms = e(t+s)(P −I) because
both exponentials have the same generator; P is a central transposition class sum, so it
                                                                 P
commutes with every right regular action. Moreover Mt = e−t n≥0 tn P n /n! is a convex
combination of stochastic matrices. It therefore preserves positivity and total mass on the
same finite history space. It generally spreads the one-step support through S4 , which is
why the one-step alphabet and the cumulative history fibre are kept distinct.

4        Emergence of 3+1 spacetime

We work with two models of the source. The D4 lattice model places the nodes on the
                         P
lattice D4 = {x ∈ Z4 : i xi even}, with couplings along the roots. The physical model
places them by the Poisson process of Definition 3.1, with couplings that depend only on
distances. The lattice model is used for exact statements at the lattice scale. The physical
model is the theory: its large-distance behaviour is what the model predicts.

model, reflection positivity makes this direction the Euclidean reconstruction axis and the source
drift gives it an orientation. The corresponding statement for the physical Poisson source still
requires averaged reflection positivity.

Figure 8: Coordinate-level construction of spacetime. The four real position coordinates split
into one area direction and the three-dimensional S4 -symmetric shape plane; reflection posi-

Proof. The unwound phase ϕ̃ ∈ R and area ρ ∈ R supply one real coordinate each, while
τ ∈ H supplies two. Their product is therefore a connected smooth manifold of real
dimension 1 + 1 + 2 = 4. The state ψ and winding w are fibre data, not coordinates.

Corollary 4.2 (The modular quotient is an orbifold). After the modular identification in
Axiom I, physical spacetime M = M/P  f SL2 (Z) is a real four-dimensional orbifold. Its
complement of the elliptic fixed-point strata is a manifold.

Proof. The central matrices {±I} in SL2 (Z) act identically on H, so the effective modular
action is that of P SL2 (Z). This action is discrete and has only finite stabilisers in H: away
from the elliptic orbits a suﬀiciently small disc has disjoint nontrivial translates, whereas
at an elliptic point a small disc is divided by a finite cyclic stabiliser. The quotient charts
are consequently U in the first case and U /Cm in the second, which is precisely a four-
dimensional orbifold atlas after multiplication by the two real factors. No cusp is added
because τ ranges over H, not its compactification. Thus all local differential calculations
may be made on the cover and descended equivariantly; they do not turn the orbifold
points into manifold points.

4.2    One time axis and its arrow
Theorem 4.3 (Time axis and arrow of time). 14 In the source of Definition 3.7 exactly
one direction of the data space is distinguished: the direction 1 along which the torus area
changes. Along it the diffusion constant differs from the other three by the factor 15/13,
and the source has a non-zero drift. The other three directions are permuted by S4 and
carry no drift. The drift fixes an orientation of the distinguished axis.
                                           P
Proof. This is Corollary 3.10. The drift r w(r)r = − 92 1 is non-zero and parallel to 1, so
it orients this axis. The other three directions span 1⊥ , where the second-moment matrix
is the multiple 13P⊥ of the identity and the drift vanishes. The group S4 generated by
the difference-root reflections acts on 1⊥ by the standard three-dimensional representation,
which is irreducible. So no direction in 1⊥ is distinguished.
 14
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 4.3 (Time axis and arrow of time): TorusModel.beta_one_exact; see
Appendix F.




                                                    – 29 –
4.3    Reflection positivity and signature
Work in the time-adapted frame y = (y0 , y1 , y2 , y3 ) of Remark 3.11, in which the distin-
guished axis is the coordinate axis y0 . Let θ be the reflection of the lattice-model lattice
through the hyperplane {y0 = 0} (a layer of sites). We call a measure on fields reflection
positive (RP) if E[θF F ] ≥ 0 for every function F of the fields at sites with y0 ≥ 0.

Lemma 4.4 (Locality of the D4 moves). 15 In the time-adapted frame every root r ∈ R has
|r0 | ≤ 1. Every edge and every triangle of the D4 clique complex lies in two adjacent layers
{y0 = k} ∪ {y0 = k + 1}, hence in one of the closed half-spaces {y0 ≥ 0} or {y0 ≤ 0}.

Proof. The entries of a root are in {0, ±1}. If three sites are pairwise joined by roots
and two of them were in layers y0 = −1 and y0 = +1, their difference would have time
coordinate 2, which is not a root entry.

    The finite certification lattice is the explicitly enumerated finite state space, selector,
measure and action used to test the stated criteria; it is a constructed finite representative,
not a continuum lattice limit.

Theorem 4.5 (Reflection positivity of the finite certification lattice). 16 Consider any
measure on bosonic site and link fields of the finite certification lattice (the 256-site lattice
described in §13) of the form
                             1              X            Y
                       dµ = exp −                      Sc      d(fields),                   (4.1)
                            Z
                                               edges, triangles c

where each Sc is a real function of the fields on the simplex c, invariant under θ when
θc = c, and with Sθc = Sc ◦ θ. Then µ is reflection positive, at every value of the couplings.
In particular this holds for the Gaussian scalar field with precision matrix I + LD4 , and for
the gauge measure obtained by integrating out bosonic matter.

Proof. By Lemma 4.4 every simplex lies in a closed half-space. We assign the simplices
contained in the plane {y0 = 0} to a plane term S0 , and each remaining simplex to the
side that contains it. This gives S = S+ + S0 + θS+ , where S+ depends only on fields in
{y0 ≥ 0} and S0 only on fields in the plane. We condition on the plane fields φ0 . Given
φ0 , the fields on the two open sides are independent, because no simplex joins them. Their
conditional laws are images of each other under θ. Hence, for F supported in {y0 ≥ 0},
                                       Z
                                                     2
                            E[θF F ] =     E[F | φ0 ] dµ(φ0 ) ≥ 0.                    (4.2)

For the Gaussian case, I + LD4 has diagonal entries 25 and off-diagonal absolute row sums
24. It is strictly diagonally dominant and therefore positive definite, so the measure exists.

Formal scope. The formal counterpart listed in the Verification Supplement verifies only
the non-negativity of the finite Gram quadratic form. The simplex factorisation, con-

mathematical steps of the paper, not claims established by that declaration.
     The area weights w(r) of Definition 3.7 are not θ-invariant: θ exchanges the weights 2
and 21 . They therefore do not enter the reflection-positive measure µ of Theorem 4.5. Their
role is to select the axis and its orientation through the source diffusion (Theorem 4.3). In
the declared finite source of the canonical finite model the reflection Θ is part of the source
data, and finite reflection positivity is proved there (§11).
Remark 4.6 (Fermions). For fermions on a lattice, reflection positivity depends on the
hopping law and the boundary conditions (antiperiodic in time), and Theorem 4.5 does
not apply directly. On random nodes, a numerical test of a hopping law with the form of
the four-dimensional Dirac propagator finds reflection positivity up to truncation effects
that vanish as the cutoff tail is removed. We include the fermionic sector in Hypothesis 4.

Theorem 4.7 (Signature). 17 Let the infinite-volume Schwinger hierarchy satisfy the cor-
rected Osterwalder–Schrader assumptions: tempered-distribution regularity and the uniform
growth bound (E0), full Euclidean covariance with the appropriate spin representation (E1),
positive-time reflection positivity (E2), permutation symmetry or fermionic graded symme-
try together with the reality condition (E3), and clustering under large spacelike translations
(E4). Assume that the distinguished reflection is the E2 time reflection. Reconstruction
along that axis then gives a Hilbert space with a vacuum and a positive self-adjoint Hamil-
tonian. The reconstructed spacetime has one time direction (the distinguished axis) and
three space directions: signature (1, 3).

Proof. By Theorem 4.5 the declared finite scalar measure has the E2 inequality across
planes orthogonal to the distinguished axis. This does not prove E0, E1, E3, E4, the
infinite-volume passage, or fermionic graded positivity; those are explicit premises of the
theorem. Under all of them, the corrected Osterwalder–Schrader theorem [14, 15] (§D.1.4)
gives a Hilbert space, a vacuum and a contraction semigroup e−tH , H ≥ 0, in that direc-
tion. The reconstruction analytically continues only the reflected direction, so the resulting
signature has exactly one minus sign. That this direction is the distinguished axis is The-
orem 4.3: it is the only direction that the source treats differently.
                                                                   P
Theorem 4.8 (Annealed reflection positivity). 18 Let E(λ, u) = b λb |∂b u|2 be a Gaussian
energy with random couplings λb ≥ 0 that are independent, identically distributed, and
 17
     Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 4.7 (Signature): TorusModel.lorentz_signature_core; see Ap-
pendix F.
  18
     Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 4.8 (Annealed reflection positivity): TorusModel.annealed_RP; see
Appendix F.




                                              – 31 –
reflection symmetric in law. Then the annealed measure P (dλ) e−E(λ,u) du is reflection
positive.

Proof. A bond b that crosses the reflection plane is mapped to itself, and its coupling
λb is a reflection-invariant variable. Condition on all crossing couplings. The remaining
couplings on the two sides are independent and reflection images of each other in law. For
                                                               P            − 2
fixed crossing couplings, the crossing part of e−E is exp(− b λb |u+   b − ub | ). Its exponent
is −λb (|u+ |2 + |u− |2 ) + 2λb u+ u− , and the cross term 2λb u+ u− is a positive-semidefinite
kernel. Its exponential is positive definite by the Schur product theorem. Mixing over the
crossing couplings with a positive measure preserves positive definiteness. The conditioning
argument of Theorem 4.5 then applies.

4.4   One light cone and the D4 dispersion
Proposition 4.9 (Common light cone). 19 Suppose the Euclidean parent action is invariant
under the full automorphism group W (F4 ) = Aut(D4 ), including the clock. Then the
quadratic O(p2 ) invariants of scalar, Dirac, gauge and (transverse-diffeomorphism-reduced)
graviton fields are each one-dimensional, and all species share one light cone. With only
W (D4 ), the graviton has three independent invariants.

Proof. Let Vp be the four-dimensional momentum representation and Vs the represen-
tation carried by a species. A quadratic two-derivative term is an element of (Vs∗ ⊗
Vs∗ ⊗ Sym2 Vp∗ )W (F4 ) , after the Dirac, gauge and transverse constraints appropriate to that
species have been imposed. Averaging the character of this tensor product over W (F4 )
gives multiplicity one for each of the four constrained spaces. Hence its momentum depen-
dence is a species-dependent constant times the unique invariant Qµν pµ pν . The constant
changes the residue of a propagator but not the zero set Qµν pµ pν = 0; after continuation,
the four species therefore have the same characteristic cone. Repeating the character aver-
age for W (D4 ) gives multiplicity three in the graviton space. These integer multiplicities
are obtained exactly over Q; the numerical character sum agrees within 6 × 10−13 and is
only a check.

(k0 , k1 , k2 , k3 ) in the time-adapted frame of Remark 3.11, so that the time axis is
e0 . Give weight wt to the twelve roots ±e0 ± ei and weight ws to the twelve roots
±ei ± ej (1 ≤ i < j ≤ 3; these are the cuboctahedron). The quartic term of the symbol

4.5   Euclidean symmetry and conditional Lorentz reconstruction
Theorem 4.11 (Euclidean invariance of the Poisson average). 21 Suppose the nodes form
a Poisson process with intensity λ dx, and every coupling kernel, for every species, depends
only on |x − y|Q for one positive quadratic form Q. (The source drift orients the time axis
but is not part of these distance-dependent couplings.) Then the configuration-averaged
correlation functions are invariant under the orthogonal group O(Q). After the linear
change of variables that takes Q to the identity they are O(4) invariant, so the averaged
Euclidean dispersion has no deterministic rotational violation at any order. If, in addition,
the averaged Schwinger functions satisfy the Osterwalder–Schrader hypotheses, including
Hypothesis 4, their reconstruction is Lorentz invariant.

Proof. The ratio 15/13 of Theorem 4.3 is an anisotropy of Q. Let L be linear with
Q(L−1 x) = |x|2 . Since L multiplies volumes by the constant | det L|, Lemma 3.3 shows that
L(Π) is again a homogeneous Poisson process, with intensity λ/| det L|. Let R ∈ O(4). It
preserves volume, so R(LΠ) and LΠ have the same law. The graph, the action and every
correlation function are built from distances |x−y| alone, so their laws are R-invariant. Av-
eraging over configurations therefore gives O(4)-invariant Schwinger functions. This proves
the Euclidean assertion without using reflection positivity. If the Osterwalder–Schrader hy-
potheses also hold, analytic continuation of the O(4) action gives the Lorentz action on
the reconstructed Wightman functions. Without that positivity and regularity input, O(4)
invariance alone does not define a Lorentzian theory.

Remark 4.12 (Why the physical theory is the averaged one). A fixed Poisson sample Π
is almost surely not invariant under any reflection, so the measure for fixed Π does not
satisfy the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms and does not define a Lorentzian theory. Only
 21
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 4.11 (Euclidean invariance of the Poisson average): TorusModel.
poisson_lorentz; see Appendix F.




                                              – 33 –
the measure averaged over configurations (annealed) can be reconstructed. By Axiom I
node data are integration variables of the source, so the annealed measure is indeed the
model’s definition. Two consequences follow.
• The averaged Euclidean correlations are exactly O(4) invariant. If Hypothesis 4 and the
  remaining reconstruction hypotheses hold, the reconstructed theory is Poincaré invariant
  and momentum is conserved. The estimate m2 /Λ2 ∼ 10−36 then bounds rotationally
  invariant higher-derivative mass-shell corrections; it is not a substitute for reflection
  positivity. The “swerves” problem of causal-set propagation [16] concerns a fixed sample
  and does not arise in the averaged correlations.

• The D4 crystal itself has a quartic Lorentz violation η ≈ 3–32 (in units (MP /Λ)2 ),
  which exceeds the bound from ultra-high-energy cosmic rays by 106 –107 . The crystal is
  therefore only a lattice model, and its quartic dispersion is not a prediction.

5     Gravity

Gravity is the first consequence of the spacetime level. It uses only the source (count =
volume) and the four-dimensionality of spacetime, not the gauge group.

5.1    Relational diffeomorphism invariance
Let g be an orbifold Riemannian metric on the position-data space M , or equivalently an
invariant metric on its marked cover. The relational construction with metric g is defined
as follows. Nodes form a Poisson process Π of intensity λ dvolg . Couplings between nodes
depend only on their geodesic distance dg (for example a Rips complex at a fixed radius, or
a kernel J(dg )). Fields live on the nodes. Let ZΠ,g be the partition function of the fields.
The induced action is Γ[g] = EΠ [− log ZΠ,g ] (quenched average) or Γ[g] = − log EΠ [ZΠ,g ]
(annealed average).




                                                    – 34 –
Theorem 5.1 (Relational diffeomorphism invariance). 22 For every orbifold diffeomorphism
ψ of M (equivalently, every compatible equivariant diffeomorphism of the marked cover),
Γ[ψ ∗ g] = Γ[g] for both the quenched and the annealed induced action.

Proof. Lift ψ to a compatible equivariant map of the marked cover.                    The map
ψ : (M, ψ ∗ g) → (M, g) is an orbifold isometry, so dψ∗ g (x, y) = dg (ψx, ψy) in every orbifold
chart. By Lemma 3.3, if Π has intensity λ dvolψ∗ g , then ψ(Π) has intensity λ dvolg . The
graph, the couplings and the action are defined only through distances and counts. Hence
the system built from (Π, ψ ∗ g) is carried by ψ onto the system built from (ψΠ, g), and
the two random partition functions have the same law. Both averages of − log Z and of Z
therefore agree. The argument is local on the cover and descends because every object is
equivariant.

Corollary 5.2 (No graviton mass). 23 Expand g = g0 + h around a flat background. The
induced action has no term of order p0 in the transverse-traceless part of h, to all orders
in h.

Proof. By Theorem 5.1, the expansion of Γ obeys the diffeomorphism Ward identity gen-
erated by δhµν = ∂µ ξν + ∂ν ξµ + O(hξ) for every compactly supported ξ. A derivative-free
quadratic tensor on the traceless sector would give a nonzero variation proportional to
hµν ∂µ ξν and cannot satisfy that identity. More generally, a local scalar made from the
metric without derivatives is only a cosmological-volume term; the fixed volume element
removes its traceless variation. Hence no transverse-traceless p0 mass vertex is allowed at
any order.

Corollary 5.3 (Local quadratic form of the Poisson ensemble). Assume that the quadratic
induced action about a flat background has a local expansion whose leading nonzero term
has two derivatives. Then that term is proportional to the Fierz–Pauli (linearised Einstein–
Hilbert) action.

Proof. Corollary 5.2 removes a derivative-free mass term. At order p2 , the unique local
quadratic form invariant under the linearised diffeomorphism Ward identity, up to nor-
malisation and boundary terms, is the Fierz–Pauli form [17].

Numerical result 5.4 (Fixed-crystal comparison). On a fixed D4 or H4 crystal the
transverse-traceless response has a fitted p0 term, ΠT T (0) ≈ 0.135, and the tested trans-
verse and full Ward ratios fail. This comparison diagnoses the symmetry breaking of the
fixed regulator; it is numerical evidence, not part of Corollary 5.3.
 22
     Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 5.1 (Relational diffeomorphism invariance): TorusModel.relational_
diff_actions; see Appendix F.
  23
     Lean 4 counterpart of Corollary 5.2 (No graviton mass): TorusModel.no_mass_from_shift_
invariance_core; see Appendix F.




                                               – 35 –
5.2   Low-energy gravity
Theorem 5.5 (Unimodular Einstein gravity at low energy). Assume that, at wavelengths
long compared with the node spacing ε, the induced action defines on a smooth four-
dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold a local diffeomorphism-covariant metric func-
tional. Assume also that its leading Euler–Lagrange tensor is a natural symmetric covari-
antly divergence-free concomitant of g, ∂g and ∂ 2 g, linear in the second derivatives, and
that nonlocal terms, torsion, extra light gravitational fields and terms with more than two
metric derivatives are absent or suppressed by powers of ε2 R. Then its leading part is the
unimodular Einstein–Hilbert action, with field equation

                                   Gµν + Λgµν = 8πG Tµν ,                               (5.1)

in which Λ is an integration constant. No graviton mass term appears, and the light-bending
factor of the linear response is 2. The sign of G is the sign of the coeﬀicient c2 of the p2
term; the theorem itself does not determine that sign.

Proof. The proof has four steps.

1. Locality. Matter propagators on the graph decay exponentially in the graph distance
   (Lemma 6.11, decay length ≈ 0.34ε independent of the volume). The induced action is
   therefore a local functional of g at scales  ε.

2. Invariance and unimodularity. By Theorem 5.1, Γ is diffeomorphism invariant. Count
                                       √
   = volume fixes the volume element g by the node density, so the variations of g are
   traceless and the theory is unimodular.

3. Derivative expansion. By the hypothesis of the theorem, the continuum tensor is local
   and natural, contains no unsuppressed nonlocal form factor or extra field, and terms with
   more than two derivatives are suppressed by powers of ε2 R for wavelengths  ε. Step 1
   makes locality plausible but proves none of these continuum regularity statements.

4. Lovelock. The hypotheses now match the classification: the manifold is smooth and
   four-dimensional; the tensor is local, diffeomorphism covariant, symmetric, divergence
   free, depends on the metric through at most its first two derivatives and is linear in the
   second derivatives. Lovelock’s theorem therefore gives aGµν +bgµν [18] (§D.1.5). A four-
   dimensional topological density does not change this local field equation. For traceless
   variations this gives the traceless Einstein equation. The Bianchi identity ∇µ Gµν = 0
   and matter conservation ∇µ Tµν = 0 then give the stated equation, with Λ an integration
   constant [19].

At linear order the exchange amplitude between conserved sources has the general-relativity
tensor structure, so the light-bending factor is 2. Masslessness is Corollary 5.2.

Numerical result 5.6 (Sign and tensor check in the D4 regulator). Two independent
evaluations give c2 ≈ 1.0 × 10−3 > 0 in node units, corresponding to the attractive sign,
and the regulated exchange amplitudes agree with the general-relativity tensor ratios to




                                           – 36 –
5×10−15 . These computations check the chosen regulator; they are not part of the analytic
Lovelock implication.

    In the minimal model, g is not an integration variable. There, Theorem 5.5 is a
statement about the form of the induced action and about the linear response to external
perturbations (light bending). It is not an equation of motion. It becomes one in the
canonical finite model (Theorem 5.13).

Proposition 5.7 (No massless composite spin-2 in the minimal model). Assume that the
annealed Poisson ensemble on the fixed background g0 satisfies the Osterwalder–Schrader
hypotheses and reconstructs a Poincaré-invariant theory with a Lorentz-covariant conserved
stress tensor. Then the minimal model has no massless composite spin-2 particle coupled
to that tensor. In particular, no such particle can supply a transverse-traceless p−2 pole.

Proof. Geometric side. By Lemma 3.3, ψ(Π) ∼ Poisson(ψ ∗ g0 ), so the metric fluctuations
produced by the ensemble are diffeomorphic to g0 (pure gauge) or change only the volume
element (a scalar). A curved metric g never enters the sum.
     Field side. Theorem 4.11 supplies Euclidean O(4) invariance. The additional
reconstruction assumptions in the proposition supply Poincaré invariance and a
Lorentz-covariant conserved energy–momentum tensor. The Weinberg–Witten theorem
[20] (§D.1.6) then excludes a massless spin-2 particle carrying that tensor. Such a particle
would contribute a transverse-traceless p−2 pole, so that pole is absent. The argument
does not classify every regular or logarithmic term in the two-point function, and it makes
no claim when the reconstruction assumptions fail.

     So a dynamical metric is the one input that the minimal model does not produce. The
recovery of dynamical general relativity, background independence, black-hole information
and the resolution of singularities all rest on this single input. We tried to obtain a
dynamical metric without a new axiom, by a fully relational reinterpretation. That attempt
fails at the Weinberg–Witten barrier (Appendix C).

through the volumes of wrapped cycles. With the lattice estimates Ig = 1.6475 × 10−7 and
IA = 1.5614 × 10−4 , and with the field content Nb = 5, nf = 4, the best-matched catalogue
row gives R/ε = 11.2 and a cutoff 1/ε = 6.77 × 1017 GeV, i.e. ε = 18.0 `P . These decimals
are results of the declared Brillouin-zone quadrature/Monte-Carlo evaluation and catalogue
scan, not exact constants.

Remark 5.9. The cutoff is obtained with MP as input. The ratio (MP /Λ)2 ≈ 322 is
therefore not an independent test.

is about 81 nats per horizon cell of area ε2 . Counting node positions accounts for 1–2% of
it; the rest is the entanglement of about 106 internal torus modes per node.
Proof. The imported induced-gravity identity states that, when the same spectral cutoff is
used for the one-loop Einstein coeﬀicient and the entanglement divergence, Sent = A/(4G).
Divide a horizon of area A into A/ε2 cutoff cells. The entropy per cell is

     The direct lattice check diagonalises the scalar covariance on the two sides of a pla-

anisotropy; it is not an independent proof of that external identity.

Numerical result 5.11 (Entropy check in the regularised model). For the minimal D4
