1.05 ± 0.08; the spread reflects lattice-direction anisotropy of the induced G.

Remark 5.12 (What gravity tests). The chain is “torus → (four dimensions, signature (1, 3),
unimodular, W (F4 ) isotropy) → Einstein”. Lovelock uniqueness needs four dimensions, and
the torus supplies them. The last arrow, however, is a universal theorem. The form of the
field equations alone therefore does not distinguish this model from other models with four
dimensions.

5.3   The canonical finite model
Theorem 5.13 (Dynamical unimodular gravity). Assume Axiom M and the local
derivative-expansion hypothesis of Theorem 5.5. Then the metric is an integration variable
with weight e−Γ[g] , and the two-derivative part of Γ is the unimodular Einstein–Hilbert
action. Its homogeneous sector obeys the unimodular Friedmann equations. Linear
transverse-traceless perturbations obey the massless wave equation; when the common-cone
hypothesis of Proposition 4.9 holds, their cone is the light cone of the other fields. Fixing
the volume element removes the conformal direction of the Euclidean metric integral and
hence the usual conformal-factor instability.
    The theory is unitary if Hypothesis 4 holds.

Proof. Theorem 5.5 identifies the two-derivative action; Axiom M now integrates its metric
                                                                            √
argument rather than treating it as an external source. Varying with δ g = 0 gives the
traceless Einstein equation. Taking its divergence and using the Bianchi identity restores
the trace as an integration constant Λ. For a spatially homogeneous and isotropic metric,
the 00 and spatial components are therefore the usual Friedmann and acceleration equations
with Λ an integration constant, which are the unimodular Friedmann equations.
     Around a flat solution, impose transverse-traceless gauge. The quadratic Einstein–
                                  R             µν
Hilbert action is proportional to hTT                                                   TT
                                      µν (−□)hTT , so its Euler–Lagrange equation is □hµν =
0. Proposition 4.9 identifies this characteristic cone with the matter cone under its stated
symmetry hypothesis. The Weinberg–Witten obstruction of Proposition 5.7 does not apply
because diffeomorphisms are now a gauge symmetry and there is no gauge-invariant local
                                                    √
gravitational Tµν . Finally, unimodularity fixes g; a conformal variation δgµν = 2σgµν
         √
changes g and is therefore not an integration direction. This removes the negative con-
formal direction, but does not assert that every possible non-conformal Euclidean saddle
is stable. Unitarity still requires reflection positivity of the averaged theory, namely Hy-
pothesis 4.

What is established for the dynamical metric. Theorem 5.13 is conditional on
Axiom M and on the relational induced-action construction. The source analysis does
not derive the microscopic source map G[X] or the absolute normalisation of the finite
rotor/block completion; positivity and local-frame invariance are finite conditional re-
sults, not a completed construction from the microscopic source. The wave–coframe and




                                           – 39 –
temporal–coframe compatibility checks are EFT/finite consistency certificates only, while
the continuum Ward and source-selection conditions remain open.

Proposition 5.14 (Reflection positivity of the relational gravitational measure). 24 In the
relational formulation the metric is given by edge lengths. The length c of an edge that
crosses the reflection plane is a reflection-invariant variable. For local edge–triangle actions
e−λ(c)(s−t) , the crossing kernel
           2


                                      Z
                                                              ⊤
                                K = P (dc) vc vc⊤ ◦ e2λ(c) ss                              (5.7)

is positive definite. Here ◦ is the entrywise product, and s, t are the lengths on the two
sides.
                                                                             ⊤
Proof. vc vc⊤ is positive semidefinite of rank one. The matrix e2λss is the entrywise expo-
nential of the positive-semidefinite kernel 2λst; its Taylor series has positive-semidefinite
terms, so it is positive definite. Entrywise products of positive-semidefinite matrices are
positive semidefinite (Schur), and integration against the positive measure P (dc) preserves
this.

     Two other formulations fail. In the position-based formulation, where a crossing length
is the sum of two half-lengths, the crossing kernel is not positive definite for any coupling
shape except a constant one; the exact test constructs an explicit negative direction. In the
relational formulation, the embeddability (Cayley–Menger) constraint on simplices strad-
dling the plane has a crossing kernel that is not positive definite; the corresponding exact
kernel test gives the same obstruction. Measure-level reflection positivity therefore fails in
both of these alternative formulations, and the relational edge–triangle form of Proposi-
tion 5.14 is the one used here. For the unrestricted Poisson source, unitarity reduces to
the averaged statement, Hypothesis 4.

Singularity. In unimodular gravity the unimodular time T is conjugate to Λ. The
constraint Λ = 3H 2 − 8πGρ then becomes a Schrödinger equation i∂T Ψ = HT Ψ with
HT = απv2 +V (v), where v is the volume. For a scalar field V = −g/v 2 ; for dust V = −β/v,
which describes the interior of Oppenheimer–Snyder collapse.

Proposition 5.15 (Integer volume removes the singularity ambiguity). In the minisuper-
space model the unimodular volume variable is identified with the node number, v = N ∈
{1, 2, 3, . . . }; this is a modelling choice motivated by count = volume, which holds in mean.
Then πv2 acts as the second difference operator (∆Ψ)(N ) = 2Ψ(N ) − Ψ(N + 1) − Ψ(N − 1)
(with Ψ(0) = 0). Then HT is a bounded self-adjoint operator on `2 (N). Its evolution is
unitary, and no self-adjoint extension has to be chosen.

Proof. k∆k ≤ 4, and |V (N )| ≤ max(g, β) for N ≥ 1. A bounded symmetric operator is
self-adjoint, and e−iT HT is unitary. In the continuum the operator −α∂v2 − g/v 2 on (0, ∞)
  24
   Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 5.14 (Reflection positivity of the relational gravitational measure):
TorusModel.reflection_gram_kernel_posSemidef; see Appendix F.




                                                 – 40 –
is in the limit-circle case at v = 0 for every g > 0, so it has a one-parameter family of
self-adjoint extensions, i.e. a boundary condition at the singularity must be chosen. The
integer lattice removes this choice.

Numerical result 5.16 (Bounce and recombination). For couplings 0.5–50, hN i(T )
reaches a minimum and grows again (a bounce). The norm is conserved to 4 × 10−15 ,
and the density h1/N 2 i stays bounded independently of the lattice size. Anisotropic
collapse adds terms of the same form, and the locality of the approach to a singularity
(Belinsky–Khalatnikov–Lifshitz) justifies the minisuperspace treatment near the singu-
larity. Recombination after the bounce was tested with gradient couplings γ(Nj − Nk )2
between patches: exact computations for two, three and four patches including inter-
patch entanglement, and a 32-patch mean field. All patches bounce, inhomogeneity
stays bounded, norm errors are below 10−12 , and the entanglement saturates in the
unimodular-bounce and multi-patch recombination tests.

Black-hole information. Take as inputs unitarity, a finite horizon dimension (S =
A/4G, Proposition 5.10), and node links across the horizon. A 12-qubit model then gives a
radiation entropy that rises to the Page time and returns to zero (a Page curve), both for
Haar and for local scrambling. With evaporation rate dM /dt ∝ −1/M 2 , the Page time is
tPage = (1 − 2−3/2 ) tev ≈ 0.646 tev . The node channel carries about 81 nats per horizon cell
per refresh time ε. This exceeds the required entropy flux ∼ TH3 by the factor 81(Λ/TH )3 
1, so the release rate is set by Hawking emission; this conclusion is supported by the explicit
Page-curve simulation. The semiclassical emission law and the Page-entropy comparison
are external inputs [24, 25]. The finite-qubit calculation illustrates these assumptions; it is
not a derivation of unitary evaporation for the unrestricted torus source.

Scale normalisation and simultaneous matching. The induced Einstein–Hilbert
tensor form does not independently determine the absolute coeﬀicients of the gravity–gauge
                                                                   Q
bridge. With q = ΛR, the assignments K = κA q 3 , MP2 = κg Λ2 q 6 i χi and M∗ = 1/R im-
pose an additional scale equation. Section 13.7 tests this equation after fixing the model’s
field content: it is satisfied by a subset of the gauge-matched points in one matching class
and thereby selects that class among the tested representatives. This is a conditional se-
lection result within the observationally constrained specialisation, not a derivation of the
bridge coeﬀicients from the microscopic source; moreover, a joint fit that also imposes the
common-radius area relation has not been carried out.

5.4   Sector Lagrangian and derivation
Final sector action. At two-derivative order the gravitational sector used below is the
unimodular Einstein action
                                   Z     √                        
                     (2)                    −g           √
                                     4
                   Sgrav [g, λU ] = d x          R + λU ( −g − ω0 ) .             (5.8)
                                         16πGeff
                                                                            √
Here ω0 is the fixed volume density. Variation with respect to λU imposes −g = ω0 ;
the trace-free metric equation and the Bianchi identity then introduce the cosmological




                                            – 41 –
constant as an integration constant. Curvature-squared and higher-derivative terms are
                                                                     (2)
suppressed terms of the effective expansion and are not included in Sgrav .

Derivation chain. Axiom M makes the metric dynamical. The relational construction
gives the diffeomorphism-equivariant induced action (Theorem 5.1); the assumed local
derivative expansion removes a graviton mass and selects its two-derivative part. Four-
dimensional Lovelock classification and the unimodular variation then give the action above
and the field equation of Theorem 5.5. Linearisation in transverse-traceless gauge gives the
massless Fierz–Pauli kinetic operator.

Newton’s law. For slowly moving masses in a weak field the action reduces to Newton’s
law. Diffeomorphism invariance of the induced matter action (Theorem 5.1) gives ∇µ T µν =
0, so a pointlike test mass follows a geodesic of g. Write gµν = ηµν + hµν and take a static,
non-relativistic source, for which T00 = ρ is the only non-negligible component. To first
order in h the field equation of Theorem 5.5 gives h00 = −2Φ with

                                      ∇2 Φ = 4πGeff ρ,                                  (5.9)

where the cosmological constant has been neglected, as it may be on laboratory and solar-
system scales. For slow motion the geodesic equation reduces to ẍ = −∇Φ. A point mass
M gives Φ = −Geff M /r, and two masses therefore attract with

                                            Geff mM
                                      F =           .                                 (5.10)
                                               r2
This is Newton’s law, with Newton’s constant Geff [26]. The inverse-square form follows
from the two-derivative action alone; the suppressed curvature-squared terms contribute
corrections that are negligible at distances large compared with the cutoff length. The
value of Geff is not derived; it is fixed by matching to the measured Newton constant.

External results. The final classification step uses Lovelock’s theorem, the contracted
Bianchi identity and the standard unimodular variational argument. A Lorentzian Hilbert-
space interpretation additionally uses Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and therefore
requires the reflection- positivity assumptions stated in Chapter 11.

Origin and determination of the coeﬀicients. The Einstein tensor form is derived
only under Axiom M and the locality and derivative-expansion hypotheses. Neither Geff
nor the absolute normalisation of the source-to-metric map is fixed by Axioms I and D.
Λ is an integration constant. The observationally restricted matching calculation tests a
proposed relation for Geff ; it is not a microscopic derivation of that coeﬀicient.

Open remainder. The unrestricted torus source has not been shown to generate one
common metric-and-matter measure with nonlinear gravitational Ward identities, reflec-
tion positivity and a continuum limit. The singularity and black-hole calculations in this
chapter are finite or semiclassical tests conditional on the displayed gravitational action,
not additional derivations of it.

6        Gauge structure and matter

6.1        The algebra of spatial-move sequences
A spatial-move sequence is an ordered list of spatial moves. By Proposition 3.9(a), each
spatial move acts on the four cusps of a node as a transposition, so a sequence acts as an
element of S4 . The corresponding sequence space is the group algebra C[S4 ], with basis
{|σi : σ ∈ S4 }. Left multiplication Lπ |σi = |πσi appends a move; right multiplication
Rπ |σi = |σπ −1 i relabels the cusps.
     This finite-commutant construction is compared with, but is not identified with, the
finite noncommutative-geometric particle constructions of Connes–Lott [5] or the spectral-
action framework of Chamseddine–Connes [6]. Those works motivate the use of finite
algebras and commutants; the representation space, the S4 multiplicities, the heavy U (1)′
factor and the matter bimodule below are model-specific calculations.

Definition 6.1 (Source move operator). 25 The one-step source operator on spatial-move
sequences is
                             1 X           1     X
                        P =         L sr =               Lτ ,                     (6.1)
                             12            6
                                            r∈A3              τ transposition

where the second form uses that each transposition is the image of exactly two roots
(Proposition 3.9). The lazy version is Bρ = (1 − ρ)I + ρP , 0 < ρ < 1. Coarse-graining is
iteration of source moves: the sequence distribution evolves under the semigroup generated
by P .

Proof. (a) Left and right multiplications commute: Lπ Rh |σi = |πσh−1 i = Rh Lπ |σi.

     (b) τ τ is the class sum of the transpositions, a central element of C[S4 ]. By Schur’s

values at a transposition are 1, −1, 0, 1, −1. Dividing by 6 gives the stated scalars.

isotypic blocks.

Remark 6.3 (Right-equivariance is forced). Proposition 6.2(a) shows that right S4 -
equivariance follows from generating the sequence dynamics by left multiplications, as the
source move operator does; it is not imposed as a separate condition. The finite retention
rules that are not right-equivariant fail: an adjacent-position block rule does not commute
with the right action, the full append-plus-adjoint rule has only a scalar joint commutant,
and the Hermitian prefix completion is not invariant under the adjoint. These obstructions
were established by exact finite calculations.

6.2   The gauge group
Theorem 6.4 (Gauge group). The local transformations of the spatial-move sequence space
                                                                       L
that commute with the full right regular action R(S4 ) form the algebra λ Mdim λ (C). They
also commute with the central move operator P . Its unitary group is

                           U (1) × U (1)′ × U (2) × U (3) × U (3)′ .                     (6.2)

The factor U (3)′ couples to no matter, so its link variables are Haar distributed and it
decouples at the cutoff scale. The remaining gauge group is

                             G = U (3) × U (2) × U (1) × U (1)′ .                        (6.3)

Proof. The coarse-grained dynamics is generated by left multiplications and commutes with
the right regular action (Proposition 6.2). The transformations that preserve this structure
are those commuting with all Rh , i.e. the commutant of the right regular representation.
This commutant is the left group algebra. By Wedderburn’s theorem it is isomorphic to
L
   λ Mdim λ (C). The dimensions are 1, 1, 2, 3, 3, with 1 + 1 + 4 + 9 + 9 = 24 = |S4 |. The
unitary group of a matrix block Md (C) is U (d).
     A block carries a dynamical gauge field only if charged matter couples to it. Its kinetic
term is induced by integrating out that matter (Theorem 6.10). Charged matter lives on
wrapped cycles, one stack per block (§6.5). The vacua have stacks for the blocks 3, 2, 1, 1′




                                            – 44 –
but none for the twisted-standard block 3′ . The induced kinetic term of U (3)′ therefore
vanishes. A gauge field with zero kinetic term has Haar-distributed links, i.e. infinite
coupling, and it carries no propagating degrees of freedom at any scale below the cutoff.
The absence of a stack for the block 3′ is part of the vacuum data (Construction 6.12).

     The notation distinguishes three levels that are otherwise easy to confuse. The full
commutant has five unitary factors, but the twisted-standard U (3)′ has no charged matter
and is removed as a nondynamical Haar factor. At the cutoff the matter-coupled group
is therefore U (3) × U (2) × U (1) × U (1)′ . The non-abelian factors contain the physical
colour and weak subgroups. The four central U (1) directions, one in each factor, combine
into the hypercharge Y = Qa /6 − Qc /2 + Qd /2 and three orthogonal combinations; the
Green–Schwarz mass matrix makes these three combinations massive (Proposition 6.16).
After these heavy directions are integrated out, the surviving low-energy gauge group is
SU (3)c × SU (2)L × U (1)Y , with the central U (1) directions of U (3) and U (2) entering the
hypercharge combination; it is this low-energy group, not a claim that the cutoff group was
already special-unitary, that is written in the conclusion.
Remark 6.5 (Which representation carries which force). The colour group U (3) comes
from the standard representation of S4 , which is the permutation representation of the
four cusps minus the trivial one. The weak group U (2) comes from the two-dimensional
representation, which factors through S4 → S3 and is trivial on the Klein four-group. The
two U (1) factors come from the trivial and sign representations.
    The cusp permutation module and the history module must not be confused. The four-
cusp space is 1 ⊕ 3, with each irreducible occurring once. Its commutant is C ⊕ C, whose
unitary group is U (1) × U (1), also after restriction to A4 . The larger group U (1) × U (3)
preserves the two summands but does not commute with the group action on the triplet.
The gauge group above is instead the commutant of the right regular representation on
the ordered history space C[S4 ]; its five blocks have dimensions 1, 1, 2, 3, 3. The level-three
Hecke action supplies an S4 permutation monodromy, while the D4 source and the regular
history algebra supply the colour and weak blocks. No commutant calculation is transferred
from one representation space to the other.
    Figure 13 separates representation dimension from multiplicity in these two spaces.
The representation-theoretic input is Schur’s lemma and the regular-module decomposi-
tion [4]. Commuting with P alone would be a weaker condition: since its five eigenvalues
are distinct and its eigenspaces have dimensions 1, 1, 4, 9, 9, its commutant is M1 ⊕ M1 ⊕
M4 ⊕ M9 ⊕ M9 . Thus the five gauge blocks above use preservation of the full right action,
not merely the spectrum of P .

  28

                                                             – 46 –
Proposition 6.8 (Contraction of the finite spatial-move renormalisation; computer-as-
sisted). 29 For the finite regularised spatial-move-sequence map used here, exact integer
Sturm sequences verify contraction to the right-equivariant fixed point on the tested param-
eter domain. This is a contraction result for that finite map; it is not yet a theorem about
the physical blocking map generated by the microscopic source for every λ > 0.
Derivation: Appendix D.2.1.

Proof. Let B(u) be the finite blocking matrix implemented in the certificate and let E be
the projection onto the right-equivariant fixed subspace. Right equivariance gives BE =
EB = E, so with K = B−E one has B n −E = K n . Decomposing the regular representation
into its five Wedderburn blocks reduces the spectrum of K to five rational characteristic
polynomials. The certificate clears denominators, applies the Cayley transform z = (1 +
s)/(1 − s), and uses signed Sturm remainders to count zeros in <s < 0. The count equals
the degree in every nontrivial block throughout the declared rational parameter intervals.
Hence every nontrivial eigenvalue obeys |z| < 1. Finite-dimensional Jordan normal form
then gives kK n k ≤ Cq n for some q < 1, so B n → E.
     This proves contraction for the specified finite matrix and parameter box. The iden-
tification of that matrix with the exact push-forward blocking operator of the microscopic
source is a separate open premise.

     In the declared finite source of the canonical finite model the blocking family Mt =
exp[t(P − I)] is part of the source data. It is an exact Markov semigroup on the 24-state
cumulative history fibre, commutes with the right S4 action and has vanishing leakage
BQP = 0 from the protected history block (Appendix E, §E.2). This is a same-space push-
forward, not invariance of the seven-mark one-step law µH . For the unrestricted source
the identification of the blocking operator, or a contracting Feshbach bound for it, remains
open.

     The induced measure is a positive combination of Wilson loops with an explicit large-
field bound. This is what makes possible the comparison arguments for the photon (§8)
and the large-field bounds of the weak and strong sectors. Decimation, i.e. integrating out
a set of sites, produces an operator of the same form, with walk weights given by sums
over walks through the eliminated sites; this follows by expanding the resolvent into closed
walks.

Scope of the microscopic-source derivation. Theorem 6.10 is an exact conditional
statement for the explicit operator M [U ] = A − KU . It does not derive the link variables,
plaquette action, or continuum Yang–Mills dynamics from the torus and spatial-move
source. Those transport and plaquette steps generated by the microscopic source remain
open in the dependency analysis of Section 12.1.

6.5   Matter and vacua
Wrapped-stack matter and Green–Schwarz/Stückelberg couplings use the intersecting-
brane framework reviewed in [27]. The global SU (2) doublet-parity obstruction is Witten’s
anomaly [28]; the flux index uses [29] (§D.1.6). These references specify the mechanisms
and tests, not the source’s particular bimodule or its finite vacuum enumeration. Charged
matter is the fermion ψ of Axiom D restricted to wrapped cycles. A gauge block of Theo-
rem 6.4 is realised as a stack x ∈ {a, b, c, d} (for U (3), U (2), U (1), U (1)′ ) of cycles wrapped
on internal two-tori. Bulk ψ carries no spatial-move-sequence index, because by Axiom I
fields are functions of node data and the sequence is relational. The block index is therefore
carried by the cycle. The vacuum data below contain no stack for the block 3′ , which is
why that factor decouples in Theorem 6.4.
Construction 6.12 (Vacuum data). A vacuum consists of winding numbers (nix , mix ) of
each stack on three internal two-tori Ti2 with shapes χi ; one of them is the node torus and
two are relation tori of the D4 structure. The data must satisfy the integrality condition of
tilted tori (mix − nix /2 ∈ Z when Ti2 is tilted) and the consistency conditions: cancellation
of all gauge and mixed anomalies, with abelian anomalies cancelled by Green–Schwarz
couplings. The chiral multiplicity of the bifundamental (x, ȳ) is the intersection number

Theorem 6.13 (Chirality, charges and anomalies). 32
 31
     Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma 6.11 (Exponential decay of matter propagators): TorusModel.geometric_
walk_tail; see Appendix F.
  32
     Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 6.13 (Chirality, charges and anomalies): TorusModel.three_

                                                  – 49 –
 (a) Chirality. In every vacuum, the number of chiral zero modes of ψ at the intersection
     of stacks x and y is |Ixy |, and their chirality is the sign of Ixy .

 (b) Charge quantisation. The hypercharge is a fixed combination of the stack U (1)
     charges, Y = Qa /6 − Qc /2 + Qd /2. Since matter is bifundamental (charges ±1
     under two stack U (1)’s), all hypercharges are integer multiples of 1/6.

 (c) Anomalies. For a vacuum with the chiral content of three Standard-Model gen-
     erations, all cubic gauge anomalies, the mixed gauge–gravitational anomaly and the
     SU (2) global anomaly vanish, and the extra abelian anomalies are cancelled by Green–
     Schwarz couplings.

Proof. (a) On a two-torus with a U (1) connection of degree k, the Dirac index is k
(Riemann–Roch, or the Atiyah–Singer index theorem). For two cycles on a torus, the
relative degree is the intersection number nx my − mx ny . On the product of three tori
the index multiplies. The same structure is verified on random nodes with the overlap
operator; the finite overlap-index check and the exact matter-charge/anomaly calculation
are included in the reproducibility materials.
     (b) A bifundamental of U (Nx ) × U (Ny ) has charge +1 under U (1)x and −1 under
U (1)y . Inserting ±1 into Y gives values in 61 Z; the corresponding exact integer check is
listed in the formal-verification appendix.
     (c) With integer normalisation q = 1 and Higgs charge h = 3, the charges of
(Q, uc , dc , L, ec ) are (1, −4, 2, −3, 6). The traces trY 3 , trY , tr Y TSU
                                                                            2              2
                                                                               (2) , tr Y TSU (3) vanish
for one generation, hence for three. There are four SU (2) doublets per generation (three
colours of Q and one L), an even number, so the SU (2) global anomaly vanishes. The
remaining abelian traces are finite integer sums matched by the Green–Schwarz couplings.
All of these sums are checked exactly in Lean and by an independent integer calculation
of the matter-bimodule charges and anomalies.

Remark 6.14 (The number three). Theorem 6.13 explains chirality, charge quantisation
and anomaly freedom, and it makes the number of families a topological number: the
intersection number. It does not force that number to be three. The vacuum catalogue
below is defined by requiring the chiral content of three Standard-Model generations. The
number of generations is therefore vacuum data, and we do not count it as a prediction
(§16). A link between the three generations and the triality of D4 was examined and is
not established.

Proposition 6.15 (Vacuum catalogue; exhaustive computation). 33 Among the vacua with
three Standard-Model generations, no exotics, up- and down-type Yukawa couplings and
two-loop gauge-coupling matching, there are exactly 76 topologically consistent ones. They
have 16 physically distinct outputs. In 52 of them (68%) the Yukawa ranks of (u, d, e, ν) are
(2, 2, 1, 1), so the first-generation quark and electron masses and the lightest neutrino mass
vanish at leading order; the exhaustive vacuum catalogue is an exact finite enumeration.
  33
   Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 6.15 (Vacuum catalogue; exhaustive computation): TorusModel.
vacua; see Appendix F.




                                                – 50 –
Derivation: Appendix D.2.3.

Proof. Exhaustiveness is relative to the finite domain specified in Construction 6.12. The
program visits every allowed integer winding tuple and every declared tilt and orientation.
It computes all intersection numbers by the determinant formula, rejects a tuple unless the
three-generation spectrum and absence of exotics hold, and then tests the tadpoles, exact
integer anomaly sums, both Yukawa selection rules and the declared two-loop matching
window. Passing rows are quotiented by the explicit finite equivalence group; the lexi-
cographically least member of each orbit is retained. Since the loops cover closed finite
integer ranges and the predicates before matching are exact integer or rational tests, every
admissible tuple in that domain occurs and no orbit is counted twice.
     The resulting list contains 76 representatives and maps to 16 output rows. Direct
rank computation of the four Yukawa matrices gives (2, 2, 1, 1) in 52 representatives. The
separate Verification Supplement gives the bounds, canonical representatives, rejection
counts and hashes. Thus the result is an exhaustive finite catalogue; it is not a classification
of unbounded winding data.

Proposition 6.16 (No extra massless U (1); computer-assisted). 34 For all 76 vacua, the
Green–Schwarz coupling matrix has a kernel of dimension one, so only hypercharge remains
massless; B − L is massive. This follows from the exact Green–Schwarz kernel and extra-
U (1) test. Moduli are coordinates of the data space in the relational construction, not
massless scalars.
Derivation: Appendix D.2.3.

Proof. For each representative form the Green–Schwarz matrix whose columns are the
four stack U (1) generators and whose rows are the axionic couplings. Its entries are integer
products of wrapping numbers. Fraction-free Gaussian elimination gives rank three for
every one of the 76 matrices, hence a one-dimensional kernel. Exact substitution of y =
(1/6, 0, −1/2, 1/2) gives M y = 0, so the kernel is precisely Qy and the surviving generator
is hypercharge. The B − L vector is not proportional to y, so it is outside the kernel
and becomes massive. The finite scan behind this elimination is distributed with the
reproducibility package (Table G, row “No extra massless U (1)”).

Theorem 6.17 (Coupling values are not fixed). 35 The source does not fix the numerical
values of the gauge couplings: there are distinct vacua, and continuous moduli within a
vacuum, that satisfy every condition of the source and give different values.
Derivation: Appendix D.2.4.

Proof. At the cutoff, αx−1 = κA (ΛR)3 Vx (χ), where Vx is the wrapped volume of stack x
and χ = (χ1 , χ2 , χ3 ) are the shapes of the internal tori. None of the conditions of the

source involves χ or ΛR, and Vx depends continuously and non-trivially on χ. Hence the
couplings vary continuously over the set of admissible data.

Proposition 6.18 (Failure of the reverse derivation in the declared catalogue). In a fixed
vacuum there are four unknowns (χ1 , χ2 , χ3 and ΛR) and four data (α1 , α2 , α3 , MP ), so
gauge couplings yield no predictions unless the source fixes the shapes. The only candidate
shapes fixed without new assumptions are the modular self-dual points (τ = i and τ =
eiπ/3 ), which are extrema of every SL2 (Z)-invariant (modular-invariant) potential. At
those points, every accepted row in the declared finite scans has an extra massless U (1)
(kernel dimension two), with hypercharge coupling errors of 30.5–37.4%. Extending the
winding range from |n| ≤ 3 to 5 and 6 does not change this. The scans therefore exclude
accepted self-dual vacua only within those finite domains; they do not classify unbounded
winding data.
Derivation: Appendix D.2.4.

Proof. The matching equations have unknowns (χ1 , χ2 , χ3 , L), L = ΛR, because αx−1 =
κA L3 Vx (χ) and the Planck equation supplies the fourth relation. The source axioms im-
pose no equation on these variables, so solving them from four measured quantities is a
parameter-conditioned fit. At a modular self-dual point the shapes are fixed and the pro-
posal becomes a finite test. The scans enumerate every winding tuple in the stated boxes,
apply the same topological and matching predicates as Proposition 6.15, and row-reduce
the Green–Schwarz matrix. Every matching survivor has nullity two and hypercharge error
between 30.5% and 37.4%. Hence no row in those finite domains satisfies both require-
ments. This does not exclude all unbounded winding data.

    Within the declared catalogue the extra U (1) is a topological kernel and cannot be
removed by threshold corrections. A catalogue vacuum that reproduces the observed cou-
plings must therefore have its moduli stabilised away from the self-dual points.

6.6   Dependence on the Yukawa selection rule
A finite intersection catalogue fixes charges and multiplicities but does not by itself specify
every Yukawa amplitude. The quoted rank counts use the catalogue’s overlap rule. In
a separate, stricter combinatorial test, a triple is retained only when its three oriented
intersection numbers close on every torus, allowing the stated brane-orientation reversals.
The stored test removes charged-lepton and neutrino couplings in every tested class. A
zero Yukawa matrix has rank zero and gives no leading-order lepton masses, so these two
rules cannot be interchanged while preserving the mass conclusions. This is a result of the
specified finite test, not a general theorem that all geometric disk amplitudes must obey
that numerical closure criterion.
     The alternative rule assigns periodised Gaussian overlaps to the localised wavefunc-
tions, including the parallel-brane Higgs sector. The observationally constrained specialisa-
tion in §13.7 explicitly adopts this rule and states its inputs. Its nonzero masses, full-rank
selection and neutrino numbers are conditional on that additional amplitude prescription.




                                            – 52 –
Anomaly cancellation and the Green–Schwarz kernel calculation do not resolve this ampli-
tude choice. The rank statement in Proposition 6.15 consequently concerns the declared
catalogue rule, not every completion of the microscopic source.

7     Internal dynamics of a node: waves, rotor, resonance

This section studies the internal degrees of freedom of a node: waves on the flat torus,
oscillations of its area, the rotor phase and the windings. It also proves limitations of these
mechanisms. Except for Proposition 6.7, already stated in §6, these results are not premises
of the main argument. Several statements concern explicit constructions: declared laws
whose consequences are then proved. They are proposed dynamics, not consequences of
the axioms.
                                Breathing area mode                         Resonance and limits
            Torus waves                                Rotor and windings
                                  wave–geometry                              exact/asymptotic
      helical plane-wave pair                           phase transport
                                    Hamiltonian                                  retention

Figure 14: The node Hamiltonian contains torus waves, the breathing mode, rotor phase and
winding. Their resonances explain several collective effects, but the exact statements must be
separated from perturbative and asymptotic ones.


7.1    Rotating plane waves
On the flat torus Tτ2 the plane waves e2πi(rξ1 +sξ2 ) have the frequencies of Definition 6.6.
A rotating (helical) plane wave is a superposition of the modes (1, 1) and (1, −1) whose
phases advance in time. Its pattern then rotates around the torus.

Proposition 7.1 (Helical pair). The modes (1, 1) and (1, −1) have equal frequency if and
only if |τ − 1| = |τ + 1|, i.e. Re τ = 0. On a rectangular torus τ = iy their superposition
is a rigid rotation for every y. At fixed wave action, the force that the helical pair exerts
on the shape vanishes if and only if y = 1: the square torus is the shape equilibrium of a
helical pair.
                           √
Proof. ω(1,±1) ∝ |τ ∓ 1|/ y, and |τ − 1| = |τ + 1| iff Re τ = 0. For τ = iy the two modes
are degenerate, so a superposition whose relative phase advances at the common frequency
is a rigid rotation. At fixed action J (an adiabatic invariant) the wave energy is E = J ω,
so the force on log y is −J ∂ω/∂ log y. From log ω = const + 21 log(1 + y 2 ) − 12 log y we get
                    y2
                       2 − 2 , which vanishes iff y = 1.
                           1
∂ log ω/∂ log y = 1+y

     The phase of a rotating wave advances linearly in time. It therefore serves as a clock: it
is the internal realisation of the unwound phase ϕ̃ of Axiom I. The corresponding collective
excitation is a scalar (acoustic) branch, not a spin-2 mode (No-go 7.8).

7.2    Breathing and resonance
The breathing mode is the oscillation of the torus area A. By Proposition 6.7 it couples to
every wave mode with the same strength − 12 . We call the source in resonance if the four
cusp moves of a node carry equal energy, i.e. equal bath amplitudes a1 = a2 = a3 = a4 .




                                                  – 53 –
Proposition 7.2 (Resonance makes spatial-move retention exact). If the bath is the breath-
ing mode and the four cusp amplitudes are equal, the induced generator of the spatial-move-
                                                                         P
sequence dynamics is a multiple of the class sum of transpositions, J τ Lτ . It commutes
with every right multiplication Rh , and the sequence defect vanishes exactly at every blocking
step and every value of the regularisation.
                                                                P
Proof. The induced coupling between moves i, j is Jij = 2 b gib gjb /Ωb , where gib is the
coupling of move i to the bath mode b. For the breathing bath, gib = − 12 ai (Proposi-
tion 6.7). With equal amplitudes, Jij = J for all pairs. The induced generator is then
  P                P
J i<j L(ij) = J τ Lτ , a central element. By Proposition 6.2(a) it commutes with every
Rh , so it preserves each isotypic block. A blocking step is a polynomial in the generator, so
it preserves them too, and the defect is zero. Numerically the defect is 10−16 at all tested
blocking steps.

     With unequal amplitudes a defect appears. For the amplitudes (1, 0.96, 1.07, 1.02) it is
at most 0.0095, because the product of the two remaining amplitudes in an adjacent-pair
term depends on the order. Theorem 6.9 shows that this defect contracts to zero in the
infrared. Resonance is therefore “exact if present, asymptotic otherwise”. That resonance
actually equalises the amplitudes is not derived.

Parametric (Mathieu) resonance. A breathing oscillation A(t) = A0 (1 + h cos Ωt)
modulates the mode frequencies, ωk (t)2 = ωk2 (1 − h cos Ωt + O(h2 )). Each mode then
obeys a Mathieu equation. Its instability tongues, in particular Ω ≈ 2ωk , give exponential
growth of mode amplitudes without a static tachyon. This is a candidate mechanism for
the dynamical formation of a condensate. It has not been computed: the saturation by
non-linear terms and the constraints from conserved quantities remain to be included. The
mechanism therefore remains open.

Mass of the breathing scalar. In the wave–geometry construction, the area cost and
wave zero-point energy produce the local minimum analysed below. A suﬀiciently massive
scalar has a Yukawa-suppressed force on Solar-System scales and can therefore evade a
long-range scalar constraint. Cassini measured the post-Newtonian parameter γ [30]; it
did not measure this model’s scalar mass. Compatibility requires both its mass and its
coupling to matter, and the source does not fix σ.

Windings. In the rotor term, a flat background aij = 2πhw, vij i depends only on the
voltage labels of the relation lattice. It defines an internal winding sector with w in the
dual relation lattice. The same cosine term supplies the Stückelberg masses required by the
Green–Schwarz mechanism (Proposition 6.16). Chiral matter at an intersection couples
                                                                     R
to the composite connection ϕX dϕY /2π. Its curvature integral dϕX ∧ dϕY /4π 2 is a
determinant, so by the index theorem the number of zero modes is the intersection number.

A spatial-move-sequence operator compatible with reflection positivity. The
sequence dynamics of Proposition 6.2 is generated by left multiplications. A finite two-
time construction with a Hermitian left-algebra generator passes all of the following checks:

• the ordered sequence coeﬀicients are uniform;

• the generator satisfies [H, Rh ] = 0 for all 24 h;

• the reflection Gram matrix is positive semidefinite;

• the M3 and M2 blocks appear;
                                        P
• the static bath kernel Jij (0) = 2       b gib gjb /Ωb is reproduced;

• the partition function is finite and positive.

It separates the internal sequence time from the physical time. This resolves, for this
construction, the conflict between reflection positivity (which needs a Hermitian transfer)
and exact sequence retention (which had been shown only for a non-Hermitian append
generator). It is a candidate: the source does not single it out.

Finite conditions do not select a nonlinear completion. The nonlinear-completion
and source-selection analyses show that finite symmetry, reflection factorisation and reten-
tion tests do not determine the nonlinear source. If Ix is any retained local invariant, adding
  P
λ x Ix4 with λ ≥ 0 preserves those finite properties while changing the fourth and higher
vertices. Consequently, the finite sequence, metric and positivity results do not select a
unique Einstein/Yang–Mills coupling or Ward remainder; a microscopic source-selection
rule must be supplied explicitly.

7.5   What waves and rotors cannot do
No-go result 7.7 (No chirality from rotation). Rotation of waves on a node torus cannot
produce chiral zero modes.




                                               – 57 –
Proof. The index of the Dirac operator on a closed surface depends only on the degree
(total flux) of the connection (Atiyah–Singer [29] (§D.1.6)). Rotation is a momentum or
holonomy, not a flux. The effective magnetic field ∇×v of a velocity field on the closed torus
has zero total flux by Stokes’ theorem [33] (§D.1.18), because the torus has no boundary.
For a torus embedded in R3 , the total Gauss curvature is 0 by Gauss–Bonnet [34], so the
curvature of the embedding gives no net flux either. Moreover, counter-rotating branches
are equally stable, so the sense of rotation is not selected.

No-go result 7.8 (No graviton from torus oscillations). The area and shape oscillations
of node tori do not produce a spin-2 field.

Proof. The moduli (ρ, τ ) are three real scalars per node: they describe the internal two-
dimensional metric up to diffeomorphism. A spacetime metric has ten components, of
which a massless spin-2 field needs the two transverse-traceless ones built from spacetime
indices. Oscillations of internal scalars carry no spacetime tensor index. The only exact
gauge direction among them is a local rescaling, and the collective mode is a scalar acoustic
branch.

No-go result 7.9 (No generations from hexagonal modes). The threefold degeneracy of
the lowest modes of the hexagonal torus τ = eiπ/3 does not by itself give three chiral
generations.

Proof. With no background flux, the torus Dirac operator anticommutes with chirality and
its non-zero eigenvalues occur in opposite-chirality pairs. The three lowest momenta at the
hexagonal point form one orbit of the lattice rotation group; they are three equal non-
zero Kaluza–Klein eigenvalues, not three unpaired zero modes. Their contribution to the
index is therefore zero. Moreover the stabiliser of the hexagonal lattice permutes the three
momenta, so the geometry supplies no invariant rule that would retain three chiral states
while removing their partners. A separate flux, boundary condition or chiral projection
would be additional input. The degeneracy alone is thus insuﬀicient.

No-go result 7.10 (No Higgs from breathing). The breathing scalar of a node torus
cannot serve as the electroweak Higgs.

Proof. The breathing coordinate is the variation δρ of the torus area. Gauge transforma-
tions act on the internal stack indices and leave ρ unchanged, so δρ is a gauge singlet. The
stabiliser of a non-zero singlet expectation value is therefore the entire gauge group. By
contrast, electroweak breaking to U (1)em requires a field in an SU (2) doublet with non-zero
hypercharge. Since the two fields carry inequivalent representations, no potential for δρ
alone can produce the required breaking.

No-go result 7.11 (No spinors from triality). Triality of the D4 cells does not endow a
field placed on those cells with a spinor transformation law, and the displayed cell complex
is not a single Kähler–Dirac complex.




                                           – 58 –
Proof. Triality permutes the three kinds of 16-cells, but in the present complex that per-
mutation is realised by an element of W (F4 ) ⊂ O(4). Consequently a full 2π geometric
rotation acts trivially on cell labels. A spinor instead is a representation of the double
cover Spin(4) on which the lifted 2π rotation acts as −1. Merely assigning a field to one
of the triality orbits cannot change the former action into the latter.
     A Kähler–Dirac field in four dimensions has one component for every basis differential
form, with multiplicities (1, 4, 6, 4, 1) and total dimension 24 = 16 at a site. The present
incidence complex has cell multiplicities (1, 12, 32, 24, 3), totalling 72, and its incidence
maps do not identify a 16-dimensional invariant subcomplex with those form degrees. Thus
neither triality nor the given cochain complex supplies the missing spin representation.
Axiom D remains an independent input.

No-go result 7.12 (Failure of the tested partition-function combinations). Within the
candidate family formed in this section from the theta-sector sum, Haar gauge integral,
Gaussian determinant, spatial-move-sequence path factor and breathing bath, none of the
explicitly tested combinations satisfies all six declared criteria simultaneously.

Proof. The candidates divide into two cases. If the transfer operator is made Hermitian,
the reflected kernel can be written as a Gram matrix and reflection positivity is avail-
able, but the one-sided append operation is replaced by a symmetrised move and the
ordered sequence is not retained. If the exact append generator is kept, ordered sequences
are retained, but the generator is non-Hermitian and the reflected transfer has no posi-
tive Gram factorisation. Adding the theta sum, Haar integral, Gaussian determinant or
breathing kernel multiplies these transfers by positive or central factors; it does not alter
this Hermitian/non-Hermitian alternative. Hence every tested member fails at least one
of reflection positivity and exact sequence retention, before the remaining four criteria are
imposed. The two-time construction avoids this particular conflict by assigning the two
operations to different parameters. Finally, every member of the tested family uses a fixed
graph rather than a fluctuating distance-defined graph, so Theorem 5.1 cannot supply dif-
feomorphism invariance. This is a no-go for the stated candidate family, not for all possible
partition functions.

8     Electromagnetism

The abelian factor that remains unbroken after electroweak breaking (§9) is a U (1) gauge
field on the node complex. Its weight is induced by the matter of Theorem 6.10. In this
section we show that its infrared action is Maxwell’s, and that the photon is massless. The
second statement is proved in three comparison models, and for the physical Wilson action
it is Hypothesis 1.

8.1    The infrared action is Maxwell’s

Theorem 8.1 (Uniqueness of the Maxwell form). 38 Let Π(p) be the induced quadratic form
of a U (1) gauge field on the D4 lattice model. Suppose Π is local (exponentially decaying
kernel), gauge invariant and invariant under the Weyl group W (D4 ), reflections included.
Then its O(p2 ) part is c Fµν Fµν for a single constant c, and c > 0.

Proof. By locality, Π(p) is analytic at p = 0. By gauge invariance, Π(p)a vanishes for
a ∝ p, so the O(p2 ) term is a quadratic form Q(F ) in the field strength F = p ∧ a ∈ Λ2 R4 .
    Λ2 R4 = Λ2+ ⊕ Λ2− (self-dual and anti-self-dual forms), each of dimension three. The
group W (D4 ) consists of the permutations of coordinates combined with even sign changes.
On each of Λ2± it acts by the three-dimensional standard representation of S4 : the even sign
changes act diagonally with distinct sign characters, and the permutations mix the basis
transitively. This representation is irreducible. By Schur’s lemma, an invariant quadratic
form on Λ2± is a scalar multiple of the norm, so Q = a|F+ |2 + b|F− |2 . A reflection in W (D4 )
reverses orientation and exchanges Λ2+ and Λ2− , so a = b and Q = c|F |2 .
                                                                P
    Positivity: by Theorem 6.10 restricted to U (1), θ⊤ Πθ = ω cω flux2ω ≥ θ⊤ Kc θ, where
Kc is the triangle part. This is a positive Maxwell form, so c > 0.
 38
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 8.1 (Uniqueness of the Maxwell form):                          TorusModel.D4.
diagonalQuadratic4_perm_isotropic_explicit; see Appendix F.




                                                    – 60 –
    An exact computation confirms the count: with |W (D4 )| = 192 there is one invariant
quadratic form on Λ2 . With the rotation subgroup (order 96) alone there are two, F 2 and
F ∧ F , so the reflections remove the θ-term. On D4 itself, eliminating the eight heavy link
modes by a Schur complement gives 4(|k|2 I − kk ⊤ ) at gauge weight 0 and 4|k|2 I4 at gauge
weight ξ ∗ = 1/9.

Remark 8.2 (Poisson model and the common light cone). In the Poisson model the configu-
ration average is statistically O(4) invariant, so the same argument gives a single Maxwell
form. Only the normalisation differs, and it is absorbed into e2 . On the weighted D4
crystal (β = 1) the symmetry is reduced to the stabiliser of the time axis. There are then
two invariant forms, F0i2 and F 2 , so on the crystal the photon light cone is not forced by
                                 ij
symmetry alone; numerically it agrees with the matter light cone to 0.8% in the principal
channel. In the physical Poisson model this ambiguity is absent: if every coupling depends
only on the same quadratic distance |x − y|Q , Theorem 4.11 makes all averaged two-point
functions O(Q) invariant, so photon and matter share one light cone exactly.
Theorem 8.3 (No confinement of the Villain U (1); computer-assisted). For the Villain
U (1) theory on the D4 clique complex with βV ≥ 1.614, Wilson loops obey a perimeter law,
so the U (1) gauge field does not confine. The proof uses the outward-rounded Kotecký–Preiss
certificate of Theorem 8.5.
Derivation: Appendix D.3.1.

Proof. In the Fröhlich–Spencer representation [35] (§D.1.10) the Villain partition func-
tion is a Gaussian photon field times a gas of integer monopole currents on the dual
complex, and a Wilson loop C enters as an external current. The Gaussian factor gives
− loghW (C)iGauss ≤ C1 Per(C), because in four dimensions the lattice Coulomb kernel
decays as |x|−2 and its double integral along the loop grows linearly in the length. The
monopole polymers have activities |z(γ)| ≤ e−c βV E(γ) with E(γ) > 0, so they decrease as
βV increases. The certificate of Theorem 8.5 gives
                            X
                       sup      |z(γ)|ea|γ| ≤ 8.6 × 10−5 < a = 0.005                (8.1)
                         p    γ∋p

at βV = 1.614, hence for every βV ≥ 1.614. The cluster expansion of the monopole gas
therefore converges, and the change of its logarithm caused by the loop current is bounded
by C2 Per(C) [35]. Together, − loghW (C)i ≤ (C1 +C2 ) Per(C). An area law with a positive
string tension would instead grow as the enclosed area and is therefore impossible.
     The physical model uses the Wilson action at βt = 3.643. Its Bessel weights
In (βt )/I0 (βt ) are a Hartman–Watson mixture of Villain weights (Lemma 8.6) in which
βV < 1.614 occurs with probability 0.046, so the Villain certificate does not cover it.
Non-confinement for the Wilson action is not claimed here; it belongs to the same open
question as Hypothesis 1.

8.2   Masslessness needs only a lower bound
After the character expansion, the dual variables of a U (1) lattice gauge theory are integer
electric fluxes n on the triangles. Integration over the link variables forces ∂n = 0 exactly:




                                           – 61 –
the flux is a closed 2-chain (Gauss’s law). In Fourier space B(k)n̂(k) = 0, where B = d∗1 is
the 32 × 12 symbol per site. Let S(k) = hn̂(k)n̂(k)∗ i.
Lemma 8.4 (Gauss-law lemma). 39 Suppose S(k) ≥ cβ Πker B(k) for some c > 0 (a lower
bound only), and suppose the limiting projection P (e) = limt→0 Πker B(te) depends on the
direction e. Then the flux two-point function is not summable, i.e. the photon is massless.
On D4 the second hypothesis holds: rank d1 (0) = 8, rank d1 (te) = 11 for t 6= 0, and
                         √
k(I − P (e2 ))P (e1 )kF = 2.
Proof. Suppose the two-point function is summable. Then S is continuous at 0 and
S(0) = limt S(te). Since range S(te) ⊂ ker B(te), passing to the limit gives range S(0) ⊂
range P (e′ ) for every direction e′ . On the other hand, the lower bound gives S(0) ≥ cβP (e).
For positive semidefinite matrices this implies ker S(0) ⊂ ker P (e), i.e. range P (e) ⊂
range S(0). Together, range P (e) ⊂ range P (e′ ) for all e, e′ . Exchanging e and e′ shows
that P (e) = P (e′ ), contradicting the direction dependence. The D4 ranks and the norm
are exact symbolic calculations, stable for t = 10−3 to 10−5 .

     For the Wilson action, nIn (β) = β2 (In−1 − In+1 ) gives hnp nq i = β 2 hsin θp sin θq i for
p 6= q. Masslessness of the flux is therefore masslessness of the field strength sin θ.

8.3   Periodic-cover Hodge calculation and its limitation
The Hodge zero modes must be counted on the actual periodic complex, not inferred from
a random finite Poisson sample. On such samples the mod-two first Betti number depends
on the sample and window (zero in some tested windows, four in others); consequently
the small eigenvalues of a sample cannot be relabelled as “four torus cycles plus gauge
modes” without an explicit periodic cover. For the periodic D4 root-link complex, the
exact connectivity theorem gives one connected component and b1 = 4 for odd side length
L, whereas even L has two components and b1 = 8. On the connected odd-L branch the
Fourier/Hodge calculation has zero-mode count

                                    4 + (L4 − 1) = L4 + 3,                                 (8.2)

with a strictly positive finite spectrum away from these modes; the periodic Fourier/Hodge
spectrum is checked exactly.
    This is a finite-volume consistency calculation: it identifies the admissible parity branch
and prevents a false Betti-number interpretation of the Poisson spectrum. It does not
derive periodicity, select odd L, or provide a uniform thermodynamic lower bound from
the original Poisson source. Those source-selection and infinite-volume steps remain open.

8.4   Three comparison models
Theorem 8.5 (Villain photon; computer-assisted). 40 For the Villain U (1) theory [36] on
the D4 clique complex, the flux variance satisfies Var ≥ (1 − c′ ) VarGauss with c′ ≤ 0.3147
for all βV ≥ 1.614. Hence the photon is massless there.
 39
   Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma 8.4 (Gauss-law lemma): TorusModel.GL.GL_massless; see Appendix F.
 40
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 8.5 (Villain photon; computer-assisted): TorusModel.GL.villain_
massless; see Appendix F.




                                             – 62 –
Proof. The Fröhlich–Spencer sine-Gordon representation [35] (§D.1.10) writes the flux gen-
erating function as a Gaussian times a monopole gas. A real shift φ → φ ± η of the dual
field changes the gas by a factor gas(η) with gas(η) = gas(−η). Jensen’s inequality gives
gas ≥ exp( 12 E[D+ + D− ]). Using |2 cos x − 2| ≤ x2 , the cluster expansion and Cauchy–
Schwarz, one gets − log gas ≤ (2π 2 /(3eb)) εF kF k2 whenever the Kotecký–Preiss condition
holds for the modified activities |zγ |e(a+b)|γ| . This yields the variance lower bound with
c′ = (2π 2 /(3eb))βV εF ; the code uses the more conservative 4π 2 /b.
     The Kotecký–Preiss condition and εF are evaluated for the monopole polymers of the
D4 complex. The evaluation uses a potential dynamic programme over the 316 link states
of the vertex link (the tesseract), an exact root-vertex programme, the exact identity
α(q) = eq α(0), exact enumeration of polymers up to size 12, and a cone-filling bound
(Dehn constant K ≤ 6n2 + 2n) for the tail. All quantities are sums, products, maxima
and quotients of positive numbers, and c′ is monotone in each input. Outward rounding by
the factor 1 + 2−50 per operation, together with interval evaluation of the special functions
(Arb [37], without relying on the system mathematical library), gives the certified values.
At βV = 1.614 these are: Kotecký–Preiss sum ≤ 8.6 × 10−5 against the margin a = 0.005,
and c′ ≤ 0.3147; the dynamic-programme, root-vertex and joint rotor checks are finite,
reproducible tests. Lemma 8.4 then gives masslessness.

Lemma 8.6 (Hartman–Watson mixture). For real ν ≥ 0, Iν (β)/I0 (β) = EHW [e−ν T /2 ],

evaluations.

Proof. Step 1 (mixture). By Lemma 8.6, w(x) = ET [e−x T /2 ] for real x. So µ = Epost [νT ],

where νT is the Gaussian with weight p e−Tp np /2 . The posterior law of T = (Tp ) is

                        Q
proportional to Z(T ) p HW(dTp ), with Z(T ) = c det(B ⊤ T B)−1/2 . Z is decreasing in
each Tp . Hence the conditional likelihood ratio of Tp given the others is decreasing, and
the conditional law of Tp is stochastically smaller than the prior. In particular P (Tp >
1/β ∗ | rest) ≤ p∗ , so the set W = {p : Tp > 1/β ∗ } is stochastically dominated by an
independent Bernoulli(p∗ ) set.
     Step 2 (total variance). Varµ (F ) = Epost [VarνT F ] + Varpost (EνT F ) ≥ Epost hf, X(T )f i,
where X(T ) = B(B ⊤ T B)−1 B ⊤ .
     Step 3 (monotonicity). X(T ) is operator-decreasing in T . Replace Tp by 1/β ∗ on
strong triangles (Tp ≤ 1/β ∗ ) and by +∞ (flux forced to zero) on W . This increases T ,
so X(T ) ≥ Xβ ∗ ,W pointwise. The bound is decreasing in W , so by Step 1 the posterior
expectation is at least the expectation over a Bernoulli(p∗ ) set.
     Step 4 (local corrector). By Legendre duality, hf, XW f i = sup{2hf, gi − kgk2 /β ∗ :
g closed, g|W = 0}. Let g0 be the optimiser without W . For each t ∈ W choose a
                                             P
tetrahedron T (t) 3 t and set g ′ = g0 − t∈W (g0 )t σt ∂T (t), where ∂T (t) is the closed
boundary 2-chain of T (t) and σt = ±1 its coeﬀicient on t. If the tetrahedra T (t) contain
no other triangle of W , then g ′ is closed and vanishes on W . Its energy exceeds that
of g0 by at most 4(g0 )2t per corrected triangle plus cross terms of the same size. For
              P
smooth f , E t∈W (g0 )2t = p∗ kg0 k2 (1 + o(1)), which gives the factor 1 − 8p∗ . A triangle
all of whose tetrahedra contain another triangle of W requires at least two triangles of W
within a bounded neighbourhood. This has probability at most C ′ p∗2 , and such clusters
are corrected with a bounded number of tetrahedra, which contributes Cp∗2 .

Remark 8.8. The first-order bound is 1 − 8p∗ = 0.64 at β ∗ = 1.6 (p∗ = 0.045) and 0.91
at β ∗ = 1 (p∗ = 0.0112). With β ∗ = 1, masslessness holds as soon as C < 7.2 × 103 . We
have not computed C explicitly. It is a count of bounded local configurations, but until it
is computed the statement for the physical coupling rests on this estimate.

Lemma 8.9 (Rank-one defect). 42 Let G be the symmetric matrix of the flux energy E(x) =
x · Gx, let m be the minimiser at fixed boundary data, and let u be the triangle vector of the
weak triangle t, with u · Gu 6= 0. Releasing the direction u changes the energy by exactly a
rank-one term:
                                  u · Gm               (u · Gm)2

Derivation: Appendix D.3.3.
 42
      Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma 8.9 (Rank-one defect): TorusModel.HWD; see Appendix F.




                                                – 64 –
Proof. Put λ = (u·Gm)/(u·Gu). By symmetry of G, E(m−λu) = E(m)−2λ u·Gm+λ2 u·
Gu = E(m) − (u · Gm)2 /(u · Gu). The new configuration is the G-orthogonal projection of

Theorem 8.10 (Poisson Gaussian photon). For the Gaussian gauge form on a Poisson
complex, the gauge-orthogonal part of the field equals the homogenisation energy of the scalar
graph Laplacian, and, under the hypotheses of the spectral convergence theorem of Armstrong
and Venkatraman [39], Var ≥ c/|k|2 . Hence the photon is massless, conditionally on those
hypotheses.
Derivation: Appendix D.3.4.

Proof. Write the quadratic gauge energy as kd1 Ak2 and decompose the link field orthog-
onally as A = d0 φ + AT + h, with d∗0 AT = 0 and h harmonic. Gauge transformations
remove d0 φ and h is treated by Lemma 8.12. On the transverse subspace the nonzero sin-
gular values of d1 agree with those of the corresponding scalar graph-Laplacian complex,
so minimising at fixed macroscopic two-form is the cell problem of Theorem 8.13. The
cited spectral homogenisation theorem applies under the stated stationarity, moment and
coercivity hypotheses and yields a deterministic limit qhom .
     The Poisson law is O(4)-invariant. The representation Λ2 R4 = Λ2+ ⊕ Λ2− and reflection
symmetry therefore imply, as in Theorem 8.14, qhom (F ) = ε|F |2 . The cited theorem gives
a positive effective coeﬀicient on the supercritical cluster, hence ε > 0 under its hypotheses.
Inverting the quadratic form on the transverse modes gives S(k) ≥ c|k|−2 Πker B(k) . Since
the limiting projection depends on the direction of k, Lemma 8.4 rules out a summable
field-strength two-point function. Thus the Gaussian photon is massless. The use of the
external homogenisation theorem is conditional on its hypotheses, not hidden inside the
last inversion.

8.5   The physical Wilson action
For the integer (physical) Wilson action, Steps 1–3 of Theorem 8.7 still hold. Each com-
ponent νT is then an integer inhomogeneous Villain measure, and Step 2 needs

                              VarνT (F ) ≥ (1 − c′ (T )) hf, Xβ ∗ ,W f i                  (8.6)

for Villain theories with couplings ≥ β ∗ and a sparse set W of deleted triangles. This is
the Fröhlich–Spencer bound for an inhomogeneous monopole gas, and it is Hypothesis 1.
The local corrector of Step 4 applies unchanged.

Numerical result 8.11 (Why the candidate defect constructions fail). All candidate
constructions based on blocks or defect polymers have been tested and fail quantitatively.

• With the actual Hartman–Watson distribution at β = 3.643, 74% of the weak triangles
  have finite strength κ̂ > 0.1, and 15% are strong defects. The expected energy loss
  per encounter is 0.074–0.107, which is 2.5–3.6 times the 0.03 that the Kotecký–Preiss
  margin allows (Hartman–Watson weak/strong-defect calculation).




                                               – 65 –
• Exact block integration shows that the joint loss is super-additive when weak triangles
  are switched off completely (median 1.03–1.21). A flux line of length n can lose all its
  energy to n − 2 nearby defects, as shown by the exact block-loss enumeration.

• A triangle can lose up to 59% of its energy to one nearby weak triangle (Lemma 8.9),
  i.e. the local effective coupling is about 0.41β (local weak-triangle loss analysis).

     The loss is not protected by any symmetry. What is needed is a multiscale method that
keeps the cancellations between defects, i.e. the same mathematical bottleneck class as the
Yang–Mills gap; Section 12.2 distinguishes the logical dependencies. Photon masslessness
is proved for the Villain action (computer-assisted) and, conditionally, for the continuous
Wilson weight (one combinatorial constant) and the Poisson Gaussian action (homogeni-
sation hypotheses). The open step is only the transfer to the integer Wilson action; it is
not logically dependent on the Yang–Mills mass-gap theorem.

8.6   Per-sample homogenisation
The scalar Poisson-graph spectral theorem [39] is not by itself a theorem for this cochain-
valued gauge energy. The operator comparison, uniform filling estimates and topological
conditions in Appendix A are additional requirements; the homogenisation conclusion here
is conditional on them. For a single Poisson sample one needs the effective Maxwell coef-
ficient to exist and to be isotropic. Let K be the Poisson Rips complex at density 1 and
radius rc = 1.5 (mean degree ≈ 25).
                                                                        P          β
Lemma 8.12 (Holonomies of holes decouple). 43 The Villain weight          n exp(− 2 |d1 A −
2πn|2 ) is invariant under translations of A by ker d1 . The image of ker d1 in the torus
RE /2πZE is a closed subtorus Z1 . Consequently the holonomies around holes of the complex
are Haar distributed and independent of every curvature observable.

Proof. For a ∈ ker d1 , d1 (A + a) = d1 A, so the weight is unchanged. Since d1 is an integer
matrix, ker d1 is a rational subspace and has an integer basis. Its image in RE /2πZE is
therefore a closed connected subgroup, i.e. a subtorus. The measure Haar × (weight) is Z1 -
invariant, so its conditional law along Z1 is the Haar measure of Z1 . Curvature observables
are functions of d1 A and do not depend on the Z1 coordinate.

Theorem 8.13 (Cell formula). The Dirichlet cell energy with boundary layer satisfies
ER (F0 )/R4 → qhom (F0 ) almost surely and in L1 , where qhom is a deterministic quadratic
form. The proof uses the subadditive ergodic theorem of Akcoglu and Krengel [40].
Derivation: Appendix D.3.4.

Proof. For a cube QR and constant two-form F0 , let ER (F0 ) be the minimum of the
quadratic energy among fields agreeing with the aﬀine datum in a boundary layer. If
disjoint cubes are tiled inside a larger cube, their minimisers may be pasted; only the fixed-
width interfaces contribute an O(|∂QR |)|F0 |2 error. Adding that boundary term makes the
 43
    Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma 8.12 (Holonomies of holes decouple): TorusModel.HC.weight_invariant;
see Appendix F.




                                              – 66 –
family subadditive. Translation of the Poisson configuration translates the cell problem,
while finite intensity and the quadratic trial field give integrability. The multiparameter
Akcoglu–Krengel theorem therefore yields an almost-sure and L1 limit of R−4 ER (F0 ), and
ergodicity makes the limit deterministic. Homogeneity of the minimum under F0 7→ tF0
and the parallelogram identity for quadratic energies show that the limit is a quadratic
form qhom . This proves exactly the cell formula; positivity of qhom is a separate input
(Theorem 8.14).

Theorem 8.14 (Isotropy). 44 qhom (F ) = ε|F |2 with ε ≥ 0. Moreover ε > 0 under the
coercivity input supplied by the supercritical-cluster homogenisation theorem [39] on the
transverse modes.

Proof. The law of the Poisson process is O(4)-invariant, so qhom is an SO(4)-invariant
quadratic form on Λ2 R4 = Λ2+ ⊕ Λ2− . These are two inequivalent, absolutely irreducible
representations, so Schur’s lemma gives qhom = a|F+ |2 + b|F− |2 . Reflections exchange Λ2± ,
so a = b =: ε, and ε ≥ 0 because qhom is a limit of energies. Strict positivity needs a
uniform lower bound on the transverse cell energy; this is the conditional homogenisation
input of Theorem 8.10. The unrestricted Poisson–Rips complex has isolated vertices and
empty shells almost surely, so a uniform coercivity bound cannot hold sample by sample
on all of it; the input concerns the supercritical cluster.

are precisely those stated at the beginning of Appendix A.

     By Lemma 8.4, this lower bound alone gives a massless photon on each sample. The
matching upper bound (Theorem A.5) identifies the coeﬀicient exactly. It is proved con-
ditionally on published percolation theorems and is not a premise of the main argument.

8.7   Direct dual-weight comparison and its limitation
The Wilson model tested here has uniform plaquette coupling β = 3.643. The random weak
couplings of the Hartman–Watson representation are auxiliary variables of a proof method.
Direct Fourier expansion instead gives normalised Wilson weights wW (n) = In (β)/I0 (β)
and Villain weights wV (n) = e−n /(2βV ) . This comparison proposes βV ≃ 3.038, versus the
                                    2


certified Villain threshold 1.614, a ratio about 1.88. This is a candidate weight comparison
with rounded constants, not a Wilson masslessness certificate. The matched Villain signed
dynamic-programming cross-check at this coupling is retained as a numerical diagnostic
in Appendix G, row calc:matched-villain-crosscheck. Its descriptive result and state
files, together with their expected hashes, are listed in the provenance index; no executable
script or standalone log for that cross-check is distributed, so it is not promoted to a
certificate or a result for the integer Wilson action.
      Even if wW (n) ≥ wV (n) is proved for every integer n, the partition function is a
constrained sum over divergence-free flux configurations. A two-point function is a ratio
of such sums, with a changed insertion or constraint. Increasing every weight need not
increase that ratio. Thus a correlation comparison or an applicable quantitative weak-
coupling theorem on the present complex is additionally required. The unresolved assertion
is this model-specific Wilson transfer. It should not be described either as a solved internal
lemma or as the absence of all weak-coupling Coulomb-phase theorems. The massive
strong-coupling Yang–Mills estimates do not establish this massless abelian conclusion.

8.8   Sector Lagrangian and derivation
Final sector action.     Before canonical field rescaling, the infrared electromagnetic sector
has the form

After electroweak breaking Aµ = sW Wµ3 + cW Bµ . The formula contains no photon mass

Derivation chain. The history-algebra commutant supplies the Abelian block; the mat-
ter determinant gives the positive closed-walk quadratic form of Theorem 6.10. Gauge
invariance supplies the longitudinal Ward null, while statistical isotropy reduces the trans-
verse quadratic tensor to the Maxwell form (Theorem 8.1). The cell formula and the
coercive lower bound give the corresponding homogenised form on the Poisson comparison
model. The electroweak stabiliser in Chapter 9 identifies the surviving field as the photon.

Coulomb’s law. For charges at rest the action reduces to Coulomb’s law. After the
                                                                µ
canonical rescaling Aµ → eAµ the source term becomes eAµ Jem      . For static charges only
                    0 = ρ survives, and the field equation reduces to Gauss’s law ∇·E = eρ.
the charge density Jem
A point charge q, measured in units of e, produces E = eq r̂/(4πr2 ), and a second charge
q ′ feels
                                  e2 qq ′   α qq ′         e2
                              F =         =        ,  α =     ,                      (8.12)
                                   4πr2      r2            4π
in natural units [41]. This is Coulomb’s law; the admissible values of q are the quantised
charges of the chiral module (§6.5), and α is fixed by matching. The inverse-square form
holds at all distances only because the action contains no photon mass term: a mass mγ
would replace the potential 1/r by e−mγ r /r. The absence of that term is proved for the
comparison models of this chapter (Theorem 8.5) and is Hypothesis 1 for the physical
Wilson action, so Coulomb’s law at long distances has the same status.

External results. The per-sample homogenisation argument uses the multiparameter
subadditive ergodic theorem and the cited supercritical-cluster and local-uniqueness esti-
mates. These results apply only after their stationarity, integrability, coercivity and filling
assumptions have been verified for the chosen random complex.

Origin and determination of the coeﬀicients. The Maxwell tensor form and posi-
tivity of its quadratic coeﬀicient are derived in the finite and stated homogenised settings.
Its normalisation e is vacuum and matching data, not a consequence of the two mini-
mal axioms. The numerical Wilson and Villain couplings in this chapter are fixed-input
benchmarks for comparison measures.

Open remainder. Photon masslessness is established for the certified Villain model and
under the stated assumptions for the Gaussian/homogenised comparisons. A correlation-
preserving transfer to the physical integer-Wilson measure and then to the unrestricted
torus source remains Hypothesis 1. Consequently the displayed continuum Lagrangian is
the conditional infrared sector, not an unconditional continuum limit of the literal source.

9.3    The electroweak gap
Because the photon is massless, the correlations of W , Z and h cannot cluster uncondi-
tionally: they couple to long-range electromagnetic fluctuations. The correct statement is
conditional on a fixed electromagnetic background. That is Hypothesis 2.

     The critical field m2W /e ≈ 1020 T exceeds every magnetic field in the universe by more

lattice computation reproduces the Landau levels (ratio 0.999–0.995) and the instability,
and confirms the lower bound in 24 random backgrounds (Gaussian electroweak bound and
Landau-level analysis).
