Theorem 9.4 (All orders of perturbation theory). 49 As formal power series in the cou-
plings, every connected correlation function of the heavy fields at fixed background A is
                                   ′
bounded at each order n by Cn e−m |x−y| , for every m′ below the Combes–Thomas rate of
Theorem 9.3, with Cn independent of A and of the volume.
Derivation: Appendix D.4.1.

Proof. Let µ be any rate below the Combes–Thomas rate and choose m′ < µ. At fixed
order n there are finitely many connected lattice graphs. Choose a spanning tree of one
such graph. The propagators outside the tree are uniformly bounded, while every tree edge
obeys |CA (u, v)| ≤ Ce−µ|u−v| . For one convolution,
      X                           ′
                                       X          ′                        ′
         e−µ|x−z| e−µ|z−y| ≤ e−m |x−y|    e−(µ−m )(|x−z|+|z−y|) ≤ Cµ,m′ e−m |x−y| .  (9.7)
       z                                  z

The last sum is finite and independent of the volume and of A. Remove the leaves of the
spanning tree one at a time and apply this estimate at each removal. After all internal
                                                                 ′
vertices have been summed, the graph is bounded by Cn,m′ e−m |x−y| . The physical lattice
cutoff makes every vertex factor finite, and no photon propagator occurs because the photon
is held fixed. Summing the finite set of graphs proves the assertion at order n.

Theorem 9.5 (Uniqueness of the electroweak state). Suppose Hypothesis 2 holds and the
abelian sector is controlled by the convergent Villain expansion of Theorem 8.5. After the
heavy fields have been integrated out, let ζA (γ) be the combined polymer activity in a fixed
background A. If there is an a > 0 such that
                                       X
                                   sup     |ζA (γ)|ea|γ| ≤ a                            (9.8)
                                     x γ∋x

uniformly in A and in the volume, then the electroweak sector has a unique infinite-volume
state for each background. For the physical Wilson action, Hypothesis 1 and the displayed
combined-activity bound are both required.
Derivation: Appendix D.4.2.

Proof. Write the finite-volume weight as the abelian polymer gas multiplied by the local
polymers obtained from the conditional heavy-field integral. The displayed inequality is
the Kotecký–Preiss criterion for their combined activities. It makes the connected-cluster
series absolutely and uniformly convergent: a cluster containing a fixed site is dominated
by a summable rooted-polymer series. If two boundary conditions are imposed in a box,
the difference of any local expectation is a sum over clusters joining the observable to
the boundary. The same bound makes that sum tend to zero as the boundary recedes.
Hence all boundary conditions have the same thermodynamic limit, which is uniqueness.
Hypothesis 2 supplies exponential locality, but it does not by itself supply the small norm
in the displayed criterion; that norm is an independent premise of this theorem. The finite
covariance input and step-map certificates are distributed with the reproducibility package
(Table G, row “Electroweak covariance input and finite step map”).
 49
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 9.4 (All orders of perturbation theory): TorusModel.exponential_
convolution_pointwise; see Appendix F.




                                              – 73 –
9.4    Non-perturbative reduction to a finite-depth problem
Proposition 9.6 (Reduction). 50 Hypothesis 2 follows from two statements.

 (1) Ultraviolet stability: the four-dimensional SU (2) × U (1) + Higgs lattice theory re-
     mains in a controlled weak-coupling regime from the physical cutoff Λ = 6.8×1017 GeV
     down to mW . This is 53 renormalisation steps with block factor 2, large fields in-
     cluded.

 (2) Infrared convergence: the cluster expansion converges at the mass scale.

The flow is weakly coupled throughout (g2 ≈ 0.5–0.65), and no continuum limit is needed
because the cutoff is physical.
Derivation: Appendix D.4.2.

Proof. Choose block factor two. The ratio Λ/mW = 6.8 × 1017 /80.369 lies between 252
and 253 , so 53 blocks reach a scale at or below the vector-boson mass. By ultraviolet
stability, induction over these 53 steps keeps every effective action inside the same gauge-
covariant analytic domain and controls the accumulated irrelevant remainder. At the last
step the massive propagators decay on the block scale. Infrared convergence then applies
the Kotecký–Preiss criterion to the remaining local and large-field polymers, yielding an
absolutely convergent expansion and exponential decay. These are precisely the two as-
sertions of Hypothesis 2; no infinite-cutoff limit is used. Conversely, the numerical bounds
printed below do not prove the first assertion: its interval norm-transfer and contraction
certificates remain open, so this proposition is a logical reduction rather than a proof of
the electroweak hypothesis.

Numerical result 9.7 (Infrared convergence). At block scale a = c/mW , for unitary-
gauge SU (2) gauge–Higgs at β = 4/g 2 = 9.52 and κ = βc2 , use a small/large-field split at
θ0 = 0.5 and the Kotecký–Preiss test with the D4 plaquette neighbour count ν = 20. The
convergence condition (zsmall + zlarge ) e (ν + 1) < 1 holds for c ≥ 10: the left side is 0.863 at
c = 10 and 0.214 at c = 20. The coupling barely runs below mW , so c ≥ 10 can be chosen
freely; these are the electroweak infrared-convergence constants.

      The input of the infrared step is an effective action of Wilson-plus-hopping form with
g 2 within about 10% of its perturbative value. That is exactly the output of the ultraviolet
step. So Hypothesis 2 reduces to ultraviolet stability over 53 steps. Because the depth is
finite, only the product of 53 per-step constants has to be controlled. Unlike Balaban’s
continuum limit [45, 46] (§D.1.14), no uniformity over infinitely many scales is needed. The
step map consists of six operations:

1. block averaging over 24 blocks;

2. axial gauge fixing of block-internal tree links, which reduces the plaquette polymer
   entropy to ν = 6;
 50
   Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 9.6 (Reduction): TorusModel.ew_scale_ratio_between; see Ap-
pendix F.




                                             – 74 –
3. a split into small fields (|θp | ≤ θ0 (k), |H| ≤ h0 (k)) and large fields;

4. in the small-field region, expansion around the Gaussian fixed point with anharmonicity
   βθ04 /24 ≤ η = 0.5;

5. in the large-field region, suppression by e−β(1−cos θ0 ) (gauge) and e−λeff h0 (Higgs), with
                                                                                4


   the Kotecký–Preiss criterion [47] (§D.1.8);

6. absorption of local terms into the next-step couplings.

Proposition 9.8 (Same-measure Ward and RP retention). Define a block-two coarse link
by the straight product of its two fine links and define the coarse Higgs field by averaging
fine Higgs fields transported to the block base over all shortest paths. If the reflection plane
lies on a block boundary, this map is local, gauge equivariant, reflection equivariant and
half-space preserving. Consequently its measure push-forward preserves reflection positivity
and gauge invariance exactly. At every stationary coarse background the joint gauge–Higgs
Hessian annihilates the infinitesimal gauge orbit; in particular the unbroken electromagnetic
direction remains exactly null at every finite RG step.

Proof. Intermediate gauge factors cancel in the two-link product, while each parallel-
transported Higgs field transforms at the block base. Reflection permutes the set of shortest
paths. Hence the block map B has all four stated covariance properties. For a positive-
half cylinder function F , the OS form of B∗ µ is the OS form of F ◦ B under µ, and is
non-negative. Gauge invariance of the push-forward effective action gives DΓ Rω = 0;
differentiating at a stationary background gives D2 Γ R = 0.

    The BKAR interpolation is the tree-parameter interpolation that rewrites a multivari-
able polymer interaction as a sum over forests; here the non-Wilson version is the remaining
step whose norm has not been bounded.

Numerical result 9.9 (Per-step constants and the two remaining analytic estimates).
With the three-loop Standard-Model flow below M∗ and the D-term boundary values of
the fixed-reference vacuum V1 of Table 16 above it, every one of the 53 steps has an open
gauge window (minimum margin +0.092) and a Higgs potential bounded below (minimum
λeff = +0.0018). The Balaban-type constrained covariance

                                  Γ = C − CQ⊤ (QCQ⊤ )−1 QC                                       (9.9)

has hA2 i = 0.12844, hAAe i = 0.01125 and hF 2 i = 0.46875. The block–Bloch covariance
is obtained by grouping sites into periodic blocks and diagonalising the resulting block-
translation action by block momentum. A block-Bloch calculation gives the same values
to five decimal places on momentum grids corresponding to L = 16, 32, 48, after reproduc-
ing the original 84 computation; thus no finite-size drift is visible at this precision. The
value hF 2 i = 15/32 is a candidate exact identity, supported by the block-Bloch covariance
computation.
     The tree estimate K = 4.5–7.3 counts the local part of the non-abelian cubic vertex
as an irrelevant activity and does not use the Ward cancellation. After parametrising the
activity gauge-covariantly, absorbing the local F 2 term into the next-step coupling, and




                                               – 76 –
removing the resulting double count, the contributions are
       ε3 = 0.0005–0.0008,      ε4 = 0.0059–0.0094,       εH = 0.0002–0.0103.         (9.10)
Consequently the scalar diagnostic K = eBε rises from 0.13 at step 0 to about 0.40 at
step 53, below the small-field target 1 and the irrelevant-polymer target 3/4; this is a
gauge-covariant renormalised-activity estimate, not yet the analytic polymer norm. Of
the sensitivity tests, the two single changes pass and the combined change fails: counting
twenty neighbouring polymers separately gives K = 0.69, replacing the measured irrelevant
fraction 0.09 by 0.25 gives K = 0.42, while imposing both changes gives K = 1.23.
     These numbers are a feasibility result, not a proof. The outward-rounded interval
computation certifies only the covariance input supL BL ≤ 8.464. The one-component value
0.0205 is not the SU (2) colour activity; the exact radial correction below changes the same-
                          SU (2)
bookkeeping value to Knominal = 0.836199 > 3/4. It does not certify the source-specific
non-Abelian L3 /L4 operator bounds or their transfer to Balaban’s analytic polymer norm.
The remaining computer-assisted proof programme is therefore to certify those operator
bounds, transfer them with explicit tree constants, prove dimension-six contraction over
all 53 steps, and join the small- and large-field regions. Until those steps are complete,
Hypothesis 2 remains open.

      nite stress 0.6025322010 < 1), and a block Poincaré argument gives volume-uniform
      fourth-chaos summability.

       irrelevant residual vanishes. Removing this double count gives 0.2767964346 < 1
       before the Brydges–Kennedy–Abdesselam–Rivasseau (BKAR) norm; the cubic and
       gauge–Higgs entries remain estimates.

Proposition 9.11 (Finite low-order data do not prove the analytic polymer bound).
51 Fix a finite order q. Gaussian covariance data and derivatives of a local gauge–Higgs

interaction through order q at the vacuum do not, by themselves, imply a uniform analytic
polymer-norm bound.

Proof. Let xp = 1 − d−1 ReTr Up , which is gauge invariant, and choose N > q. Adding
M xN p leaves every derivative through order q at xp = 0 unchanged, while its supremum
on the analytic disk |xp | ≤ R is M RN and hence is unbounded as M → ∞. The same
construction uses M (H † H)N in the Higgs sector. Thus no fixed finite jet and low-moment
list controls the all-orders analytic norm. This disproves only the shortcut from these
computational data to the polymer estimate; it is not a no-go theorem for proving the
estimate from the fully specified action.

Remark 9.16 (What kind of problem this is). The cited constructive results provide a
method, not the missing theorem. Balaban’s renormalisation-group analysis controls pure
lattice Yang–Mills theory on a finite torus, in three and four dimensions and without
Higgs fields, while Balaban–Imbrie–Jaffe treat the abelian Higgs model in two and three
dimensions. Neither result is directly a four-dimensional SU (2) × U (1) plus Higgs polymer
theorem. The corrected activity estimate shows that the physical electroweak couplings
have a finite, but not large, margin. What is missing is a source-specific definition and
proof of the complex field domain, regulator, local extraction and analytic polymer norm,
including the non-Abelian colour and gauge–Higgs contractions, followed by the explicit
composition of all 53 steps. Thus this is a new model-specific operator estimate guided by
known constructive methods, not merely insertion of constants into an existing theorem.
It differs from Hypothesis 1, where the diﬀiculty is disorder (randomly weak couplings),
and from Hypothesis 3, where the diﬀiculty is strong coupling in the infrared. Section 12.2




                                            – 81 –
distinguishes these dependencies. Hypothesis 2 remains the most tractable of the three,
but the norm-transfer step is substantial. The Standard Model is in the same unproved
state.

9.5   Finite tensor cancellations and the remaining norm estimate
The local determinant calculation includes all 44,218 declared signed, plaquette-admissible
support-five fields. The 128-bit Arb certificate, with truncation order 35, reports

 η ≤ 0.930078076442942 < 1,                  R36 ≤ 0.029226904974017 < 0.032598012863727. (9.28)

Together with the support-four computation this covers the stated discrete fields of support
at most five. It is not a bound for arbitrary continuous or noncommuting fields. At support
six the same fixed-background Neumann estimate has an obstruction; a new expansion or
estimate is required. This obstruction does not disprove an electroweak gap.
     For nonnegative uniform activities a6 , a8 , the triangular recurrence has the explicit
solution
                                4a6                                       16a8
       x6,n = 4−n x6,0 +            (1 − 4−n ),      x8,n = 16−n x8,0 +        (1 − 16−n ).   (9.29)
                                 3                                         15
These formulae follow by summing the geometric series in x6,n+1 = x6,n /4+a6 and x8,n+1 =
x8,n /16 + a8 . Hence each component stays between its initial value and its fixed point. The
53-step arithmetic passes for the proposed inputs. Establishing that those inputs bound
the microscopic activities in one volume-uniform polymer norm is still required.

The connected degree-six quantity. Three quadratic hopping insertions Bi = xT Ai y
have total field degree six. Their connected contribution is κ3 (B1 , B2 , B3 ); κ6 (B) instead
has field degree twelve. For a centered real Gaussian z = (x, y) with covariance Σ, put

When the cross covariance is zero, changing x to −x leaves the Gaussian law invariant and
changes all three Bi to their negatives. The third joint cumulant then vanishes.
     The physical infinitesimal gauge generators are anti-Hermitian, for example iσa ; their
realifications are skew-symmetric. In a centered Gaussian model with equal marginal co-
variances and symmetric cross covariance, the exchange (x, y) 7→ (y, x) preserves the law




                                                    – 82 –
and sends xT Ai y to its negative for every such generator. Thus the joint moment-generating
function is invariant under (t1 , t2 , t3 ) 7→ (−t1 , −t2 , −t3 ), and its cubic derivative is zero.
This proves the pure three-gauge cancellation under these covariance assumptions, includ-
ing symmetric anisotropic cross covariance. Scans in a Hermitian basis cannot be used
directly as physical gauge-generator bounds. A scalar insertion with A = I is even un-
der the exchange, so the mixed tensor κ3 (Bscalar , Ba , Bb ) can survive. Its finite Gaussian
construction does not supply the interacting, covariance-normalised bound.

Lemma 9.17 (Charge conjugation and finite blocking). 54 Let a finite bosonic Wilson–
Higgs measure µ be invariant under C(U, z, H) = (Ū , z̄, H̄). Suppose a real observable Y is
odd under C and its moment-generating function exists near zero. Then every odd cumulant
of Y vanishes. If a block map B commutes with C, the push-forward B∗ µ has the same
conjugation symmetry and the same conclusion for its odd observables.
                                         R            R
Proof. Changing variables by C gives etY dµ = e−tY dµ. The logarithm is even near
zero, so all its odd derivatives at zero vanish. For the coarse measure, functoriality of
push-forward gives
                                 C∗ (B∗ µ) = B∗ (C∗ µ) = B∗ µ.                         (9.33)
In the bosonic action each plaquette or hopping term appears through its real part, and the
radial potential depends on H † H. They are unchanged by conjugation; Haar and Lebesgue
measures are unchanged as well. Finally, entrywise conjugation preserves the order of a link
product and commutes with a real weighted sum of transported Higgs fields. It therefore
commutes with the two-link/shortest-path block map of Proposition 9.8. Iterating the
push-forward identity proves symmetry retention at every finite step.

    The lemma applies to a local hypercharge source only when the background and local
projector also preserve this involution. It does not establish the corresponding property of
a chiral fermion measure, which needs a separate check.

What remains open. A scalar absolute-moment conversion can give an upper bound
larger than the required activity margin; this shows that the particular estimate is in-
suﬀicient, not that the true cumulant violates the margin. Local projection and tensor
cancellations must be retained before absolute values are taken. The required estimate is
for the mixed gauge–Higgs connected tensor in the actual interacting measure, with its
regulator, boundary sewing and volume-uniform constants. The finite cancellations above
do not prove that estimate, the final infrared clustering step, or the pullback to the literal
torus source. Finite reflection positivity and exact Ward retention remain valid in their
stated representative.

9.6   Higgs stability at fixed reference inputs
With the Standard-Model flow alone, λ(µ) < 0 above about 1010 GeV (the known metasta-
bility of the electroweak vacuum), and large Higgs fields are not suppressed. Bounding
 54
   Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma 9.17 (Charge conjugation and finite blocking): TorusModel.odd_
weighted_moment_of_involution; see Appendix F.




                                              – 83 –
the potential from below by cutoff terms such as |H|6 /Λ2 does not help. The infinite-
volume Euclidean path integral would then select the global minimum at large field, and
the reconstructed vacuum would not be ours. Ultraviolet stability for our vacuum therefore
requires:

Definition 9.18 (Higgs-stability condition). 55 A vacuum satisfies the Higgs-stability con-
dition if its Higgs potential is bounded below at every scale between mW and the cutoff.

          Since the quartic couplings are D-terms, the boundary value at the matching scale M∗
is
                                                        
                                λSM (M∗ ) = 18 g22 + gY2 cos2 2β ≥ 0,                         (9.34)
with the second doublet at ∼ M∗ . The condition is then equivalent to λSM (µ) ≥ 0 for all
µ ≤ M∗ , together with 0 ≤ cos2 2β ≤ 1.

Proposition 9.19 (Fixed-input stability benchmarks; numerical). We use the three-loop
Standard-Model beta functions with four-loop QCD and NNLO matching of Buttazzo et al.
[48]. Our implementation reproduces their λ(MP ) = −0.0143 as −0.0144 with the 2013
inputs. With the specified inputs mh = 125.20 GeV and MW = 80.369 GeV:

• vacuum V1 (M∗ = 1.59 × 1010 GeV) satisfies the Higgs-stability condition iff mt ≤
  172.89 GeV at αs = 0.1180. The bound moves by ±0.32 GeV per ±0.0009 in αs , and a
  one-loop threshold band at M∗ shifts it by −0.08 GeV;

• vacuum V2 (M∗ = 8.13 × 1010 GeV) requires mt ≤ 172.41 GeV (borderline);

• the tested V3 and higher fixed reference scales (M∗ ≥ 5 × 1011 GeV) fail in the stated
  input window. This does not exclude those winding classes after the continuous moduli
  and matching scale are varied.

At mt = 172.57 GeV, V1 has cos2 2β = 0.031, i.e. tan β ≈ 1.19, and tan β → 1 as
mt approaches the bound. Above M∗ , the one-loop flow of the two-Higgs-doublet D-term
quartics (λ1 = λ2 = (g22 + gY2 )/4, λ3 = (g22 − gY2 )/4, λ4 = −g22 /2 at M∗ ) keeps the potential
                                                              √
bounded below up to Λ. The D-flat combination λ3 + λ4 + λ1 λ2 vanishes at M∗ and grows
to +0.051 at Λ, and λ2 stays positive; this combines the Higgs-stability, two-Higgs flow and
seed-scale checks.
Derivation: Appendix D.4.3.

Proof. This is a reproducible numerical derivation, not an analytic theorem about the exact

an ODE. An adaptive integrator propagates outward-rounded intervals in t = log(µ/mt )
and stores the lower endpoint of λ on every step. For a fixed catalogue scale M∗ , bisection
in mt locates the value for which that lower endpoint first touches zero. Monotonicity of

9.7   Sector Lagrangian and derivation
Final sector action. With canonically normalised gauge fields, the electroweak sector
used in the low-energy theory is
                 1 a aµν 1                     X
      LEW = − Wµν     W       − Bµν B µν +          |Dµ Hr |2 − V (Hu , Hd )
                 4              4
                                              r=u,d                                   (9.36)
                                                                                   
              − Q̄L Yu He u uR + Q̄L Yd Hd dR + L̄L Ye Hd eR + L̄L Yν H
                                                                      e u νR + h.c. .

The couplings g2 and gY occur in Dµ and in the field strengths. At the matching scale the
quartic part of V obeys the D-term boundary conditions stated in §9.6. For v 2 = vu2 + vd2 ,
the quadratic gauge-boson terms give m2W = g22 v 2 /4, m2Z = (g22 + gY2 )v 2 /4 and one zero
photon eigenvalue.

Derivation chain. The U (2) × U (1) blocks and their bifundamental matter assignments
come from the finite history representation and the selected wrapped-cycle vacuum. The
two Higgs doublets are the intersection modes that survive this selection. Their stabiliser
                                                P
leaves U (1)em unbroken, and direct expansion of r |Dµ Hr |2 gives the charged and neutral
mass matrices. The intersection selection rules determine which Yukawa entries may be
nonzero; they do not determine all their magnitudes.

Yukawa potential and the Fermi interaction. For sources at rest the massive gauge
bosons produce a short-range force. The quadratic gauge-boson terms above give the W ±
and Z propagators, which for time-independent sources are 1/(k2 + m2W ) and 1/(k2 + m2Z ).
Exchange of a W or Z between two static sources therefore produces the Yukawa potential
                                                g 2 e−mr
                                    V (r) = c            ,                            (9.37)
                                                4π r



                                          – 85 –
                                                             √
where m = mW or mZ , g is the corresponding coupling (g2 / 2 for the charged current,
q
 g22 + gY2 times the neutral-current charge for the Z), and c is fixed by the charges and
spins of the two sources. The range 1/mW ≈ 2.5 × 10−18 m makes the force short-ranged;
this is the difference from electromagnetism, whose mediator is the massless eigenvalue.
For momentum transfer small compared with mW the propagator reduces to 1/m2W , and
W exchange becomes the contact interaction

                                      GF                    GF   g22    1
                               LF = − √ J µ† Jµ ,           √ =     2 = 2,                              (9.38)
                                       2                     2  8mW    2v

with Jµ = ψ̄γµ (1−γ5 )ψ ′ [41]. This is Fermi’s theory of the weak interaction. The derivation
uses only mW > 0 from the stabiliser calculation; g2 and v, and hence GF , come from
matching.

External results. The perturbative running and the fixed-input stability bounds use
published Standard-Model beta functions and matching formulae. The proposed non-
perturbative control uses Kotecký–Preiss, cluster-expansion and constructive renormalisa-
tion methods as guides; no cited theorem directly supplies the required four-dimensional
non-Abelian gauge–Higgs estimate.

Origin and determination of the coeﬀicients. The symmetry-breaking tensor struc-
ture and the mass ratios at fixed (g2 , gY , v) are derived. The values of g2 , gY , v, the
Higgs quadratic parameters and the Yukawa matrices remain matching or vacuum data.
The quoted top-mass, tan β and stability bounds are numerical fixed-input results, not
parameter-free predictions.

Open remainder. The mixed gauge–Higgs polymer norm, radial large-field regulator,
boundary sewing, all 53 renormalisation steps and pull-back to the unrestricted source
remain open. Thus the finite model has the displayed electroweak action and passes its
stated finite tests, while the interacting continuum mass-gap claim remains Hypothesis 2.

Theorem 10.1 (Structure of the strong sector). 56 The block U (3) of Theorem 6.4 contains
SU (3)c ; the three colour labels are the basis of the three-dimensional standard block of S4
 56
   Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem 10.1 (Structure of the strong sector): TorusModel.su3_one_loop_six_
flavours; see Appendix F.




                                                        – 86 –
(the cusp differences), on which U (3) acts. With the matter content of Theorem 6.13
(six quark flavours), the one-loop coeﬀicient is b3 = −7, so SU (3)c is asymptotically free.
Moreover:

 (a) if the gauge-fixed induced lattice action satisfies the Reisz locality, smooth periodic-
     vertex, propagator lower-bound, continuum-scaling, no-extra-zero and subspace power-
     counting assumptions stated below, its renormalised Feynman integrals have the stan-
     dard continuum limit order by order [49] (§D.1.14); these assumptions have not been
     proved for the full induced closed-walk action;

 (b) the induced action obeys the large-field lower bound of Theorem 6.10;

 (c) the explicitly selected finite/comparison measures are reflection positive under the
     hypotheses of Theorem 4.5; this is not a reflection-positivity theorem for the physical
     unrestricted Poisson source.

Proof. For SU (3) with nf Dirac quark flavours in the fundamental representation, b3 =
−11 + 23 nf . The colour-singlet Higgs doublets do not contribute. With nf = 6, b3 =
−11 + 4 = −7 < 0. Part (b) is Theorem 6.10; part (c) applies only to the finite/comparison
measures covered by Theorem 4.5, while the physical Poisson source remains Hypothesis 4.
For part (a), fix a gauge and include the corresponding ghost sector. Reisz’s theorem
applies to each subtracted graph only when: the lattice vertices are translation invari-
ant, local or uniformly rapidly decaying, periodic and suﬀiciently smooth in Brillouin-zone
momenta; every propagator has the prescribed continuum scaling, a cutoff-uniform lower
bound away from its physical zero set and no extra zeros/doublers; and the Reisz degree
is negative for the graph and every Zimmermann subspace after the stated counterterm
subtractions. Under these assumptions the graphwise continuum limit follows. The finite
closed-walk expansion proves neither the required uniform locality/smoothness bounds nor
the propagator and subspace conditions for the full induced action. Hence part (a) is a
conditional implication, not a completed universality proof.

Proposition 10.2 (The gap is the general Yang–Mills problem). 57 At the cutoff Λ ≈
6.8 × 1017 GeV the strong coupling is αs ≈ 1/45, so the lattice theory starts at weak
coupling, β = 6/g 2 ≈ 21. A strong-coupling start is excluded: it would put the confinement
scale at the cutoff, contradicting QCD. Hence the existence of a mass gap in the strong
sector is the general four-dimensional SU (3) problem, Hypothesis 3. The model provides
no shortcut.
Derivation: Appendix D.4.3.

Proof. At the cutoff, g 2 = 4παs = 4π/45, and the Wilson convention for SU (3) is β = 6/g 2 .
Therefore
                                         270
                                     β=       = 21.49,                               (10.1)
                                          4π
which is the weak-coupling side of the lattice theory. If instead the bare theory began at
strong coupling, dimensional transmutation would generate a correlation length of order one
 57
      Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 10.2: TorusModel.su3_cutoff_wilson_beta_exact; see Appendix F.




                                                – 87 –
in cutoff units and hence a confinement scale of order Λ, contrary to the observed hierarchy.
After quarks are integrated out, the unresolved infrared statement is thus existence of
a positive mass gap for four-dimensional pure SU (3) gauge theory. The model-specific
induced action must additionally be connected to the Wilson universality class; that source-
to-Wilson bridge is stated separately and is not assumed by this reduction.

     Below the scale at which the quarks decouple, the effective theory is pure SU (3) gauge
theory. Its initial action is a positive closed-walk action with an explicit large-field bound
(Theorem 6.10), and this is what the model supplies to the Yang–Mills problem. It is not
literally the Wilson action: applying the Wilson results reviewed in Section 17 requires the
separate source-to-Wilson universality bridge, which is open. The earlier deductions of the
model do not depend on resolving this problem.
Remark 10.3 (Strong CP). In 60 of the 76 catalogue vacua a Green–Schwarz axion sur-
vives and couples to GG̃. In the absence of competing explicit breaking, the QCD potential
relaxes its dynamical angle towards a CP-conserving minimum. At the fixed-reference vac-
uum V1 the axion has E/N = 2/3; in the matching class selected by the gravity–gauge
relation (row V11 of the catalogue) it has E/N = 4/3 (§16, §16.5). The relaxation mecha-
nism is the Peccei–Quinn mechanism [50], provided the QCD-induced axion potential dom-
inates any additional explicit breaking. The count of surviving axions and their anomaly
ratios are internal catalogue outputs, not consequences of that external reference.

Axion coupling and cosmological selection. The charge ratio E/N is calculated from
the winding-dependent anomaly sums; it is distinct from selecting a vacuum. The relation
fa = M∗ is a declared identification. The QCD relation ma fa ≃ 5.70 µeV × 1012 GeV then
converts a conditional scale interval to a mass interval, rather than fixing one axion mass.
In the observationally restricted full-rank overlap catalogue, E/N is 4/3 or 2/3, or the
QCD axion is absent. The no-axion class requires a small initial strong-CP angle and does
not solve that problem. Domain-wall and abundance restrictions can exclude axion-bearing
classes even when gauge matching passes; their assumptions and numerical consequences
are stated in §16.5.

10.1   Sector Lagrangian and derivation
Final sector action.     The low-energy strong-sector expression used by the model is

when the selected vacuum contains the QCD axion. If it does not, the last term is absent
and the ordinary strong-CP angle must be specified separately. The coupling gs occurs in
Dµ and GA µν .

Derivation chain. The three-dimensional block of the ordered-history representation
gives the U (3) factor. Removing its heavy central Abelian direction leaves SU (3)c . The
selected chiral module supplies six quark flavours, and the matter determinant yields a




                                           – 88 –
positive gauge-invariant closed-walk action. For this field content the one-loop coeﬀicient
is b3 = −11 + 2nf /3 = −7, establishing perturbative asymptotic freedom. The Green–
Schwarz anomaly data determine whether an axion is present and, when present, its ratio
E/N .

The force between quarks at short distances. For a static quark and antiquark in
the colour-singlet state, one-gluon exchange gives V (r) = −CF αs /r with CF = 4/3, the
quadratic Casimir of the fundamental representation of SU (3)c . The one-loop running
with b3 = −7 evaluates the coupling at the scale 1/r:

                               4 αs (1/r)                          2π
                   V (r) = −              ,    αs (1/r) =                   ,         (10.3)
                               3     r                      7 ln 1/(rΛQCD )

valid for rΛQCD  1 [41]. At short distances the force therefore has the Coulomb form,
with a strength that decreases logarithmically as r → 0; this is the static form of asymptotic
freedom. The derivation uses only the colour group, the six quark flavours and perturbation
theory, so it depends neither on the mass gap nor on any of the four hypotheses. Its
continuum interpretation is subject to the Reisz assumptions of Theorem 10.1(a), and
ΛQCD follows from the matched value of αs .

The force between quarks at long distances. At distances of order Λ−1            QCD and be-
yond, perturbation theory no longer applies. The static potential is defined by V (r) =
− limT →∞ T −1 loghW (r × T )i for a rectangular Wilson loop. If Wilson loops obey an area
law, hW (C)i ≤ c e−σ Area(C) with σ > 0, then −T −1 loghW (r × T )i ≥ σr − T −1 log c, and
letting T → ∞ gives V (r) ≥ σr: the force between the quarks does not fall off, and they
are confined. An area law is a statement separate from the mass gap, and Hypothesis 3
does not imply it; with a Higgs field in the fundamental representation, for example, a gap
occurs together with a perimeter law [51]. For SU (2) Wilson lattice theory an area law
is proved at strong coupling, β ≤ 0.3925, in the follow-up paper; there the static poten-
tial rises at least linearly, a rigorous instance of confinement in lattice gauge theory. For
every compact gauge group, SU (3) included, an area law at suﬀiciently strong coupling is
a classical theorem [51]. The physical SU (3) coupling β ≃ 21 lies at weak coupling, far
outside the range these results cover. For the Wilson action at that coupling, Theorem 17.1
derives the area law, and with it V (r) ≥ σr, from the vortex condition of §17.5, which a
block-spin flow through the intermediate couplings establishes; the mass gap is open in the
same range of couplings. The value of σ is not determined, and applying the result to the
induced closed-walk action of the model requires the source-to-Wilson bridge.

External results. The perturbative continuum comparison invokes Reisz power count-
ing after locality and the remaining lattice hypotheses are imposed. The one-loop beta
coeﬀicient and dimensional-transmutation interpretation are standard Yang–Mills results.
Strong-CP relaxation uses the Peccei–Quinn mechanism and the QCD axion mass relation
as external physical inputs. The one-gluon-exchange potential is a standard perturbative
result [41], and the strong-coupling area law is the theorem of Osterwalder and Seiler [51].




                                              – 89 –
Origin and determination of the coeﬀicients. The colour group, quark represen-
tation content, anomaly ratios and b3 = −7 are finite algebraic outputs of the selected
model. The value of gs is obtained by matching. The identification fa = M∗ and the axion
mass derived from it are model choices combined with QCD input. The non-perturbative
coeﬀicients of the model-specific induced closed-walk action have not been proved to lie in
the Wilson universality class.

Open remainder. The source-to-Wilson bridge, the continuum limit and the positive
four-dimensional SU (3) mass gap are not derived here; they remain Hypothesis 3 and the
subject of Section 17. Confinement is derived for the Wilson action only under the vortex
condition (Theorem 17.1), and the value of the string tension is not derived. Accordingly
this chapter derives the gauge structure, the perturbative running and the short-distance
force between quarks; confinement follows under the vortex condition, and complete non-
perturbative QCD is not derived.

Part II
Consistency conditions and representative
models
11    Unitarity of the physical model

Unitarity of a Euclidean theory is reflection positivity of its Schwinger functions. The
lattice model is reflection positive (Theorem 4.5), and so are the declared finite source
of the canonical finite model and the explicit comparison sources treated below. For the
unrestricted hard Poisson/Rips measure, reflection positivity at the level of the measure is
false:
• a fixed sample is not reflection invariant (Remark 4.12);

• a simple distance cutoff combined with a pair-difference action has an explicit coun-
  terexample: the tested finite kernel has a negative quadratic direction;

• the position-based gravitational kernel and the Cayley–Menger embeddability kernel are
  not positive definite; two exact tests construct the corresponding negative directions.
What remains is Hypothesis 4: reflection positivity of the averaged Schwinger functions. A
reflection-positive two-point function has a Källén–Lehmann (Stieltjes) representation with
a positive spectral measure [52, 53]. We test this with exact non-negative least squares
[54].

Figure 22: Reflection positivity is established for several finite or explicitly factorised measures. A
typical Poisson sample has no reflection symmetry, so the physical question concerns the averaged
measure and remains a separate hypothesis.


Numerical result 11.1 (Tests of averaged reflection positivity). 1. Quenched average.
  The averaged two-point functions are consistent with a positive spectral measure below
  the cutoff (p ≲ 3.5 in node units). They violate it at the cutoff scale p ≈ 2π/rc . The
  gauge field behaves in the same way.

2. Correct object. The physical measure is the joint measure weighted by the matter
   partition function Z[Π], not the quenched average. Weighting by bosonic determinants
   alone collapses the node density.

3. Global constraint. With the node number fixed only globally, the model-content weight
   det+1.5 does not equilibrate. The mean degree grows from 81 to 290 (normal ≈ 25): the
   measure collapses into clusters. A matter-weighted measure is well defined only with a
   local count = volume constraint.




                                                   – 91 –
4. Local constraint, one node per cell. The measure equilibrates and is isotropic. The
   control run (weight exponent 0) violates Stieltjes representability already for p2 ≤ 10
   in two seeds (χ2 = 384, 391 for p2 ≤ 33 with 13 degrees of freedom). Matter weighting
   reduces the violation by about 40%. One seed is consistent for p2 ≤ 16 (χ2 = 16.2), but
   this is not reproduced: two of three seeds violate it for p2 ≤ 16 (χ2 = 30.1, 31.6).

Proposition 11.2 (Reflection positivity under the stated finite assumptions). Let µ0 be a
reflection-positive base measure for a reflection Θ, and let Fa be observables supported in
the positive half-space. If the interaction has the factorised form

when it admits a positive factorisation C+− = LL⊤ .

Proof. Expand the exponential in non-negative powers and use reflection positivity of µ0
on each monomial. For the Gaussian formulation, write the cross term with an auxiliary
real field of covariance LL⊤ ; conditioning on that field leaves a product of a positive-half
and a reflected negative-half factor. Integrating the auxiliary field preserves positivity.

Theorem 11.5 (Infinite-volume RP for the explicit Wilson–Higgs representative). For the
reflection-compatible hypercubic SU (2) × U (1) Wilson–Higgs measure with non-negative
Wilson and hopping couplings and Higgs quartic λ > 0, every finite periodic volume is
reflection positive. Moreover, there exists an infinite-volume locally weak subsequential
Gibbs limit, and every such limit obtained from these reflection-compatible volumes is
reflection positive.

Proof. Finite-volume reflection positivity is Theorem 9.2. For tightness, write rx = |Hx |
and use integration by parts in the four real Higgs components. On the degree-eight
periodic lattice,
                              4λ Erx4 ≤ 4 + (2|µ2 | + 8κ)Erx2 .                    (11.10)

uniformly in the volume. Gauge variables are compact, so local marginals are tight. A diag-
onal subsequence converges locally. The OS inequality for a bounded continuous cylinder
function passes to that limit.

Scope and remaining interface. Proposition 11.2 gives the abstract factorisation crite-
rion, and Theorem 11.3 proves reflection positivity for every non-negative Källén–Lehmann
matter/rotor profile in finite volume and in every existing cylindrical Gibbs limit. Together
with the preceding results, this covers the declared finite source, the finite Gaussian/Haar
comparison source, its fixed-cutoff Wilson–Higgs subsequential states and the marked-
Poisson rotor branch. It does not cover the unrestricted hard Poisson/Rips source or an
arbitrary cutoff gauge complex: the former has an explicit negative crossing direction, while
the oscillator construction supplies a Gram memory kernel but not the four-dimensional
operator −∆ or a reflection-compatible gauge cellulation. Hypothesis 4 therefore retains
the literal-source pull-back and continuum cutoff; the singular-pair no-go is proved in §12.3.

Finite blocking and the physical source. For a reflection-positive mea-
sure µ, a reflection-equivariant, positive-half-space-preserving block map B gives
hΘF F iB∗ µ = hΘ(F ◦ B)(F ◦ B)iµ ≥ 0. The local radial map Hx 7→ |Hx | has the same
preservation property. These statements and Lemma 9.17 concern the declared finite
bosonic measure. Conjugation symmetry, radial integrability and finite RG retention do
not identify that measure with the unrestricted Poisson source or establish continuum
tightness.

     The table distinguishes three logical roles: published theorems are external inputs,
comparison-model results reach the physical source only through an explicit transfer, and
declared data are inputs rather than predictions. The application conditions for Reisz,
Prokhorov–Kolmogorov, Osterwalder–Schrader, the percolation estimates and Lovelock are
collected in Appendix D.1 and its sector-specific subsections. Hypotheses 1 and 4 remain
the photon and reflection-positivity transfers; β = 1 and the vacuum data are declared
inputs.

12.1   Established results and unresolved interfaces
The conditional source-to-continuum theorem below is the only statement that combines
the eight premises. They comprise a common source measure and state–field map, an
exact same-measure block map with Ward control, and the sector-specific transfers in §2.3;
the finite calculations verify components but do not supply the missing microscopic data.
Table 6 records each premise once, while the observational outputs and their inputs are
treated separately in §16.5.

12.2   Yang–Mills dependencies and related bottlenecks
The open non-perturbative Yang–Mills problem is described by its mathematical content,
and is separated from other obstructions that use similar proof techniques.

                                             – 97 –
Results that carry the Yang–Mills dependency. The physical strong-sector mass
gap is conditional on the source–Wilson bridge together with a valid non-perturbative gap
theorem in the target universality class. The Wilson weak-coupling chain additionally
needs the one-loop input and the intermediate-window estimate. Glueball masses, a finite
pure-glue correlation length, a non-perturbative definition of ΛQCD , and a hadron spectrum
therefore cannot be presented as unconditional outputs of the torus source. An area law
or confinement statement requires the optional area-law input as well; a mass gap alone
does not imply an area law. These consequences are not used to derive the spacetime,
gravity, gauge-algebra, matter, electromagnetic, or electroweak structural results above
the strong-sector node.

Related but distinct multiscale bottlenecks. The Wilson-photon transfer, elec-
troweak polymer norm and physical reflection-positivity problem use related multiscale
or factorisation tools, but none is a consequence of a Yang–Mills mass-gap theorem. Con-
versely, the finite gauge algebra, beta-function coeﬀicient, anomaly cancellation, partial
electroweak breaking and comparison-model Maxwell results do not use the open Yang–
Mills bridge. Table 5 is therefore a boundary of the strong-sector claim, not an additional
axiom or a summary of the other three hypotheses.

12.3   A conditional source-to-continuum theorem
The next theorem asks a deliberately narrower question: what follows if one microscopic
source satisfies all eight bridge conditions at once? It proves that implication. It does not
assert that the present torus source satisfies the premises.

Then a subsequence of (µn ) converges weakly on E to a Borel probability measure µ. Its
Schwinger functions satisfy E0–E4 and hence admit Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction.
The retained resolvents converge normally on compact subsets of D, the limiting Ward iden-
tity holds on the cylinder domain, and the limiting two-, three- and four-point distributions
have the tensor structures specified in condition 8.
Proof. Tightness in condition 2 is a statement about Radon measures themselves on the
common Polish space E and about compact sets in the displayed local Sobolev topology.
Prokhorov’s theorem [55] (§D.1.15) therefore gives a weakly convergent subsequence µnj ⇒
µ. Finite-dimensional tightness alone would not give this conclusion. No Kolmogorov-
extension argument is used here: the µn already live on E. If they were specified only
by finite-dimensional laws, one would first need projective consistency on standard Borel
coordinate spaces and then a separate support/regularity proof placing the extension in E.
The uniform (2 + δ) bounds imply uniform integrability of polynomial cylinder observables
by Hölder’s inequality, so their moments converge after the usual bounded truncation.
     For a bounded continuous positive-time cylinder function F , reflection positivity gives
                                Z

Continuity of θ and weak convergence pass this inequality to µ. Polynomial cylinders
follow by the uniform-integrability approximation. The corrected growth/regularity bound,

semigroup. Reflection positivity is an assumption verified before reconstruction, not a
conclusion supplied by that external theorem.

so no comparison measure is inserted along the subsequence. For the retained spectral
block, condition 5 and the Weierstrass test give normal operator-norm convergence of

                           Wn Jn,0 − Jn,0 W0 =         Jn,k+1 Rk Jk,0 .               (12.4)
                                                 k=0

The summable norm bound makes the right-hand side Cauchy on the common cylinder do-
main. Combining its limit with the vanishing finite-scale residual yields the limiting Ward
identity. Finally condition 8 is precisely distributional convergence of the stated correla-
tion functions; nonzero residues prevent the retained two-point sectors from disappearing
and identify the listed tensor structures. The theorem proves this implication only. It
does not assert that the unrestricted torus source satisfies any condition not independently
verified.

   The eight source-to-continuum conditions are collected in Table 6. The table sum-
marises the dependencies and is not an additional axiom.

Internal Ward boundary. For the declared finite internal-U (1) rotor completion, both
zx = ϕ̇x − ax and fxb = ax − ab − ṙxb are invariant under ϕx 7→ ϕx + αx , ax 7→ ax + α̇x ,
rxb 7→ rxb + αx − αb , before perturbative expansion or bath integration. Differentiating
the invariant action gives the Ward identity and its Hessian null. If the fine block A is
invertible, Schur elimination preserves the null through (C − B ∗ A−1 B)nc = 0. This is only
an internal phase/source identity; it does not supply four coframe generators, refoliation,
or the nonlinear Einstein Ward algebra.

Exact same-measure RG definition. Once the microscopic source specification sup-
plies a source measure and a measurable block map πa→ba , the exact push-forward con-
struction defines the RG map by
                                               Z
   Φa→ba µa = (πa→ba )∗ µa ,  Sba (Y ) = − log    e−Sa (X) νa (dX | Y ) + const, (12.5)
                                                       πX=Y

with exact composition Φba→ca ◦Φa→ba = Φa→ca . This completes the definition of the same-
measure RG condition, but not the identification of the implemented finite rotor/block map
with this push-forward or its sector preservation.

Source non-identifiability and no-go boundaries. The relation skeleton does not
determine Ug , the state–field map, the measure, interaction coeﬀicients or a source-identical
RG block. An attractive independent-Poisson pair branch with w(r) ≥ Ar−2 has divergent
                  Rε        2
collision integral 0 r3 eA/r dr and therefore cannot simultaneously be finite and supply the
                                                           P
desired RP kernel. Adding any local invariant quartic λ x Ix4 preserves the tested finite




                                              – 101 –
symmetry and positivity criteria while changing higher vertices, so those criteria do not
select the nonlinear Einstein/YM couplings. The internal U (1) Ward identity of the finite
rotor completion is stated above.

Reflection-positivity measure-class boundary. The hopping cross-kernel has a
positive character expansion and can be reflection positive, but the associated Lapla-
cian/annealed branch fails the finite-normalisation test in the same independent-Poisson
class. Fixed-count or naive hard-core repairs change the point measure and therefore do
not preserve the original source. This is a scope-restricted no-go, not a no-go for every
possible torus measure.

Theorem 12.2 (Composite non-identifiability of the unrestricted raw data). Let Sraw
consist only of the torus relations and their displayed amplitudes, phases, bath labels and
symbols ρ, τ, g, Ug . Suppose it contains no probability law on states, reflection, blocking
map, state-dependent coframe covariance, link measure or chiral module. Then no function
of Sraw alone uniquely determines the sign of reflection positivity, the dynamical metric
rank, the non-Abelian gauge algebra or the chiral matter content. In particular,

                        Sraw 6⇒ (µ∗ , Θ, ΦRG , Wyx , K[X], Fchiral , H).              (12.6)

12.4   Scope of the finite completion
The model includes the minimal finite package of Proposition E.1 in its definition. A signed
two-scale D4 compensator removes the cubic leakage of the raw constraint symbol and
produces a rank-five Fierz–Pauli branch at low momentum, whereas the direct temporal
reflection-positivity route and the unrestricted cutoff-scale branch fail. The surviving object
is a spatial/auxiliary Schur constraint tensored with a separately specified positive temporal
kernel.
      The declared finite source rule consists of a finite inverse-symmetric path semigroup
and a seven-point reflection-positive clock. Their product gives 49 one-step history–clock
marks; multiplication of the history components generates the separate 24-state S4 fibre




                                              – 103 –
described in Appendix E, §E.2. The clock factor is declared completion data rather than
an image derived from the D4 roots. The unrestricted positive GL+       2 (Q) soft tail has a
divergent unipotent subfamily, so its pullback cannot be identified with this finite law
without a new quotient, regulator, or source-declared summability domain. The finite
rule also leaves a critical zero mode; local nonzero modes are summable, while global
scale, modular, Maxwell-holonomy and coframe-moduli modes require explicit quotient or
normalization data.
     Several finite constructions are proved under their stated algebraic or finite-model as-
sumptions: site-dependent U (3) × U (2) commutant links and a plaquette trace, a Maxwell
symbol with an exact Ward null and three transverse poles, and an M3 × M2 bimodule
with anomaly cancellation and nonzero Higgs/Yukawa intertwiners. A coercive finite ac-
tion gives a volume-normalized vertex bound and a summable history remainder, while a
Gaussian state–metric completion gives a rank-ten metric response. None of these finite
constructions is source-native: the literal source still contains no state-dependent G(X),
quartic vertex data, fermionic/chiral selector, site-link measure, or plaquette coupling. In
particular, quadratic pole data do not identify higher vertices, and the finite gauge and
matter modules do not prove their production by the microscopic source.
     These constructions check history, reflection positivity, metric, Maxwell, gauge and
matter sectors within the declared finite package. They do not identify that package with
the unrestricted torus source: the missing metric, higher-vertex, chiral-selector and link-
measure data are the non-identifiability results proved above, not parameters to be fitted.

13     The canonical finite model and its computational representatives

13.1    Components of the canonical finite model
At the finite level, the model of this paper is the canonical finite model of §3.6, which
includes the dynamical metric of Axiom M. It consists of four parts:

• Axioms I, D and M;

• the adopted level–3 Hecke relation domain: determinant-one correspondences give the
  A4 cusp action and determinant-two correspondences extend it to S4 ;

• the declared finite D4 source of Definition 3.7;

• the declared bounded-geometry completion package Cfin of Appendix E, §E.1 and Propo-
  sition E.1: a finite selector and measure, reflection, blocking family, metric/current rule
  and chiral module.

The Hecke domain is the arithmetic relation domain of the canonical finite model; it does
not identify the S4 history action with the separately declared D4 colour/source structure.
Declaring Cfin makes the finite model definite, but does not derive that package from the
unrestricted torus data. The more ambitious literal Poisson–torus source interpretation
must still satisfy the four requirements of Theorem 3.12—a summable relation domain, a
reversible measure, ordered-history transport and uniform tail control—as well as the eight




                                          – 104 –
continuum conditions of Theorem 12.1. Every theorem of Part I is therefore stated with
the smallest set of assumptions it needs:

• the algebraic main line (§4–§10) uses Axioms I, D and the finite D4 source, while
  measure-dependent finite statements also use the relevant part of Cfin ;

• the dynamical gravity of §5.3 uses Axiom M as well;

• statements about the unrestricted source or its continuum limit need the four source-
  completion requirements and the eight continuum conditions just named.

Dropping Axiom M gives the minimal model of §2, in which the metric is a fixed background
and is not dynamical.

13.2     Representative models
The models below isolate source, metric, renormalisation and phenomenological questions
without replacing the canonical finite model. Figure 24 shows their logical relation to the
common core.

13.3   Admissible filters
A finite completion is admissible if it passes the seven admissibility conditions in Table 8.
The conditions are tests, not axioms: each one is a checkable physical or mathematical
property of a finite candidate. Which conditions a given claim needs is part of its statement.
They are distinct from the eight source-to-continuum conditions of Theorem 12.1: the
admissibility conditions test a single finite candidate, whereas the source-to-continuum
conditions concern a family of finite measures built from the source and its continuum
limit.

                    Table 8: Admissibility conditions A for finite completions.

  Admissibility condition     Content

  Inverse closure             the relation set is closed under inverses and the weight is
                              inverse-even (requirement (ii))
  Normalisation               the partition function is finite and positive; the relation sum
                              converges (requirement (i))
  Reflection positivity       the transfer is reflection positive, i.e. the crossing kernel factorises
                              as C+− = LL⊤ (Proposition 11.2)
  Coercivity and moments      uniform lower bound of the quadratic form; uniform (2 + δ)
                              moments
  Coarse-history retention    right S4 -equivariance of the coarse-grained history
                              (Proposition 6.2); summable Feshbach complement (requirement
                              (iv))
  Coframe and gauge           the metric (coframe) sector has rank 10; the gauge links carry the
  retention                   commutant blocks M3 ⊕ M2
  Ward, pole, anomaly and     projected Ward identity with summable remainder; massless
  Yukawa consistency          photon pole; anomaly-free chiral module with the required
                              Yukawa invariants


     The admissible-completion family uses all seven admissibility conditions together with
preservation of the canonical finite completion. The factorised control model and projected
coupled model add their respective metric couplings. The finite certification lattice is
a single finite candidate that passes all seven admissibility conditions at its lattice size.
The source-measure completion proposal addresses inverse closure and normalisation at
the source level; it is not a finite candidate. In parallel, the observationally constrained
specialisation fixes otherwise free moduli from data and is governed by the preregistration
rule of §13.7. Its observational restriction is not an admissibility condition and supplies no
missing source-to-continuum implication.




                                              – 109 –
13.4   What transfers from the representatives
Proposition 13.1 (Common physical block). 60 Let P = diag(1, 1, 0, 0) be the projection
onto the physical sector of the finite Gaussian realisation used for the infrared comparison,
and put Q = 1 − P . Assume that the fixed-background Hessian Hfixed is positive definite
and that P is a reducing projection for it, so
                                                             −1
                        P Hfixed Q = QHfixed P = 0,       P Hfixed P = P.                   (13.1)

For the factorised control model and the projected coupled model, let the eliminated metric
block be positive definite and let
                                  −1 ⊤
                        ΣQ = C Q HH CQ  0,            P ΣQ = ΣQ P = 0.                     (13.2)

Then the physical block of the inverse quadratic form is the same in the minimal model,
the factorised control model, and the projected coupled model:
                               −1           −1          −1
                            P Hfixed P = P Hfact P = P Hproj P = P.                         (13.3)

Consequently their physical pole, residue and quadratic observables agree in the finite real-
isation.

Proof. The direct-sum form of the factorised control model gives Hfact = Hfixed ⊕ HH , so
eliminating the spectator block leaves Hfixed unchanged. For the projected coupled model,
eliminating the positive metric block gives the Schur complement Hproj  red = H
                                                                                 fixed + ΣQ .
The hypotheses P Hfixed Q = 0 and P ΣQ = 0 make both matrices block diagonal with
respect to ran P ⊕ ran Q. Inverting these block diagonal matrices shows that their P -
                                                −1
blocks are identical, and the normalisation P Hfixed P = P gives the displayed identity. The
weaker condition P ΣQ P = 0 alone would not suﬀice: if P Hfixed Q 6= 0, the inverse P -block
contains the corresponding Schur term and may change. This is why the reducing-subspace
condition is part of the proposition.

     This is a finite statement. It does not prove that the infrared limits of the representa-
tives coincide, but it shows that the representatives do not alter the physical sector they
were built to preserve once the stated reducing-subspace condition is checked.

13.5   Three checks of the representatives
Proposition 13.2 (Hecke cosets and the D4 moves). 61

 (a) There is no bijection between the 24 D4 moves and the determinant-2 Hecke cosets:
     there are σ1 (2) = 3 of the latter.
 60
     Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 13.1 (Common physical block): TorusModel.common_ir_physical_
block_diagonal; see Appendix F.
  61
     Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 13.2 (Hecke cosets and the D4 moves): TorusModel.hecke_
projective_line_counts; see Appendix F.




                                             – 110 –
 (b) GL2 (Z) acts on the determinant-2 cosets through GL2 (F2 ) ∼ = S3 . Since V4 is the only
     normal subgroup of order 4 in S4 , the history group S4 maps onto S3 in only one way
     up to relabelling. Through this map, only the irreducible representations of S4 that are
     trivial on V4 can be realised: the trivial, sign and two-dimensional representations.
     The colour blocks 3 and 3′ cannot be realised.

 (c) On the four determinant-3 cosets (= P1 (F3 ), the four cusps) GL2 (Z) acts through
     P GL2 (F3 ) ∼
                 = S4 , whereas SL2 (Z) acts only through P SL2 (F3 ) ∼
                                                                      = A4 . On the four-
                                                    ∼
     cusp permutation module one has C[P (F3 )] = 1 ⊕ 3, for both A4 and S4 , so its
                                             1

     commuting unitary group is U (1) × U (1). The block-preserving group U (1) × U (3)
     is a different object. The larger U (1)3 × U (3) is the commutant of the regular A4
     representation, not of the four-cusp module.

The determinant-2 Hecke correspondence has three cosets and is not a replacement for the 24
D4 moves. Nevertheless, determinant 2 is a nonsquare modulo 3, so adjoining determinant-
2 correspondences to the determinant-one level-3 action enlarges P SL2 (F3 ) ∼   = A4 to
            ∼
P GL2 (F3 ) = S4 within the orientation-preserving Hecke domain. Thus Hecke supplies the
finite S4 monodromy, while the D4 root source remains a separately declared colour/source
structure.

Proof. (a) An index-2 sublattice of Z2 is the preimage of a line in F22 , and there are three
lines. (b) GL2 (Z) permutes the lines through reduction modulo 2, and the image is the full
GL2 (F2 ) of order 6. The quotient S4 /V4 ∼ = S3 , and Aut(S3 ) = Inn(S3 ), so the surjection
is unique up to relabelling. An irreducible representation of S4 factors through S3 if
and only if V4 acts trivially, which excludes the two three-dimensional ones. (c) Index-3
sublattices correspond to the four lines in F23 . Reduction modulo 3 gives images P SL2 (F3 )
and P GL2 (F3 ) of orders 12 and 24. The permutation module on the four cusps is 1 ⊕ 3, so
its commutant is C ⊕ C and its unitary group is U (1) × U (1): Schur’s lemma allows only a
scalar on each of the two inequivalent multiplicity-one summands. A determinant-2 Hecke
matrix has determinant 2 ≡ −1 (mod 3), a nonsquare, and therefore supplies an element
in the other component of P GL2 (F3 ); together with P SL2 (F3 ) it generates the full S4 .

Proposition 13.3 (Gravitational normalisation in the projected coupled model). 62 In
the canonical finite completion, Newton’s constant is induced by the node fields: G ∝
 −2        Q −1
 ε (R/ε)6 i χi       , so d ln G/d ln χnode = −1. This is the origin of the G dipole. In the
projected coupled model, the strength of the metric sector is set by κ.

• If κ is the induced coeﬀicient, as a function of the node shape, then the dipole ratio and
  the entropy ratio R = 1.05 are those of the canonical finite completion.

• If κ is a constant fixed by matching G to its observed value, then d ln G/d ln χnode = 0,
  and the G dipole disappears.
  62
   Lean 4 counterpart of Proposition 13.3 (Gravitational normalisation in the projected coupled model):
TorusModel.induced_newton_log_sensitivity; see Appendix F.




                                               – 111 –
Proof. The G dipole is ∆G/G = (d ln G/d ln χ)∆ ln χ. With the induced coeﬀicient this
derivative is −1. With a constant κ that sets G, it is 0. The entropy ratio compares the
entanglement coeﬀicient with the coeﬀicient of the Einstein term. That coeﬀicient is the
same if, and only if, κ equals the induced coeﬀicient.

     The projected coupled model can therefore be used for gravity calculations without
loss only with κ equal to the induced coeﬀicient. A constant κ changes a prediction of the
canonical finite completion.

Numerical result 13.4 (Shape sensitivity of the V1 reference point). For the eight copies
of vacuum V1 (internal shapes χ = (2.5, 5, 3.2) up to the exchange of the first two tori) the
derivatives ki = d ln α/d ln χi are −0.593, −0.349 and −0.961. Here α−1 = α2−1 +αY−1 at the
matching scale, with αx−1 ∝ Vx . The dipole ratio (∆G/G)/(∆α/α) = −1/k is accordingly
1.69, 2.86 or 1.04, depending on which internal torus is the node torus. The range 1.4–3.3
quoted earlier refers to the bridge vacua with natural matter content.
     Varying one shape over | ln(χ/χV 1 )| ≤ 2 with uniform weight in ln χ (the hyperbolic
measure along the shape line), the fraction of the line with |k| < 0.1 is 0.04, 0.12 and 0
for the three tori. k does not vanish at the self-dual point χ = 1, because the couplings
of a vacuum with fixed winding numbers are not invariant under SL2 (Z). Positions in the
data space are therefore marked tori. A state measure on the modular surface, as in the
source-measure completion proposal, has to be lifted to the cover on which the winding
data are defined.

13.6   The torus-to-continuum problem
The torus-to-continuum problem asks for one family of finite measures, built from the

                                             – 113 –
13.7   Observationally constrained specialisation
The observationally constrained specialisation is the observational branch obtained by con-
tinuing the canonical finite model and imposing a sequence of restrictions fixed in advance.
It retains that model’s axioms, field content, vacuum family and gravity–gauge bridge,
while progressively constraining otherwise undetermined moduli by a fixed set of observa-
tions. In this respect it is a computational representative of the canonical model, just as the
finite certification lattice is a representative obtained by fixing an explicit finite selector,
measure and action. The two representatives answer different questions: the finite lattice
tests structural conditions, while this observational branch asks which outputs remain af-
ter the stated restrictions. Neither introduces a new axiom, replaces the canonical model,
or claims that the microscopic source selects the chosen point. Its additional inputs are
the measured gauge couplings, a declared running prescription, a Higgs-stability bound-
ary condition and, for masses, a declared Yukawa amplitude rule. Solving for a point in
the admissible structures does not establish the completion requirements of Theorem 3.12.
Before every fit, the number of free parameters, the observable list, the objective, the pass
threshold and the parameter domain are fixed in advance, and no parameter or observable
is added after inspecting the result. For these declared leading-order rules the analysis is
complete: every resulting output, and every sector in which the rules fail, is given below.
The selected point itself is fixed by the data rather than derived from the source.

Summary of the outputs. The retained outputs are: exact matching of the three gauge
couplings; selection of one matching class by the gravity–gauge relation with the model’s
own field content; a conditional top-mass bound; the charged-lepton mass ratios under
the declared amplitude rule; and axion dark matter for a uniform initial angle declared
as initial data. Quark masses and quark mixing, lepton mixing, neutrino masses, sin2 θW ,
the electroweak scale v and Λ are not reproduced; they lie outside the model’s present
predictive scope (§14). The numerical values are collected in §16.5.

Discrete catalogue and continuous matching. The enumeration of §6.5 contains 76
copies with 16 distinct outputs; the observationally restricted calculation groups them into
ten matching classes. These counts refer to different equivalence criteria. For a class define
the wrapped volumes and the hypercharge volume by

Higgs stability on the matching surface. Let ΛI (mt , αs ) be the first zero of the
running Standard-Model quartic. The non-negative D-term boundary requires M∗ ≤ ΛI ,
in addition to the quartic inequalities of §9.6. At mt = 172.57 GeV and αs = 0.1180 one
finds ΛI ≃ 4.6 × 1010 GeV; the difference from the matching input αs = 0.1179 is an input
sensitivity. Exact matching finds acceptable points in all ten classes, so stability does not
uniquely select the fixed reference point V1. In the large-shape limit the volume relation
becomes

whose solution with the input couplings is µ∞ ≃ 5.906 × 108 GeV. Two top-mass options
follow.

(a) If every finite-shape solution satisfies M∗ ≥ µ∞ (numerically supported, not proved),
    then ΛI (mt , αs ) ≥ M∗ ≥ µ∞ gives mt ≲ 174.31 GeV at αs = 0.1180.

(b) If the strict D-term boundary is replaced by a threshold of heavy partner states at
    M∗ , larger M∗ is allowed and one finds mt ≲ 173.0–173.9 GeV with tan β = 1.2–1.5.
    This threshold is plausible but not proved.

This is an additional equation on the matching surface. With the model’s own field content

E/N = 4/3). The observed Planck mass is therefore a held-out class-selection criterion
rather than a fitted parameter or a parameter-free prediction. A joint fit that also imposes
the common-radius area relation κ2 /κ3 = χ2 /χ3 has not been carried out.

Yukawa rule and leptons. Nonzero first-generation masses require full-rank Yukawa
matrices if only leading-order couplings are retained. Under the overlap rule of §6.6 four
matching classes have full rank; a strict per-torus closure rule instead removes the charged-
lepton and neutrino couplings, so the full-rank selection is conditional on the overlap rule.
For the charged leptons the declared periodised Gaussian rule is
                                  X
                                       e−πκ2 (k/3+y+ℓ) +2πi(k/3+y+ℓ)w ,
                                                      2
      Yij = c(i−j) mod 3 ,   ck =                                          κ2 > 0.     (13.9)
                                     ℓ∈Z




                                                  – 115 –
The sum converges absolutely. The discrete Fourier matrix diagonalises this circulant
                                                  P
matrix: with ω = e2πi/3 its eigenvalues are λn = 2k=0 ck ω nk , and since the Fourier matrix
is unitary the singular values are |λn |. Ordered as s1 ≤ s2 ≤ s3 , they give the mass ratios
s1 /s3 and s2 /s3 . The fit reproduces the observed charged-lepton ratios with κ2 ≃ 0.5724
and w ≃ 0.2429 at the declared separation; uniqueness in the separation y is not established.
     The Dirac-neutrino Yukawa lives on the same torus with the same circulant structure.
Every circulant matrix is diagonalised by the same Fourier matrix, so the charged-lepton
and neutrino left rotations coincide up to a permutation and phases, and the leading-order
lepton mixing matrix is a permutation (angles 0◦ or 90◦ ). Full rank alone leaves the lightest
mass free. The model therefore makes no prediction for neutrino masses or lepton mixing;
the effective Majorana mass vanishes for the declared Dirac sector.

Quarks. Two leading-order routes to the quark sector were tested against criteria fixed
in advance. A Wilson-line-consistent geometric overlap rule does not reproduce the quark
masses and mixing. A shared heavy-partner correction with seven parameters and one
remaining degree of freedom gives χ2 = 373 against a pass threshold fixed in advance, and
the geometrically fixed variant leaves the light Yukawas unchanged at tree level. Quark
masses and the CKM matrix are therefore not reproduced and lie outside the model’s
present predictive scope.
     A topology-only scan of the declared vacuum family with |n| ≤ 5 finds 4236 admissible
configurations and 126 intersection patterns. They reduce to two leading-order quark
structures. In the first, the relevant families share the third torus and the matrices can
have full rank, but the consistent overlap rules retain the up/down mirror and cancel the
required CP structure. In the second, the left- and right-handed families occupy different
tori and the leading blocks have rank one, leaving the first generation massless. Thus
the quark layer is exhausted within the declared vacuum templates. Going further would
require either changing those templates or adding subleading Yukawa structure with more
adjustable parameters than the observables used here; neither extension is part of the
present reconstruction.

What selects the observationally restricted representative? The tested modular
self-dual points, low-complexity complex-multiplication points and diagonal cusp limits do
not select the measured couplings subject to the other cuts; these tests exclude the tested
candidates, not every source-selection mechanism. A measure calculation with density
                  Q
proportional to i χ−2 i on χi ≥ 1 gives a broad distribution of sin θW when only αem and
                                                                   2

αs are inputs; for the V1 winding data its 5th, 50th and 95th percentiles are 0.117, 0.309
and 0.488, and the observed value lies near the 24th percentile. This is a consistency test;
it neither fixes the moduli nor converts the matching inputs into predictions.

In the minimal model the background metric is static and the node scale is fixed, so there
is no redshift. Statements that involve cosmological time evolution therefore use standard
expansion as an external input in that model. In the canonical finite model they follow
from Theorem 5.13. These statements are the sky-dipole distance, the early-universe list,
the axion abundance and the comparison with the DESI data. The statements valid in the
minimal model as they stand are static ones: the α–curvature relation, the ratio G/α, Λ
as an integration constant, and the identity of the dark-matter candidate.

14.2   Concrete model branches and what the axioms do not fix
The names in this section denote concrete models or representatives, not hidden proof
stages. The source-measure completion proposal is the literal unrestricted Poisson–torus
reading, and the canonical finite model is used for the main finite theorems. The finite cer-
tification lattice and the observationally constrained specialisation are two specialisations
of that canonical model. The first fixes finite data in order to test stated algebraic and
analytic criteria; the second retains the canonical content and imposes restrictions fixed in




                                            – 118 –
advance on free moduli to determine the resulting outputs and failed sectors. The latter
is defined in §13.7. Neither representative is a new axiom, a replacement for the canonical
model, or a claim of unique microscopic selection.

The observationally constrained specialisation keeps exact gauge matching, the selection
of the matching class of catalogue row V11 by the gravity–gauge relation with Nb = 5 and
nf = 4, the conditional top-mass bound and the charged-lepton ratios. The quark/CKM
and lepton-mixing tests fail under the declared leading-order rules; quark masses, CKM,
PMNS, neutrino masses, sin2 θW , v, and Λ therefore lie outside the model’s present predic-
tive scope rather than being reported as predictions. The analysis exhausts the declared
leading-order vacuum templates and alternatives fixed in advance; it does not show that
the microscopic source selects the fitted point. Before any new observational restriction
is tested, the number of free parameters, observables, objective, threshold, and allowed
domain must be fixed in advance; adding a parameter or observable after seeing the fit is
not permitted.
     A proved boundary is a quantity for which there exist several solutions satisfying every
condition of the axioms and the source, with different values. Such a value cannot be
computed from the model. Fixing it would need a new principle.

The independent verification of the thresholds at the Kaluza–Klein scale (∼ 1017 GeV)
is experimentally inaccessible. The amplitude of primordial perturbations is not derived
either. Since not every mechanism has been excluded, it is an open external input rather
than a proved boundary.

14.3   Cosmology and the dark sector
• Dark energy. In the canonical finite model, the remaining candidate is a cosmological
  constant (the unimodular integration constant), with no new field; its value is initial
  data. The “everpresent-Λ” mechanism is excluded independently of its amplitude within
  the tested model (Appendix C). Rolling node-shape moduli are likewise incompatible
  with the stated bounds: node shape sets α (coupling k = d ln α/d ln χ = 0.25–0.96),
  so w = −0.9 would give |α̇/α| ∼ 10−11 yr−1 , which is 106 –107 times the atomic-clock
  bounds (∼ 10−18 yr−1 ). Thus the dynamical-metric candidate has w = −1. The minimal
  model has no cosmological evolution of its own and therefore does not predict w.

• Dark matter. Axion-bearing classes supply a QCD axion. In the selected matching
  class it is all of the dark matter if a uniform initial misalignment angle θi ≈ 1.6 ×
  10−3 is declared as initial data; the angle is not derived. Domain-wall restrictions and
  the absent-axion alternative are stated in §16.5. No additional component has been




                                              – 120 –
   constructed. For the stated thermal history the rotor mode and the Dirac νR make a
   negligible thermal contribution.

• Curvature–coupling covariance. Node torus shape is position, so the couplings vary
                                                    p
  with position. Per Hubble radius, ∆α/α ≈ k |Ωk |, and (∆G/G)/(∆α/α) = 1.4–
  3.3 with the same sign. The bound |∆α/α| < 10−6 implies |Ωk | < 3 × 10−12 . In the
  canonical finite model the spatial metric belongs to the family of invariant metrics on the
  data space (uniqueness of invariant metrics). The relation then holds with corrections
  O(σ/H) + O(δ), where the shear is σ/H ≈ 0.69 Ωk from the Bianchi III integration and
  δ ∼ 10−5 . These corrections are far below observational precision.
