14.4   Grand-unified gauge bosons and proton decay
A grand-unified gauge boson is a vector field associated with a generator of a gauge algebra
larger than the Standard Model algebra. For example, in SU (5) the X, Y bosons and
their conjugates transform as (3, 2)−5/6 and the conjugate representation, and convert
quarks into leptons. Their exchange can generate a dimension-six operator of schematic
form qqql/MX  2 , mediating proton decay such as p → e+ π 0 . Once the breaking scale and

coupling are specified, their mass is of order MX ∼ gGUT vGUT ; a numerical prediction
therefore requires the unified group, breaking pattern and vacuum expectation value.
     In the present model, M∗ is only the scale at which the three low-energy gauge couplings
meet. The gauge algebra is not enlarged and no X, Y generators or fields are introduced.




                                           – 121 –
Their mass is therefore not a missing number to be predicted: these fields are absent from
the model’s field content. The statement “no proton decay” is a structural prediction con-
ditional on this absence and on not adding independent baryon-number-violating effective
operators. Coupling matching alone does not imply the existence of grand-unified gauge
bosons.

14.5   Relation to existing theories
Here “grand unification” means a grand-unified gauge theory (GUT), in which the low-
energy gauge couplings meet at a higher scale. It refers to coupling unification, not to a
claim that gravity and all interactions are one classical “unified field.” At M∗ the couplings
meet, but the model does not introduce grand-unified gauge bosons.

                                                   – 123 –
Cosmology without inflation.              Three internal seeds of primordial perturbations are
excluded.

• Thermal winding gas (of Brandenberger–Vafa type [56]). The windings live on the
  internal torus, so the heat capacity scales as CV ∝ R3 , and the spectral tilt is ns −1 = −1.

• Matter bounce at node density [57–60]. A contracting phase with w ≈ −0.0029 would
  give ns = 0.9649. The exact mode functions give r = 24cs , so r < 0.036 needs cs <
  1.5 × 10−3 . The amplitude As ≈ (HB /MP )2 /(12π 2 cs ) = 2.1 × 10−9 needs HB ≈ 1012 –
  5 × 1013 GeV. But the integer-volume bounce occurs at node density, HB ∼ 7 × 1016 –
  2 × 1017 GeV, which gives As six to seven orders of magnitude too large.

• Node-scale thermal seed with area-law heat capacity. It gives P ≥ (`P /ε)4 = 9.5 × 10−6 ,
  at least 5 × 103 times too large.

A single-clock seed needs the energy scale V 1/4 ≈ 6 × 1015 –1.4 × 1016 GeV, which matches
neither the cutoff Λ = 6.8 × 1017 GeV nor the fixed-reference matching scale M∗ = 1.6 ×
1010 GeV. In the selected matching class under the heavy-threshold option, M∗ ≈ 5.8 ×
1016 GeV lies within a factor of about four of this range, but no mechanism links the seed
to M∗ . The primordial amplitude is therefore an external input.

16     Numerical benchmarks and conditional phenomenology

16.1    The fixed-reference vacuum catalogue
The finite vacuum catalogue has 76 copies and 16 distinct outputs, listed below at fixed
reference moduli. It is an exhaustive finite enumeration; its stability column refers only
to those fixed points, not to all continuous matching solutions. The grouping into ten
matching classes and the class selection are explained in §13.7. Throughout, V1 denotes
the fixed-reference benchmark vacuum, while V11 denotes the catalogue row whose matching
class is selected by the gravity–gauge relation. M∗ is the scale where the three couplings
meet; it is interpreted as the internal Kaluza–Klein scale, with no grand-unified gauge
bosons. α∗ is the common coupling there (1/α∗ is a wrapped-cycle volume), and fa =
M∗ . The residual two-loop matching error δth = 0.7–1.9% is the required Kaluza–Klein
threshold correction (∆(1/α) ≈ 0.3–0.8, a natural size). The last column gives the Higgs-
stability verdict (Proposition 9.19).

       76 consistent vacua   couplings, ranks, axion                           conditional tests
                                                       interval arithmetic

16.2     How the tabulated values are calculated
The entries combine discrete vacuum data with measured running inputs and chosen mod-
uli. Their calculation proceeds in the following order, with the assumptions of each opera-
tion stated separately:
1. Finite catalogue. For each allowed winding vector (nix , mix ), including the tilted-torus
   parity condition, we compute the intersection numbers
                                              Y
                                              3
                                      Ixy =         (nix miy − mix niy ),                              (16.1)
                                              i=1

   the tadpole and anomaly sums, the Green–Schwarz matrix, and the allowed Yukawa
   couplings. These are integer calculations. We reject exotics, retain three-generation
   spectra and quotient only by the stated relabellings. This gives 76 consistent copies and
   16 distinct rows; it is an exhaustive finite enumeration, not a Monte Carlo sample.

2. Couplings and scale. For each surviving row we evaluate the wrapped volumes Vx (χ)
   and use
                                  αx−1 = κA (ΛR)3 Vx (χ)                       (16.2)
   at the matching scale. Two-loop running and the stated threshold interval are then
   applied to the three measured gauge couplings. Their common crossing gives M∗ and
   α∗ . Thus M∗ and 1/α∗ are outputs of the catalogue plus the declared running/matching
   inputs; they are not fitted independently.

3. Ranks and axion entries. The ranks in the table are the ranks of the finite
   Yukawa/intersection matrices over the rationals. The anomaly sums of the same
   winding data give E/N . We set fa = M∗ and use the chiral axion relation
                                                            1012 GeV
                                      ma = 5.70 µeV                                                    (16.3)
                                                               fa




                                               – 125 –
  using the external QCD coeﬀicient 5.70(6)(4) of [61], where the two parenthesised uncer-
  tainties arise from quark-mass inputs and higher-order corrections. For V1 the central
  value is ma ≃ 358 µeV; it inherits these uncertainties and those of fa = M∗ .

4. Neutrinos. No neutrino number is tabulated. The charged-lepton and neutrino Yukawa
   matrices are circulant and share their Fourier eigenvectors, so the leading-order mixing
   is a permutation; neutrino masses, ordering and mixing lie outside the model’s present
   predictive scope (§13.7). External cosmological and oscillation constraints are compar-
   ison data, not model outputs [62–64].

5. Higgs stability and tan β. Starting from mh , MW , mt and αs , three-loop Standard-
   Model beta functions, four-loop QCD and NNLO matching are integrated with outward-
   rounded intervals. The boundary condition

                               λSM (M∗ ) = 18 (g22 + gY2 ) cos2 2β                   (16.4)

  is imposed, and bisection in mt finds the largest interval for which λSM (µ) ≥ 0 at every
  step. The reported 172.89 GeV bound is the endpoint of that interval; the quoted αs
  sensitivity and threshold shift are obtained by varying those inputs.

6. Geometry and entropy.          Differentiating the wrapped-volume functions gives
   k = d ln α/d ln χ and the ratio (∆G/G)/(∆α/α). The curvature relation is then
               p
   ∆α/α ≃ k |Ωk |, and integrating it along the last-scattering line of sight gives the
   factor 3.1 in Dα . Finally, the finite-volume entropy fits give κS at sizes 24–40; with
   c2 = 1/(16πG) we form R = κS /(4πc2 ). The spread between the two masses and lattice
   directions is the quoted uncertainty, so this last entry is a consistency check rather
   than an exact theorem.

The exact integer enumeration and interval-certified running are distinct from finite
floating-point checks. Whenever an input such as oscillation data, αs , or Ωk enters, the
resulting number is labelled as input-dependent rather than as a new parameter-free
prediction.
     In the leading-order rank, the suppression exponents of the masses that vanish there,
∆Af = ln(mLO /msub ), are ∆Au = 6.4, ∆Ad = 3.0, ∆Aµ = 2.8, and ∆Ae = 5.3, all of
natural size. The neutrino suppression is not tabulated, since the neutrino sector makes
no prediction.

16.4    Conditional cosmological history of the V1 benchmark
This paragraph assumes the specified contracting history suppresses wall formation; it
is not established throughout the observationally constrained specialisation. The wall-
forming alternative is tested in §16.5. Without inflation, the initial misalignment angle of
the axion could be random across domains. In the canonical finite model, however, the
contracting phase before the integer-volume bounce keeps the angle uniform on large scales
by causal contact. A light spectator field acquires the spectral index nϕ − 1 = 3 − 2|ν|
with ν = 3(1 − w)/(2(1 + 3w)). For w > 0.068 near the bounce the angle is uniform on
the QCD horizon scale (δθ ≈ 10−12 for w = 1/3). Near a bounce, radiation or stiff matter
dominates dust, and dust contraction is in any case excluded by the amplitude bound
above (spectator-uniformity calculation). Hence no domain walls form, and no alignment
of an instanton bias is needed. Two further facts support this:

• every O(1)-type cycle of V1 and V2 carries an even axion charge, and each vacuum has
  exactly one light closed-string axion (axion-charge and light-mode catalogue);

• some copies of V1 have NDW = 1.

The dark-matter abundance is then set by a single initial angle θi (initial data). A typical
angle gives about 4% of the dark matter, and all of it requires π − θi ≈ 1.4 × 10−3 .
Naturalness is not a consistency condition, so this does not affect consistency, but it is
a tuning of initial data. In the selected matching class the corresponding angle is θi ≈
1.6 × 10−3 (§16.5).

Not counted as predictions. The following quantities are not counted:

• the number of generations (vacuum data, Remark 6.14);

• sin2 θW and α3 /α2 , which were used as filters for the vacuum catalogue;

• neutrino masses and mixing, which the model does not predict;

• the ratio (MP /Λ)2 ≈ 322, which is circular.

16.5    Conditional outputs of the observationally constrained specialisation
Section 13.7 gives the equations and fitting procedure for the observationally constrained
specialisation of the canonical finite model. This specialisation has continuous moduli in
addition to discrete winding data, so its outputs are conditional estimates rather than
the fixed reference values listed above. The table lists the outputs for the matching class

Axion mass and abundance. With fa = M∗ ≈ 5.8 × 1016 GeV, the QCD relation of
[61] gives ma ≈ 5.70 µeV × 1012 /(5.8 × 1016 ) ≈ 0.1 neV. At such a large decay constant
a typical misalignment angle overcloses the universe; the observed dark-matter density
requires θi ≈ 1.6 × 10−3 . The model does not derive this angle; it is declared as initial data,
and the statement “the axion is all of the dark matter” is conditional on it. Experimental




                                              – 129 –
sensitivity at this mass depends also on E/N and the photon coupling; no exclusion or
projected sensitivity is inferred here.
    Under option (a), the matching scale lies in [5.906 × 108 , 4.6 × 1010 ] GeV, conditional
on the unproved lower bound, and the same relation gives

                        5.70 × 1012             5.70 × 1012
                                    µeV ≲ m a ≲             µeV,                      (16.5)
                         4.6 × 1010             5.906 × 108

or approximately 124–9651 µeV; the endpoints reverse because ma ∝ fa−1 . For randomly
sampled initial angles in a no-inflation history this branch gives a dark-matter fraction of
only 0.001–0.22. This interval is an alternative branch, not one of the reported outputs,
and it is not an experimentally allowed band.

Domain walls and strong CP. The selected class has NDW = 3. If the initial angle is
uniform over the observable universe, as the dark-matter statement assumes, no string–wall
network forms there. If instead a wall network forms in an axion-bearing full-rank class,
the bias needed to remove the walls must be compared with the maximum bias compatible
with |θ̄| < 10−10 . With wall tension 8ma fa2 and the quality ceiling 3.25 × 10−15 GeV4 , the
required bias exceeds the ceiling over the sampled range of option (a), except for a marginal
strip near the lower scale where the mismatch is a factor of about 1.2, within the order-one
uncertainty of the wall constants. That wall-forming branch is therefore disfavoured; it
does not solve the wall problem.
     The fixed-reference discussion of §16 assumes a contracting history that keeps the angle
uniform. Transferring that assumption to the selected class requires the same background,
thermal and initial-data conditions; wall-forming and wall-suppressing histories cannot
both be used without stating which is assumed. The class with no QCD axion avoids axion
walls but requires a small initial θQCD and supplies no axion dark matter. No additional
dark-matter component is identified.

Neutrinos and vacuum selection. The charged-lepton and neutrino Yukawa matrices
share their circulant eigenvectors, so the leading-order lepton mixing is a permutation;
neutrino masses, ordering and mixing lie outside the model’s present predictive scope.
Neither the rank count nor Higgs stability alone gives a unique neutrino spectrum or a
unique vacuum; the class selection above comes from the gravity–gauge relation.




                                          – 130 –
Part IV
Yang–Mills interface and the open
mass-gap problem
17     Yang–Mills interface and the open mass-gap problem

In Yang–Mills theory the gauge field is the only dynamical field. Its classical equations are
scale invariant and describe massless waves, and perturbation theory around the free field
likewise contains only massless gluons. The strong interaction is nevertheless short-ranged:
in lattice simulations the lightest states of pure gauge theory, the glueballs, are massive,
and the coupling, which decreases at short distances by asymptotic freedom, grows at
long distances where perturbation theory no longer applies. The mass-gap problem asks
for a proof of this behaviour: that four-dimensional quantum Yang–Mills theory with a
compact simple gauge group exists as a quantum field theory and that its Hamiltonian has
no spectrum in an interval (0, ∆) above the vacuum, for some ∆ > 0. In Euclidean terms,
connected correlations of gauge-invariant local observables then decay exponentially with
distance at a rate set by ∆. On a lattice the measure is well defined from the outset, and
the question becomes whether the gap stays positive uniformly in the volume and survives
the continuum limit.

17.1    The problem and its place in the model
Hypothesis 3 asks for a volume-uniform spectral gap of four-dimensional lattice SU (3)
Yang–Mills theory at the physical bare coupling. The coupling calibration in Proposi-
tion 10.2 puts that point near β ≃ 21, so a complete argument would have to pass from
weak coupling through the crossover to the confining regime. No rigorous method for that
crossover is known.
    The estimates for this problem and their proofs are developed in the follow-up paper,
where most of the certified constants are obtained for SU (2); the mass-gap statement for
the physical gauge group SU (3) remains open.

                                                    Follow-up estimates    Physical SU (3) gap
       Torus calibration   Wilson lattice measure
                                                          selection,           source-to-
          β ≃ 21            weak-coupling start
                                                       flow, windows         Wilson bridge

Figure 30: The torus construction fixes a bare-coupling benchmark, while the Wilson measure
defines the mass-gap problem to be solved. The follow-up analysis supplies conditional bounds, but
it does not close the physical SU (3) gap.


17.2    How the analysis connects to the torus model
The follow-up paper studies the standard Wilson lattice problem, whose foundational lat-
tice formulation is due to Wilson [1]. Mondal’s recent geometric proposal [10] obtains a
mass gap under its own conditions and is used here for comparison. The torus construc-
tion does not directly produce a Wilson action at the quark-decoupling scale; it produces




                                              – 131 –
a positive closed-walk action with a large-field bound (Theorem 6.10). A source-to-Wilson
universality theorem, including transfer and renormalisation-group control, is therefore a
separate condition and is not assumed here.
    The dependency analysis in Section 12.2 identifies the logical role of the required
ingredients:
  • the physical SU (3) continuum gap is a hypothesis/open problem;

  • the source-to-Wilson bridge is an open bridge, not a consequence of the torus action’s
    positivity;

  • the Wilson weak-coupling chain uses one qualitative one-loop input and an unresolved
    intermediate-window covariance bound;

  • an area law is not needed for the mass gap and does not imply it; it is the subject of
    §17.5;

  • photon, electroweak, reflection-positivity, and microscopic-source bottlenecks are log-
    ically distinct.
Thus the SU (2) Wilson theorems in the follow-up paper are not conclusions for the
microscopic-source SU (3) theory. Even after a source-to-Wilson bridge, the source-to-
continuum conditions and the source-selection decision must still be supplied before a
continuum statement can be transferred to the torus model.

17.3   Wilson setting
Let Λ be a finite periodic lattice in Z4 with link set E. Each link carries Ue ∈ G = SU (N )
and the Wilson action is
                        X                                             Y
                S(U ) =       1 − N1 Re tr Up ,     µβ (dU ) ∝ e−βS(U )   dUe .       (17.1)
                        p                                            e

Here Up is the ordered product around a plaquette and dUe is Haar measure. For SU (2)
we identify G with the unit sphere S 3 ⊂ R4 (unit quaternions), with its bi-invariant round
metric; the Ricci curvature is Ric = 2. Conditional on all other links, one link has the von
Mises–Fisher law µa (du) ∝ e⟨a,u⟩ du with a = β times the sum of the six staples, hence
|a| ≤ 6β.
     The follow-up paper starts from this setting and contains the strong-coupling proofs,
the weak-coupling chain, the conditional continuum theorem, and the intermediate-window
obstruction. It is a technical continuation of this overview, not an additional upstream
input to the main torus derivation.

17.4   What the torus model supplies and what it needs
The Wilson-measure framework is that of [1, 51] (§D.1.4); the conditional uniqueness and
covariance tools are [70, 71] (§D.1.13).
    The strong sector of the torus model enters the Yang–Mills problem at the quark-
decoupling scale. The model supplies four finite ingredients, and needs one statement from
the Wilson analysis.




                                          – 132 –
Initial action. Integrating out the bosonic matter of the nodes gives a positive closed-
                    P
walk action Γ[U ] = ω cω (N − Re trUω ) with cω ≥ 0. This action obeys the explicit large-
                    P
field bound Γ[U ] ≥ t 2ct (N − Re trUt ) (Theorem 6.10). Its plaquette part has positive
character coeﬀicients, so it is a candidate input for Wilson-type reflection-positivity and
large-field arguments. Positivity alone does not identify this closed-walk action with the
Wilson action or prove the required continuum transfer. Its bare coupling corresponds to
β = 6/g 2 ≃ 21.5 (Proposition 10.2).

Finite colour carrier. The colour group is the determinant-one subgroup of the M3 (C)
block of the history commutant (Theorem 6.4). On a finite reflected cell complex one
can declare SU (3) link variables in this block, with Haar measure and the Wilson weight
       P                                                    P
exp[ β3 □ Re trW□ ]. Covariant matter couples through ρ e kΨy − We Ψx k2 . Gauge co-
variance of this finite realisation is exact. The finite construction also gives an 18-link
retained checkerboard carrier with an exactly defined induced retained action, obtained by
integrating out the block variables. These are declared finite objects, not consequences of
the literal source.

What transfers from the Wilson analysis. The results of the follow-up paper divide
into three classes for the SU (3) sector of the model. No SU (2) theorem or numerical con-
stant in that paper is an SU (3) mass-gap result; the SU (3) constants must be recomputed.

• Valid for every SU (N ): uniqueness and exponential decay for β < 12 log 19 17 (total-
  variation Dobrushin with 18 influence neighbours). Also valid is the conditional con-
  tinuum implication: if a transfer operator satisfies qa = e−aM +o(a) with compatible
  reflection positivity and matrix-element convergence, the continuum generator has gap
  at least M .

• Valid for compact groups with positive Ricci curvature, with changed constants: the
  heat-flow log-Sobolev criterion, the exact identities for the renormalised potential, the
  pinned Poincaré Riccati bound and the structure of the pinned joint measure. For
  SU (3), Ric = 14 Killing> 0.

• Specific to SU (2): the Haar-point constant 8/(9π), the certified gap for β < 0.24, the
  area-law certificate for β ≤ 0.3925 and the weak-coupling threshold βw , whose value in
  the Wilson-lattice follow-up model is given in that paper. These must be recomputed
  for SU (3).

Confinement. The long-distance force between quarks needs an area law, which the
mass gap does not supply. Section 17.5 derives it from a condition on twisted partition
functions that any method may establish, and shows that a block-spin flow through the
window of the gap programme (§17.6) establishes it.

What is missing. Three statements are needed before a Wilson result can be read as
a statement about the model. The first is a source-to-Wilson universality bridge: the
closed-walk action at the decoupling scale must lie in the Wilson universality class non-
perturbatively. Perturbatively this holds by Reisz’s theorem. The second is the explicit




                                         – 133 –
SU (3) retained conditional kernel, with its influence coeﬀicients and gauge-invariant signed
operator norm. The present construction defines these objects but has not evaluated them;
the executable conditional generators of the Wilson analysis are SU (2). The third is the
window statement of §17.6 at the SU (3) coupling β ≃ 21.5. That point lies inside the
window, so the model needs precisely the statement that is open for the Wilson theory.

17.5   Confinement and the vortex free energy
The long-distance force between quarks (§10.1) requires an area law, which the mass gap
does not supply. This subsection proves that the area law follows from one condition on
twisted partition functions, condition (VF) below, whatever method establishes it, and
then shows that a block-spin flow through the window establishes (VF).
     Let Λ be a periodic four-dimensional box with side lengths L1 , . . . , L4 , let the weight
                              P           
be the Wilson weight exp β3 p Re trUp at β = 21.49, and let z = e2πi/3 . Fix a coordinate
plane (µ, ν). A twist stack Υ is the set of (µ, ν)-plaquettes at one fixed position of the
(µ, ν)-torus, taken for all values of the two remaining coordinates. The twisted partition
function Z[k], k = 0, 1, 2, replaces Up by z k Up for p ∈ Υ ; thus Z[0] = Z, and Z[k] does not
depend on the position of Υ [72].

Vortex condition (VF). There are constants c, ρ > 0 and an integer p0 such that, for
every coordinate plane (µ, ν), every box whose sides are divisible by 2p0 , and k = 1, 2,
                                          Z[k]

positivity and the Schwarz inequality [72]. Condition (VF) states that a centre vortex costs
no free energy once the box is large in the plane that the vortex pierces.

satisfies V (r) ≥ ρr, so the string tension is at least ρ.

A route to (VF): the block-spin flow. The rest of this subsection derives (VF) from

Block-spin maps. One block step with block factor b assigns to each coarse link `′ from

multiplication of all of them by one central element.

form in which block-spin estimates are proved (§D.1.14). Condition (RG) is the statement
that the multiscale flow crosses the window and ends in the strong-coupling regime; it is
not proved here.

                                          – 136 –
Relation to the mass-gap programme. Condition (VF) does not depend on the
method used to establish it: any proof of (VF) at the physical coupling, whether by a
block-spin flow, by functional inequalities or otherwise, gives confinement through Theo-
rem 17.1. No known argument derives (VF) from Hypothesis 3. A mass gap concerns local
correlations and an area law the non-local Wilson loop; with matter in the fundamental
representation a gap without an area law occurs [51]. A proof of the gap therefore yields
confinement when it also establishes (VF). For the block-spin route of §17.6 this requires
nothing further: such a proof supplies (RG) for the untwisted weight, its bounds have the
block-local form in which these estimates are proved, and the lemmas above give (VF).
A method built from other local estimates is in a similar position, since by Lemma 17.2
a twist is invisible in every region that does not wrap the (µ, ν)-torus; what it must add
is the exponential bound of (VF) itself. Condition (VF) fails for U (1) at weak coupling,
where Wilson loops obey a perimeter law (Theorem 8.3), so any route to (VF) must use the
non-abelian structure; the block-spin route uses it through the negative one-loop coeﬀicient
that drives the flow to strong coupling. What remains open for confinement is (VF) at
the physical coupling, which lies in the same window as the mass gap, together with the
source-to-Wilson bridge for the induced action of the model.

gap. The follow-up work is organised around one target that unifies the two routes used

The straight lines illustrate the target qa = e−aM +o(a) with the o(a) term omitted: the lattice gap

and an SU (2) estimate alone do not give the physical SU (3) result.

so far: the heat flow with pinned measures, and blocked renormalisation with conditional
block generators.




                                                  – 137 –
Common structure. In both routes, coercivity averaged over the environment is easy
against a test vector that does not depend on the environment. In the heat-flow route,
EP [Ric+2HessVt ]  2 holds exactly for every deterministic vector. In the blocked route, the
conditional block generator is coercive on regular boundaries up to a bad-set correction.
In both routes the worst-case statement fails. The test vectors that matter, however,
depend on the environment: in the heat-flow route they are the gradients of the conditional
expectations v = ∇mF . The window is therefore the covariance between the environment
and its own test vectors.

The target. Environment-decorrelation bound (E): for every β in the window there is
Ξ̄(β) < ∞ such that, uniformly in the volume and in local observables F ,

      EPt v, Covoff
                ν (s) v ≤ Ξ̄(β) EPt |v| ,
                                       2
                                                v = ∇mF ,   t ∈ [τ ∗ (β), tpos (β)],   (17.8)

where Covoffν (s) is the cross-link part of the pinned score covariance. If (E) is proved
with the stated uniformity, the follow-up estimates give a volume-uniform log-Sobolev
inequality and hence a lattice mass gap at β. Together with the weak-coupling chain and
the Osterwalder–Schrader structure of its limit, this would give the continuum gap; (E)
itself remains an open target.

Evidence and next steps. The present evidence and the planned work are as follows.

1. Saturation of the cross-link form. At the worst tested point (β = 3, t = 0.2) the cross-
   link form per pin is −3.75 ± 0.30, −4.18 ± 0.64 and −3.71 ± 0.47 for L = 4, 5, 6, flat in
   the volume. This is numerical evidence for (E), not a proof.

2. Coarse versus fine modes. On a 24 block the off-link form is positive on the 12 block-
   constant directions in every tested pin and negative on the 180 fine directions. The
   planned proof treats the fine modes by a block-local Poincaré inequality and the coarse
   modes by one renormalisation step. This converts (E) into a multiscale statement.

3. Blocked-renormalisation side. Finite marked-pullback and transverse-floor computa-
   tions pass. Still open are the identity of the blocked kernel, a uniform interacting floor
   m0 , and a certificate for the weak-to-strong crossover.

4. Rigour of the endpoints. The area law for β ≤ 0.3925 now has a self-contained proof: an
   exact slab reduction, centre symmetry and a single-site Dobrushin coeﬀicient, together
   with the Arb certificate (49,372 cells, minimum slack 9.7 × 10−9 ). It no longer cites the
   Durhuus–Fröhlich and Föllmer theorems.

5. SU (3). Recompute the strong-coupling certificate, the Haar ceiling, the heat-flow con-
   stants and the weak-coupling threshold for SU (3), and evaluate the retained 18-link
   conditional kernel of the model.

6. Source-to-Wilson bridge. Compare the closed-walk action of Theorem 6.10 with the
   Wilson action through their character expansions and the large-field bound, starting
   with the relevant blocking scale.




                                            – 138 –
Items 1–4 concern the Wilson theory alone and are the content of the follow-up paper.
Items 5 and 6 connect it to the torus model.

Relation to the photon and electroweak calculations. The direct Wilson–Villain
comparison of §8.7 seeks masslessness in an abelian weak-coupling regime. Strong-coupling
Yang–Mills gap estimates cannot supply that conclusion. The finite electroweak cancella-
tions of §9.5 similarly do not prove the physical SU (3) gap. A common source-to-continuum
construction would provide necessary shared infrastructure, but the sector-specific uniform
estimates and continuum identification would still be required.




                                         – 139 –
Part V
Conclusion and outlook
18     Conclusion and outlook

We first collect the low-energy action to which the construction leads and record how each
of its coeﬀicients is determined, then compare the construction with earlier uses of the
torus in physics, and close with what has been established and what remains open.

18.2   Differences from prior theories and scope of the contribution
The torus appears in several earlier frameworks, each time in a different role. Comparing
those roles with the one it plays here shows both what the present construction adds and
what it does not attempt.




                                            – 141 –
Noncommutative tori and spectral geometry. The noncommutative torus is an es-
tablished C ∗ -algebraic geometry whose generators need not commute; differential-geometric
data such as connections, curvature and projective modules can be defined intrinsically
[75, 76]. That framework is designed to describe geometry on a deformed algebra, so it
does not by itself specify a stochastic source, a finite move system, or an ordered-history
algebra from which spacetime dimension, gauge group and matter content are derived.
The present paper addresses that missing construction from a different starting point: or-
dinary commutative torus data are used as node labels of a Poisson source, and the finite
D4 move system and S4 history algebra are derived explicitly rather than identifying the
noncommutative torus with the spacetime algebra.

Matrix compactifications and finite torus approximations. Connes, Douglas and
Schwarz obtain noncommutative tori as compactification backgrounds of Matrix theory
[77]; Landi, Lizzi and Szabo study finite-dimensional matrix approximants and their con-
tinuum limit [78]. These constructions begin with a noncommutative coordinate algebra
and approach it through matrices, whereas the present finite object is a Poisson source with
an independently defined D4 move action and S4 history algebra. The state–field map and
source-to-continuum passage used here are therefore conditional bridges stated directly for
the source; they are not identified with an N → ∞ matrix limit or a Morita-equivalence
step.

Toroidal compactification in string and brane models. In string and brane
model building, toroidal compactifications are prescribed backgrounds on which higher-
dimensional fields, branes and fluxes are reduced to a lower-dimensional effective theory
[27]. The gauge group and chiral spectrum then depend on the chosen brane configuration,
and the construction does not by itself reduce the broad configuration space to a finite
catalogue. The present model uses the same intersection-number calculus for chiral
multiplicities (§6.5) in a different setting: the gauge blocks are fixed by the S4 history
commutant, each stack realises one such block, and the three internal two-tori are the
node torus and the two relation tori of the D4 structure rather than a higher-dimensional
compactification space. The consistent vacua consequently form an exhaustive finite
catalogue of 76 copies and 16 distinct rows (§16), although the catalogue alone does not
select a unique vacuum.

String-net condensation and graph-based emergence. Levin and Wen derive topo-
logical phases from nets of strings on a lattice [79]; Konopka, Markopoulou and Severini
construct emergent locality from a graph and its connectivity rules [80]. Those approaches
provide topological order or emergent locality, but do not in the cited constructions derive
the Standard-Model gauge group in 3 + 1 dimensions from a finite algebra that simulta-
neously fixes dimension, gauge factors and matter content. The present model supplies
that additional structure through a Poisson node graph with D4 moves and a specified
S4 history algebra whose commutant determines the gauge group and chiral matter; the
graph is consequently tied to a declared source and finite history data rather than being
an unconstrained kinematical graph.




                                          – 142 –
Connes–Marcolli Q-lattice theory. Connes and Marcolli study commensurability
classes of marked Q-lattices, their quantum statistical mechanics, modular Hecke algebras,
KMS states and arithmetic/Galois actions [2, 81]; the commensurability and Hecke vocab-
ulary used here has its mathematical origin in that programme. That number-theoretic
framework does not, in the cited construction, build a Poisson source on node labels,
derive a D4 move system from torus data, or produce a state–field map and a low-energy
gauge/gravity action. The present construction has a different input and target: Poisson
nodes labelled by tori lead conditionally to 3 + 1-dimensional spacetime with gauge fields,
while the D4 source moves remain distinct from the A3 reflection sector. The Q-lattice
KMS phase structure, Shimura-variety identification and Galois action are not imported
or asserted here.

Spectral-action gravity–Standard-Model construction. Chamseddine, Connes and
Marcolli use a product of a spin manifold with a finite noncommutative geometry and obtain
gauge, Higgs and neutrino-mixing data through the spectral action [7]. That construction
takes the smooth spin manifold and finite geometry as inputs, and its action coeﬀicients
depend on the chosen cutoff function rather than being fixed by the finite algebra alone.
The present model instead takes the ordered-history algebra generated by the level-three
move action as its primary finite object; its commutant supplies the stated finite gauge
factors, while no spectral triple or spectral-action coeﬀicient is reconstructed or imported
and the finite geometry is not identified with the torus-node source.

Ascárate’s toroidal quantum-gravity proposal. Ascárate’s 2026 proposal takes
noncommutative tori with spectral triples, discrete holonomy, causal ordering and
Markov/percolation dynamics as basic ingredients [82]. Its geometry and dynamics are
developed inside that noncommutative-torus framework; the cited proposal therefore does
not supply the finite D4 /S4 algebra or the eight-condition source-to-continuum interface
used here. The present paper uses ordinary torus data as node labels of a Poisson network
and derives the finite D4 move model and S4 history algebra from those data, so neither
Ascárate’s spectral triple nor its dynamics is constructed or imported, and no result
established in one framework is used as a proof in the other.

Toroidal causal dynamical triangulations. CDT with a fixed toroidal spatial topol-
ogy sums over Lorentzian triangulations and examines phase structure and continuum
behaviour [83, 84]. In that setting the torus is a global topology imposed on the trian-
gulated histories, and the triangulation measure does not derive the finite D4 /S4 history
algebra, gauge group or state–field map used here. The present model obtains these struc-
tures from a Poisson source labelled by torus data rather than from a sum over Lorentzian
simplicial geometries; its microscopic measure, history variables and conditional continuum
interface are therefore different.
     The prior frameworks thus use the torus as a noncommutative coordinate algebra,
a compactification background, a matrix approximation target, a string-net substrate, a
number-theoretic lattice, a spectral-action input, or a fixed spatial topology. In the present
paper the torus is the node label of a Poisson source, and from this label the finite D4 move




                                           – 143 –
system and S4 ordered-history algebra are derived independently. The state–field map and
the source-to-continuum passage are formulated as explicit conditional bridges. Conse-
quently, this construction does not import the torus algebra, spectral action, N → ∞
matrix limit, or CDT triangulation measure of any prior framework. The individual math-
ematical ingredients—commensurability, Hecke operators, history algebras, lattice gauge
theory—have antecedents in the cited literature; what is new is their combination under the
declared axioms into a single conditional chain from torus-node data to a 3 + 1-dimensional
spacetime with gravity, gauge fields and chiral matter.

What is claimed here, and what is not. Under the stated hypotheses, the contri-
bution lies in five connected pieces: the separation of the axioms from the source spec-
ifications; the finite level-three Hecke and cusp calculation, kept distinct from the D4
source; the ordered-history commutant with its matter and anomaly calculations; the non-
identifiability of the source together with the eight-condition source-to-continuum theorem;
and the explicit dependence of the Lagrangian and of the vacuum outputs on declared in-
puts. No new general theory of Q-lattices or noncommutative tori is proposed, and no
spectral-action derivation is given. The unrestricted continuum limit, the electroweak gap,
physical reflection positivity and the Yang–Mills gap remain open.

18.3   Conclusion: the significance of the model
The construction begins with a single object, the flat two-torus node. Within the declared
finite completion, Axioms I and D determine the four-dimensional signature with one time
direction, the gauge group SU (3)c × SU (2)L × U (1)Y and anomaly-free chiral matter; with
Axiom M and the stated local derivative assumptions they also give the Einstein–Hilbert
tensor form. The static limits of the sector actions give Newton’s and Coulomb’s laws, the
short-range weak force with the Fermi coupling and the asymptotically free short-distance
force between quarks. Long-distance confinement of the Wilson SU (3) theory follows from
the vortex condition of §17.5, which a renormalisation flow through the intermediate cou-
plings would establish; the mass gap is open in the same range of couplings.
     The source does not determine the couplings, Λ or the mass hierarchies. The values
in §16.5 solve an observationally constrained matching problem and depend on declared
moduli, thresholds and overlap inputs; they are not parameter-free predictions. Each step
of the construction is proved in the finite model, certified numerically, or stated as a named
hypothesis or transfer condition, so the paper establishes a checkable finite construction
together with the conditional source-to-continuum implication of Theorem 12.1. The four
physical transfers in Hypotheses 1–4, the identification of the unrestricted source and the
continuum Yang–Mills result remain open; Lean and the certificates cover only their listed
finite statements and bounds. The remaining task is to connect the unrestricted Poisson
source to the finite completion and then to the continuum. If those steps succeed, the torus-
node construction supplies a common microscopic origin for structures otherwise described
by separate theories; if they fail, the finite construction locates the failed interface.
     Among the attempts made so far to describe spacetime, gravity and the gauge forces
within one framework, the model is of interest for three features. It rests on a small axiom




                                           – 144 –
system: three axioms and one declared finite completion. It leaves a finite, exhaustively
enumerated set of consistent vacua instead of an open space of possibilities. And, once its
matching inputs are declared, it carries the same finite data through to phenomenological
outputs such as the gauge couplings, the top-mass bound and the axion coupling ratio.
These are also the points on which it is most directly compared with the frameworks
discussed in §18.2.

18.4   Outlook and next steps
The next work is organised by the interfaces that remain between the finite construction
and its physical interpretation:

1. Microscopic completion. Construct a single source-level transport, state–field map, mea-
   sure, interaction and same-measure renormalisation map, including the metric coupling,
   higher vertices, link measure, chiral selector and global zero-mode quotients. A selec-
   tion theorem must show that these data survive the spatial-move, coframe and gauge
   projections.

2. Sector transfers. Establish photon masslessness for the integer Wilson action (Hypothe-
   sis 1), the joint gauge–Higgs polymer norm and cutoff removal, and the source-to-Wilson
   comparison with its intermediate-window bound before seeking the physical SU (3) con-
   tinuation.

3. Phenomenological consistency. Test the matching-scale lower bound, certify the overlap-
   fit errors and heavy-state threshold, and impose the common torus areas, gravity nor-
   malisation, Higgs stability and cosmological history in one simultaneous solution.

4. Verification. Complete the review of the correspondence between the prose statements
   and their Lean counterparts. The incidence matrices, enumerations, interval certificates,
   sources and hashes on which these checks rest are already distributed in the archived
   release [11].

These tasks test the existing model; they do not add axioms to the canonical finite comple-
tion. Until the microscopic source, its measure and state–field map, and the sector transfers
are established, the numerical matching results remain input-dependent finite diagnostics
rather than consequences of an unrestricted continuum theory.

Part VI
Appendices
A      Per-sample homogenisation: upper bound and supporting lemmas

Scope of the external inputs. The deterministic attachment formula uses Mayer–
Vietoris [85] (§D.1.17); cochain decompositions use discrete Hodge theory [86]. The proba-
bilistic filling and homogenisation conclusions additionally require the source-specific event
comparison, localisation and weighted-operator bounds distinguished in Appendix D, espe-
cially the homogenisation conditions in Appendix D.6. Neither macroscopic-cluster unique-
ness nor scalar graph-Laplacian convergence alone verifies these transfers. In particular,
the high-density estimate below remains conditional on those checks.

Percolation comparison model. Every continuum-percolation estimate in this ap-
pendix refers to the following comparison law, not directly to arbitrary marked torus nodes.
A homogeneous Poisson process Πλ is sampled in R4 ; two points are adjacent when their
Euclidean distance is at most a deterministic radius r; equivalently the occupied Boolean
set is the union of balls of radius r/2; and the complex is the clique complex of this Gilbert
graph. The parameter λr4 is assumed supercritical. Statements concerning an inserted
point use the Poisson Palm law. The Poisson Palm law is the distribution of the process
conditioned to contain a distinguished point at the insertion location. Applying a discrete
percolation estimate also requires a declared box discretisation whose good-box field has
finite dependence and a proved connection comparison. The marked-torus source obeys
these assumptions only if its marks do not alter adjacency, its normalised metric is the
Euclidean metric used here, and the Palm, good-box and graph–Boolean identifications
are proved. These are premises of this appendix, not conclusions of the cited percolation
papers.

Lemma A.1 (Vertex addition). 63 Work over a field. If K ′ = K ∪ st(v) is obtained from
a clique complex K by adding a vertex v, then
                                                                                    
  dim H1 (K ′ ) = dim H1 (K) − rank H1 (lk v) → H1 (K) + dim ker H̃0 (lk v) → H̃0 (K) .
                                                                                    (A.1)
In particular, new H1 classes arise only at vertices whose link is disconnected.

Proof. In a clique complex the closed star st(v) is the cone over the link, and K ∩ st(v) =
lk v. Cones are contractible, so the Mayer–Vietoris sequence reads H1 (lk) → H1 (K) →
H1 (K ′ ) → H̃0 (lk) → H̃0 (K). Count dimensions.

Lemma A.2 (Locality of births). Add vertices in sweep order in the comparison model
above. New H1 classes are generated by local cycles within the reconnection radius ρx .
Assume the source-specific event inclusion {ρx > `} ⊆ Uℓc , where Uℓ is the precise unique-
crossing-cluster event in the Penrose–Pisztora box construction, with finite link components
 63
      Lean 4 counterpart of Lemma A.1 (Vertex addition): TorusModel.vertex_addition; see Appendix F.




                                               – 147 –
controlled separately. Then the absence of extended H1 (condition (T)) follows from the
supercritical continuum-percolation estimate [87] (§D.1.1).
Derivation: Appendix D.6.1.

Proof. When x is inserted, Lemma A.1 says that every new class is represented by joining
two components of lk x through the cone vertex x. Define ρx as the least radius for which
those components are connected in the previously exposed complex inside Bρx (x). Choosing
such a connecting path and the two cone edges produces a representative contained in
Bρx (x). Thus a class born at x is local whenever ρx < ∞.
      The implication from a disconnected link to failure of the Boolean unique-crossing
event is not a consequence of the cited theorem: small finite link components may coexist
with one macroscopic crossing cluster. It is therefore precisely the event inclusion assumed
in the lemma, after those finite components have been controlled. Under the supercritical
finite-range and moment hypotheses of the Penrose–Pisztora input, P (Uℓc ) ≤ Ce−cℓ . The
tail is summable over a dyadic exhaustion, so Borel–Cantelli excludes births whose diameter
is comparable with the box size. This proves condition (T) under those explicitly stated
percolation hypotheses and the event inclusion; it does not prove that the source lies in
that regime.

Lemma A.3 (Anchored dual). Let c be a local cycle anchored in a ball B of radius r that
is non-zero in H1 (K). Then there is a cocycle η ∈ H 1 (K, K \ B2r ), supported in the ball,
with hη, ci 6= 0. Hence the representability of local classes by finitely supported cocycles
(condition (L)) reduces to the event that local classes are anchored in balls.
Derivation: Appendix D.6.1.

Proof. Let U = B2r and let j : K \ U ,→ K. Since c is supported in Br ⊂ U and is nonzero
in H1 (K), its class is not in the image of a cycle supported in K \ U . Over a field, the
annihilator of im j∗ ⊂ H1 (K) is the image of the relative group H 1 (K, K \ U ) in H 1 (K).
Hence there exists a relative class [η] with h[η], [c]i 6= 0. A relative cochain vanishes on
simplices of K \ U ; extending it by zero gives a cocycle supported in U = B2r . This is the
required anchored dual. The only geometric input is that the class has an anchor ball for
which the stated non-image condition holds; condition (L) asserts this uniformly.

Lemma A.4 (Stationary Weyl decomposition). Assume (T) no extended H 1 , (L) a sta-
tionary square-summable family of finitely supported representatives of the local H 1 classes,
and (P) L2 -sublinearity of the scalar potential:
                                        X
                                   R−6      |φ(x)|2 −→ 0.                               (A.2)
                                       x∈BR

Assume also that the finite-volume Hodge projections converge strongly in the stationary
L2 cochain space. Then every stationary harmonic 1-field is the sum of a deterministic
constant field and the L2 -convergent sum of the local hole fields.
Derivation: Appendix D.6.1.




                                           – 148 –
Proof. Let u be the stationary harmonic field. In a finite box take the orthogonal cochain

                                C 1 = im d0 ⊕ im d∗1 ⊕ H1 .                          (A.3)
The assumed strong convergence of the projections passes this identity to stationary L2

sentatives for the remaining classes, so h = a ha converges in L2 . The residual v = u − h

The strong-projection and square-summability assumptions are part of this conditional
