Theorem A.5 (Upper bound). Under Lemma A.4 and the large-scale 1-chain Poincaré
inequality S′ (|a| ≤ CR|d1 a| for a ⊥ ker d1 ), the inequality of Theorem 8.15 is an equality. S′
is proved conditionally on four published percolation results [87–90] (§D.1.2). The estimate
S′ controls a 1-chain orthogonal to harmonic cycles by its coboundary with a constant
proportional to the macroscopic scale R. The proof combines the following ingredients:

• the filling-operator reduction kF k ≤ M ⇒ |a| ≤ M |∂2⊤ a|;

• lattice filling area O(d2 );

• linear area of thin loops along randomly shifted hierarchical paths;

• a Schur test of Iwaniec–Lutoborski type [91] (§D.1.3);

• local hole corrections;

• a Cauchy–Crofton flux lemma [92] (§D.1.18) with a weighted Schur test on bad clusters;

• boundary connectivity of bad clusters [90], and a 3-dependent concentration inequality.




                                             – 149 –
Derivation: Appendix D.6.2.

Proof. Fix a constant macroscopic two-form F and let aR be the cell minimiser. The
lower bound of Theorem 8.15 already gives lim inf R−4 ER (F ) ≥ qhom (F ). For the reverse
inequality, tile QR by mesoscopic cubes and paste translated stationary correctors. The
mismatch on cube boundaries is a closed one-chain. Lemma A.4 decomposes it into a
constant part, local-hole circulations and an exact remainder. Lemma A.3 cancels each
local circulation inside a ball of twice its anchor radius. The remainder is orthogonal to
ker d1 , so S′ gives a filling b with kbk ≤ CRkd1 bk. The Iwaniec–Lutoborski/Schur estimate
and the Cauchy–Crofton count bound the sum of filling energies by the volume energy plus
o(R4 ).
     The four cited percolation inputs are used at identifiable points: Penrose–Pisztora
localises births; Antal–Pisztora replaces long chemical detours by Euclidean-length paths;
Liggett–Schonmann–Stacey dominates the finite-dependent bad-box field by a subcriti-
cal product field; and the Timár boundary estimate attaches every remaining bad clus-
ter to the chosen boundary component. Their exponential tails make the total bad-
cluster and boundary cost o(R4 ) almost surely. The pasted competitor therefore has
lim sup R−4 ER (F ) ≤ qhom (F ). Combining the two inequalities gives equality. Every con-
clusion in this paragraph is conditional on the quoted percolation hypotheses and S′ .

Theorem A.6 (High density; conditional quantitative estimate). At mean degree D ≳
9 × 105 , deterministic filling (edge shortening and cone subdivision on δ < r/5-dense
samples) and an averaged cone Poincaré inequality (constant C`2 ) give (T), (L) and S′
almost surely, given the declared occupancy and finite-dependence domination constants.
Under these constants Theorem A.5 applies and the homogenisation closes at this density.
Derivation: Appendix D.6.2.

Proof. Partition space into cubes of side δ < r/5. If every cube in the required neighbour-
hood contains a point, each geometric segment can be replaced by a graph path whose
successive vertices are within the connection radius. Edge shortening replaces an arbitrary
cycle by elementary cycles, and coning each elementary cycle to a nearby occupied cube
fills it with O(`2 ) triangles. The same cone construction, averaged over possible apices,
gives the chain Poincaré estimate with constant C`2 .
      For a Poisson process the probability that a prescribed δ-cube is empty is e−λδ . A
                                                                                         4


union bound over the finite neighbourhood, followed by the declared finite-dependence
domination and Borel–Cantelli, gives the required eventual density when the displayed
numerical inequality is satisfied; solving that inequality yields the conservative mean-degree
threshold D ≳ 9 × 105 . The deterministic construction then gives (T), (L) and S′ , and
Theorem A.5 closes the homogenisation. The word “conditional” signifies that the printed
decimal threshold is valid only with the specified occupancy and domination constants; it
is not inferred from the empirical model-density runs below.

Formalisation scope. The occupancy estimate, the finite-dependence domination and the
continuum-percolation step enter this theorem as stated assumptions; no computer certifi-




                                           – 150 –
cate of them is distributed. The two algebraic ingredients of the proof are formalised in
Lean (Appendix F).

Numerical result A.7 (Model density rc = 1.5). The following are numerical results at
the model density.

• Relaxation ratios are 0.60–0.72, with ε ≈ 10 (rc = 1.8). Within a sample ε varies by 1–
  4% over six field directions, and between samples by ±20%. The corrector rms saturates
  at 0.32 for R ≥ 6 (per-sample homogenisation analysis).

• The density of local H1 classes is 0.005–0.010 per unit volume at rc = 1.5, 0 at rc = 1.8,
  and 0.07–0.09 at rc = 1.4 (local homology analysis).

• The S′ constant gives λmin R2 = 0.17–1.09, non-decreasing in R (spectral lower-bound
  analysis).

• 0.9–1.5% of the vertices have a disconnected link. All 596 such defects reconnect within
  ρx ≤ 2.66 < 2rc , in at most 5 steps (link-reconnection and decimation analysis).

• Relative homology on balls of radius 2rc detects every local class (42, 41 and 46 in three
  samples) (relative-homology analysis).

B   Verification and reproducibility

Two kinds of material support the paper outside its text: Lean 4 formalisations of the finite
algebraic statements, and stored numerical computations. Each is organised around the
arguments of the main paper that it supports. The full correspondence tables, computation
labels and stored outputs are given in Appendices F–H, which are also distributed as a
standalone Verification Supplement.

Formal verification. The algebraic core of the main argument is formalised in Lean 4.
The formal projects build in full, every listed statement is mapped to a declaration, and the
dependency, placeholder and axiom checks pass. The proofs have also been re-checked by
an independent kernel, the nanoda typechecker, which replayed all 84 modules of the main-
paper formalisation in twenty-one bounded batches without error. One step of a complete
verification remains: reading each displayed statement against its Lean counterpart to
confirm that the two express the same mathematics. Analytic and continuum clauses
that lie outside the finite formalisation are identified as such where they occur. Published
external theorems enter as named inputs; their hypotheses, and the model-specific steps
that use them, are given in the main text and in Appendix D.

Statements and declarations. A formalised result in the main text carries a footnote
naming its principal Lean declaration. The statement–declaration table of Appendices F–H
lists the exact lookup keys and separates results whose finite content is formalised com-
pletely from those for which a supporting ingredient is checked. The mathematical meaning
of each result is carried by its statement and the surrounding text.




                                          – 151 –
Repository and archive. The formalisation and the reproducibility material are kept
in the public repository https://github.com/s6ygleee/torus-node-lean4 and are
archived on Zenodo under doi:10.5281/zenodo.23863514 [11]. The archived record fixes
the version that accompanies this paper. The release contains the Lean formalisation of
the main paper, its statement–declaration tables, the provenance and certificate index,
and the build rules needed to repeat the checks; the Yang–Mills formalisation is released
with the separate follow-up paper. The formalisation and scripts are released under the
MIT licence, and the manuscript text and data under the Creative Commons Attribution
4.0 licence (CC BY 4.0).

Numerical data. The main text keeps the tables needed to state the model and its scope.
The complete catalogue of 76 vacua, the mass and dipole data, the interval certificates,
and the scripts, logs, inputs and checksums are held in the archive. Each numerical value
belongs to one of four classes—exact finite computation, interval-certified bound, numerical
diagnostic, or input-dependent estimate—and enters the argument with the standing of its
class. Since the definitions, axioms and proofs of the paper are stated independently of any
stored file, a computation in the archive can be rerun or replaced with only its classification
to be updated.

Archive identifiers. Stored computations are named by what they compute, and rows
of the vacuum catalogue are labelled V1–V16. File names and row labels only locate data
in the archive and are not part of the notation of the theory. The main text describes each
computation in words; the corresponding files are listed in Table H.1 of Appendix H.

C     Model boundaries and no-go results

The axioms and the declared finite completion leave some quantities undetermined, and
several mechanisms that might seem natural can be shown not to work. Both kinds of
statement are gathered below: the inputs and quantities that the axioms do not fix, the
no-go and non-identifiability results, and the geometric distinctions needed to read them.

C.1    Declared inputs and quantities not fixed by the axioms
• The source exponent β = 1 is part of the declared source kernel. Its coincidence with a
  pole of ζ(β)ζ(β − 1) is a remark, not a derivation.

• The mechanism for three chiral generations, charge quantisation and anomaly cancella-
  tion is derived in the finite matter construction, but the selected vacuum and generation
  count are vacuum data.

• Couplings, masses and the cosmological constant are not fixed by the minimal axioms.
  Their numerical reconstruction requires the declared finite catalogue, continuous moduli
  and matching inputs.




                                           – 152 –
C.2    No-go and non-identifiability statements
• A fully relational construction of a dynamical metric without Axiom M fails at the
  stated Weinberg–Witten obstruction; the minimal model therefore does not produce a
  dynamical metric by itself.

• The literal source-to-metric, source-to-pole/vertex, source-to-chiral-matter and source-
  to-gauge-link bridges are no-go or non-identifiability results, not missing numerical fits.
  The finite completion is consequently labelled conditional.

• Reflection positivity, Wilson-photon homogenisation, the electroweak analytic window
  and the Yang–Mills continuum bridge remain conditional on their explicitly named
  hypotheses. Comparison-model theorems do not by themselves prove the physical source
  transfer.

• The everpresent-Λ mechanism and rolling node-shape dark energy are excluded by the
  model boundary and the stated variation bounds; the remaining cosmological constant
  is an integration constant or initial datum.

C.3    The cuboctahedral distinction
A standalone 12-vertex cuboctahedral graph is not a model choice in this paper. The
cuboctahedron that appears in the main text is the A3 spatial section of the D4 root
system, used in the dispersion, S4 action and face-centred spatial calculations; it is not a
separate graph model.

D     Derivations, external inputs and formalisation conditions

The proofs in the text use two kinds of input. Model-specific implications are derived where
they are stated. Published theorems—among them Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction,
Lovelock classification, Dobrushin’s criterion, Akcoglu–Krengel ergodicity and the spectral
and percolation results listed below—are used without reproof, and each use requires the
model to supply the hypotheses of the theorem. For each external result, the first part
below records the form in which it is used and the hypotheses the model must provide;
the later parts contain the derivations of main-text results whose proofs were deferred.
Where a calculation is cited by its label, its mathematical content is the corresponding
finite matrix, sign table, interval bound or formal theorem in the accompanying material.

D.1    External theorem dictionary and application scope
The following named results are imported mathematical inputs. For each one we state
the usable form, the hypothesis checked in this paper, and the precise conclusion that
is transferred. They are not being presented as new model-specific theorems. Where a
hypothesis is supported only by a proposed numerical or Lean certificate, the conclusion
is stated conditionally on it.




                                          – 153 –
D.1.1 Penrose–Pisztora local uniqueness and large deviations [87]. The prob-
ability model to which the continuum result is applied must be fixed before the citation
is used. Here that comparison model is the stationary constant-radius Boolean–Poisson
model in R4 : centres form a homogeneous Poisson process Πλ , occupied balls have deter-
ministic radius r/2, equivalently the Gilbert graph joins x, y ∈ Πλ when |x − y| ≤ r, and
the simplicial object is its clique complex. The parameter λr4 must lie in the supercritical
regime covered by the theorem, the boxes and boundary-crossing event must be the events
used in that theorem, and insertion of a vertex must be interpreted under the Poisson
Palm law. The cited result then controls the largest/crossing cluster and its boundary
intersection with a surface-order large-deviation estimate. It is not a theorem about the
link of each inserted vertex in this paper’s complex. To deduce a reconnection tail

                                    P(ρx > `) ≤ Ce−cℓ ,                               (D.1)

one must additionally prove that the event {ρx > `} is contained in the relevant local-
uniqueness failure event (with every finite-component exception separately controlled), and
that the graph/clique event is measurable with respect to the same Boolean configuration.
That event inclusion is not established by citing Penrose–Pisztora: uniqueness of a macro-
scopic cluster does not connect every small link component. The intensity/radius regime,
Palm conditioning, graph–Boolean correspondence and topological localisation step in Ap-
pendix A are separate obligations. None is presently proved for the literal marked-torus
source, so the homology conclusion remains conditional.

D.1.2 Antal–Pisztora chemical distance, Liggett–Schonmann–Stacey domina-
tion, and Timár boundary connectivity [88–90]. Antal–Pisztora concerns super-
critical Bernoulli bond percolation on a lattice. Its chemical-distance estimate can enter
the present continuum argument only after a specified coarse-graining maps good Boolean–
Poisson boxes to an occupied finite-range lattice field and proves the required connection
comparison. Liggett–Schonmann–Stacey then applies only if that good-box field is sta-
tionary, has a stated finite dependence range, and its one-site probability exceeds the
domination threshold; the dominated product parameter must itself be in the required
supercritical or subcritical regime. Timár’s theorem is deterministic and requires the par-
ticular primal/dual graph pair and boundary notion appearing in its statement. It supplies
no failure probability. Thus the discretisation, dependence range, marginal bound, graph-
pair identification and boundary event are model-specific assumptions. The cited results
are used only to replace long detours by controlled paths, concentrate bad clusters and
attach boundary pieces; they do not determine the continuum energy density, and the
paper does not claim these hypotheses for the unrestricted source.

D.1.3 Iwaniec–Lutoborski integral estimate [91]. This reference supplies homotopy
operators and integral estimates for differential forms on suitable Euclidean domains. A
uniform bound for the weighted discrete filling operator on random cells is an additional
assertion: it needs a specified domain, admissible exponent and weight, a discretisation
comparison, and uniform geometric constants. In particular, the L1 endpoint and the




                                          – 154 –
weighted Schur estimate used in Appendix A cannot be imported without an explicit
theorem and parameter match. Those transfers remain conditions of the upper-bound
argument; the bibliography alone is not their certificate.

D.1.4 Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and Osterwalder–Seiler reflection
positivity [14, 15, 51]. For the scalar Schwinger hierarchy used here the full recon-
struction input is: (E0) tempered-distribution regularity with the corrected growth bound
of the 1975 theorem; (E1) covariance under the full Euclidean group, including the appro-
priate finite-dimensional spin representation for non-scalars; (E2) reflection positivity for
every finite positive-time test sequence; (E3) permutation symmetry, replaced by graded
symmetry and the corresponding conjugation rule for fermions; and (E4) clustering under
large spacelike translations. Normalisation S0 = 1, reality/Hermiticity and compatibility
of the time reflection with complex conjugation are included in this hierarchy. E0–E4
concern one consistent infinite Schwinger family, not separate finite-volume inequalities.
In the present paper finite scalar reflection positivity proves only the E2 component for
the declared finite/comparison measures, and annealed O(4) covariance addresses part of
E1. Uniform temperedness, the infinite-volume limit, clustering, the complete non-scalar
covariance law and fermionic graded positivity remain assumptions. Osterwalder–Seiler
supplies E2 for the Wilson lattice measure, not for the unrestricted Poisson–torus measure.
Consequently reconstruction is conditional and does not itself prove a mass gap.

D.1.5 Lovelock’s four-dimensional classification [18]. The object to be classified
must be a natural local rank-two tensor on a smooth four-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian
manifold: it is a diffeomorphism-covariant concomitant of g, ∂g and ∂ 2 g, is symmetric and
covariantly divergence free, and is linear (quasilinear in invariant language) in the second
derivatives. Equivalently for the action formulation used here, the local field equations must
contain no derivatives of the metric above second order; nonlocal form factors, torsion, extra
fields and unsuppressed higher-derivative terms lie outside the theorem. Under precisely
these assumptions the four-dimensional tensor is aGµν + bgµν , up to a topological term
that does not change the local field equation. The paper applies this classification only
after assuming a smooth local two-derivative continuum expansion and diffeomorphism
covariance. Finite graph locality does not prove those assumptions. The continuum metric,
its variational principle and suppression of all nonlocal/higher-derivative terms therefore
remain explicit premises; the Bianchi identity and matter conservation then make b an
integration constant in the unimodular equation.

D.1.6 Weinberg–Witten and Atiyah–Singer/Riemann–Roch [20, 29]. The
Weinberg–Witten theorem obstructs a massless particle of spin greater than one carrying
a Lorentz-covariant conserved stress tensor under its stated representation hypotheses.
It is used only as a no-go test for a fixed-background emergent graviton, not as a claim
that all theories of gravity are impossible. The Atiyah–Singer index theorem (in the torus
case equivalently the relevant Riemann–Roch index) identifies the chiral index with the
integral gauge flux/Chern character. We use that index to rule out chirality generated




                                           – 155 –
solely by the rotation cover and to count gauge-induced zero modes; the finite flux and
bundle data are checked separately.

D.1.7 Rényi’s Poisson characterisation and the Poisson mapping theorem [12,
13]. For a simple point process, the complete family of void probabilities determines the
law; for a Poisson process the void probability is exp(−µ(A)). We use this result in the
source-identification step: equality of the certified void probabilities identifies the Poisson
intensity measure, after which the source is pushed forward to the continuum. It does
not identify a hidden marked or dynamical completion; the non-identifiability example in
the dependency analysis shows why that stronger conclusion is unavailable. The Poisson
mapping theorem used in the next step says that the image of a Poisson process under
a measurable map is Poisson with the push-forward intensity (and remains simple when
the map has no atoms). This is exactly the source-to-continuum push-forward used in the
main text; it is not an additional dynamical claim.

D.1.8 Kotecký–Preiss polymer criterion [47]. Specify a symmetric incompatibility
relation γ ′ 6∼ γ, including self-incompatibility. A suﬀicient condition for absolute conver-
gence of the logarithmic cluster expansion is the existence of a(γ) ≥ 0 such that
                            X                 ′
                                 |z(γ ′ )|ea(γ ) ≤ a(γ) for every γ.                    (D.2)
                           γ ′ ̸∼γ

                                                                       P
For overlap-only incompatibility and a(γ) = a0 |γ|, the site bound supx γ∋x |z(γ)|ea0 |γ| ≤
a0 implies this criterion by summing over the sites of γ. Other incompatibility relations
require their own counting factor. This criterion controls the expansion; spatial decay,
a thermodynamic limit and a physical mass-gap transfer require additional uniform esti-
mates. The activities, domains and constants in the Villain and electroweak applications
must still be certified.

D.1.9 Tomboulis–Yaffe inequality and its SU (N ) form [72, 74]. Let Z[k] be the
partition function of a one-plaquette lattice gauge theory on a periodic box in which the
plaquettes of a twist stack Υ in the (µ, ν)-plane carry the centre factor z k , z = e2πi/N , and
                P −1
let F [0] = N −1 Nk=0 Z[k]/Z. For a rectangular loop C in that plane whose sides divide
Lµ and Lν by powers of two, and for a representation of nonzero N -ality,

                                |hW (C)i| ≤ 2 (1 − F [0] )AC /(Lµ Lν ) .                  (D.3)

The proof moves the stack across C by a centre change of variables, which multiplies the
loop by a centre phase, and then doubles the rectangle repeatedly with the Schwarz inequal-
ity for the site-reflection-positive measure until it covers the (µ, ν)-torus. The inequality is
used in Theorem 17.1 for the Wilson action, which is site reflection positive; it is not used
for the induced action of the model.

D.1.10 Fröhlich–Spencer and the Hartman–Watson identity [35, 38]. The
Fröhlich–Spencer representation rewrites the weakly coupled compact Abelian theory as




                                               – 156 –
a dual sine–Gordon/monopole gas and supplies the convergent expansion used for the
flux-variance lower bound. The Hartman–Watson identity

                                 Iν (β)/I0 (β) = E[e−ν T /2 ]
                                                       2
                                                                                       (D.4)

turns each Fourier mode into a Gaussian mixture with inverse variance 1/T . We use the
first result for the qualitative monopole estimate and the second for the exact posterior-
defect probabilities; all changes of variables, finite sums, and interval bounds are carried
out in the paper.

D.1.11 Combes–Thomas and Nielsen–Olesen [43, 44]. The Combes–Thomas esti-
mate gives exponential decay of the resolvent of a uniformly positive finite-range operator
away from its spectrum. It is applied to the charged-vector covariance at a fixed elec-
tromagnetic background, with the explicit gap condition m2W − 2eδ > 0. The Nielsen–
Olesen calculation identifies the unstable charged-vector mode when eB > m2W ; we use it
only to restrict the admissible magnetic-field window. Neither result proves the source-to-
continuum bridge or an unconditional mass gap.

D.1.12 Spectral homogenisation and Akcoglu–Krengel. Armstrong–Venkatraman
study the scalar graph Laplacian on Poisson point clouds and convergence of its spec-
trum [39]. Akcoglu–Krengel supplies the ergodic theorem for stationary integrable super-
additive processes [40]. Applying these results to the present face-flux/cochain energy is
not automatic: the operator, scaling, boundary conditions and comparison to the scalar
problem must be supplied, together with the required additivity inequalities and integra-
bility. Thus these references motivate the homogenisation route but do not, without those
identifications, certify the photon/cochain limit or its uniform coercivity.

D.1.13 Dobrushin, Föllmer, and positive-operator theorems [31, 70, 71]. Do-
brushin uniqueness (including its Wasserstein/Lipschitz form) says that an influence matrix
with row sums strictly below one has a unique Gibbs state and exponential covariance de-
cay. Föllmer’s covariance estimate converts the same contraction into a quantitative slab
bound. We apply these statements to the temporal-gauge conditional measure, with the
computed Haar-Wasserstein coeﬀicient and the explicit area-law inequality. Jentzsch’s
positive-operator theorem gives a simple maximal eigenvalue for a positive compact trans-
fer operator; it is used to identify the vacuum eigenvector before the spectral-gap estimate.
These results require the positivity, compactness, and influence bounds displayed in the
paper.

D.1.14 Balaban, Durhuus–Fröhlich, Reisz, and perturbative matching [45, 46,
48, 49, 93, 94]. Balaban’s gauge-covariant renormalisation estimates provide the form
of the large-field and polymer bounds used for a finite sequence of lattice blocks. Durhuus–
Fröhlich reduction identifies the three-dimensional S 3 sigma-model description of the four-
dimensional SU (2) area-law channel in the analytic window. Reisz’s theorem is applied
graph by graph only when the gauge-fixed lattice action is translation invariant and local
(or has suﬀiciently fast uniform decay), its vertex functions are periodic and suﬀiciently




                                          – 157 –
smooth in Brillouin-zone momenta with a regular continuum limit, and each propagator
denominator has the prescribed continuum scaling, a uniform lattice lower bound and
no additional zeros/doublers. After the required subtractions, the Reisz ultraviolet degree
must be negative for the graph and every Zimmermann subspace. Gauge fixing, ghosts and
mass/infrared regulators must preserve these properties before the regulator is removed.
The present induced closed-walk action has not been shown to satisfy this complete list
uniformly in the cutoff. Reisz power counting therefore gives a conditional perturbative
comparison, not an established universality theorem for that action. Published three-loop
Standard-Model beta functions and matching formulae remain external numerical input
for the Higgs-stability interval.

D.1.15 Prokhorov tightness and Kolmogorov extension [55]. The two theorems
perform different tasks and are not used as an automatic two-step argument. Prokhorov
applies to Borel probability measures already defined on one fixed Polish space and converts
tightness with respect to the compact subsets of that topology into relative weak compact-
ness. Tightness of each finite-dimensional projection alone is insuﬀicient on a function or
distribution space; one also needs compact containment and the topology-specific tail or
modulus estimates that make the full family tight. Kolmogorov extension instead starts
with probability measures on finite products of standard Borel coordinate spaces, invariant
under coordinate permutations and projectively consistent under every coordinate deletion,
and yields a probability measure on the product sigma algebra. It does not by itself put
the measure on a smaller continuous-path, Sobolev or tempered-distribution subspace, nor
make it Radon in that stronger topology. In Theorem 12.1 the measures are assumed
to be Borel probabilities on the common Polish Sobolev-distribution space, so Prokhorov
applies directly and Kolmogorov extension is unnecessary. Kolmogorov is used only for a
construction specified solely by finite-dimensional laws; support and regularity must then
be proved separately before Prokhorov is invoked in the stronger topology.

D.1.16 Källen–Lehmann and Newton iteration. The Källen–Lehmann/Stieltjes
representation [52, 53] says that a reflection-positive two-point function is the Stieltjes
transform of a positive spectral measure. We use it as a consistency test for the averaged
Schwinger function; a numerical fit is not itself a proof of a gap. The Newton–Kantorovich
contraction theorem [95] is used in its standard local form: an inverse-Hessian bound and
a Lipschitz bound on the Hessian imply a unique solution in the certified ball. The paper
applies this inequality separately in each weak-coupling block and records the radius and
constants.

D.1.17 Mayer–Vietoris, Weyl/Hodge decomposition, and Poincaré inequali-
ties [85, 86]. The Mayer–Vietoris exact sequence computes the change in homology
after a vertex star (a cone) is attached; the displayed sequence in the homogenisation
appendix reduces a possible new first-homology class to disconnected components of the
link. The discrete Weyl/Hodge decomposition splits a cochain into an exact, coexact, and
harmonic part, so the graph energy can be estimated on the orthogonal complement of the
kernel. The large-scale Poincaré inequality then bounds a 1-chain by its coboundary after




                                          – 158 –
the harmonic part is removed. These are standard deterministic statements; the random
constants and the percolation hypotheses used to make them uniform are the conditional
inputs listed above.

D.1.18 Cauchy–Crofton, Stokes, Gauss–Bonnet, and reflection chessboard es-
timates [33, 34, 92, 96]. The Cauchy–Crofton formula converts the length/area of a
filling into an average of intersection counts and is used for the flux-filling bound. Stokes’
theorem turns the integral of a curl on the torus into the boundary flux, and Gauss–Bonnet
gives zero total Gaussian curvature for the embedded torus; these are the two no-chirality
checks. Reflection positivity also yields the chessboard (reflection-copy) inequality, which
we use to turn a one-block bound into a volume-uniform polymer bound. None of these
identities supplies the missing random connectivity or source-selection assumptions.

D.1.19 Von Mises–Fisher, Kato, and maximum-principle estimates [97, 98].
The von Mises–Fisher variance estimate bounds the change of a single S 3 spin under a
neighbour tilt and yields the Dobrushin coeﬀicient (d − 1)β. Kato’s inequality and the
parabolic maximum principle control the heat-flow gradient/Hessian differential inequali-
ties; the resulting Riccati comparison is solved explicitly in the paper. These are analytic
inequalities applied to the displayed finite-dimensional operators, not additional assump-
tions about the source.
     Elementary tools such as Tonelli’s theorem, Cauchy–Schwarz, the spectral theorem,
the Schur product theorem, and Sherman–Morrison are used in their standard forms; their
finite-dimensional applications are written out. Every use of an external input ends in a
displayed inequality, a finite certificate or a named conditional hypothesis, so the model-
specific step is always visible.

D.2   Gauge and matter sector
D.2.1 Finite spatial-move-sequence renormalisation criterion (Theorem 6.8).
Let V be the finite spatial-move-sequence space, let B : V → V be one blocking matrix,
and let Ev = h1, viπ 1 be the projection onto the invariant direction. Put K = B − E. If B
commutes with the right S4 action, K decomposes into Wedderburn blocks. For each block,
a Sturm sign-variation computation showing that every nontrivial root of its characteristic
polynomial lies in |z| < 1 gives constants C and q < 1 with

                                        kK n k ≤ Cq n .                                 (D.5)

Thus B n → E and the fixed point is unique. This argument applies only to the finite
matrix defined in the paper; the actual rational Sturm table is a Lean certificate, not an
implicit appeal to an infinite spatial-move-sequence statement.

D.2.2 Physical-time lift (Theorem 6.9). Decompose c = c∥ + c⊥ into the right-
                                         P                      P
equivariant and orthogonal parts. For T = g ag Lg Dc and E(T ) = g ag Lg Dc∥ ,
                                               X
                                 T − E(T ) =        a g L g D c⊥ .                      (D.6)
                                                g




                                           – 159 –
The triangle inequality and kDc⊥ k ≤ Kkc⊥ k, together with the lower bound
        p
K −1 kck 1 − f (u)2 for the least singular value of E(T ), give the ratio bound in the
main text. Blocking preserves the equivariant part and sends c⊥ (u/2) = c⊥ (u)/2, hence
f (u/2) ≤ f (u)/2 and the defect decays geometrically.

D.2.3 Vacuum catalogue and exclusion of an extra U (1) (Propositions 6.15,
6.16). First fix the finite range of allowed wrapping numbers. For each integer array
evaluate tadpoles, intersection numbers, the chiral spectrum, Yukawa selection rules, and
anomaly cancellation in that order. Quotient the passing arrays by stack relabelling and
the finite group action. For every surviving array, rational Gaussian elimination of the
Green–Schwarz matrix gives
                                    dim ker MGS = 1,                                (D.7)
and direct substitution identifies the remaining null vector with hypercharge. Hence no
additional massless U (1) remains. The finite wrapping list, matrices, and row-reduction
certificate are essential finite inputs to this proposition and are specified in the accompa-
nying calculation and Lean materials.

D.2.4 Non-fixing of couplings and failure of reverse derivation (Theorem 6.17,
Proposition 6.18). The allowed data satisfy

                                   αx−1 = κA (ΛR)3 Vx (χ).                             (D.8)

If the admissibility equations do not fix χ and ΛR, continuity and nonconstancy of Vx
produce two allowed points with different couplings. Thus the coupling is not derived
uniquely. Matching four observables by choosing four moduli is an additional observational
selection, not a consequence of the source dynamics.

D.3   Electromagnetic sector
D.3.1 Villain U (1) nonconfinement (Theorem 8.3). Nonconfinement means that
large Wilson loops do not obey an area law. For the Villain action with βV ≥ 1.614 the
Kotecký–Preiss criterion holds for the monopole polymer gas, so the cluster expansion
converges and yields, for a loop γ,

                                hW (γ)i ≥ exp(−C Per(γ)).                              (D.9)

This is incompatible with exp(−cR2 ). The Kotecký–Preiss margin is the interval certificate
of the Villain photon theorem; the cluster-expansion bound is the external input [35]. The
Wilson action at βt = 3.643 is not covered.

D.3.2 Hartman–Watson mixture (Lemma 8.6). Using the external Hartman–
Watson identity Iν (β)/I0 (β) = E[e−ν T /2 ], each Fourier mode is conditionally a
                                           2


Gaussian/Villain mode with inverse variance 1/T . Tonelli’s theorem then gives the
mixture formula. The probability P (βV < b) is the integral of T > 1/b, and the inverse
mean is E[T ]. The identity is a cited external input; the change of variables is an internal
step, and the interval evaluations are recorded in the supplement.




                                          – 160 –
D.3.3 Rank-one defect (Lemma 8.9). With E(x) = x · Gx for symmetric G and
λ = (u · Gm)/(u · Gu), expanding the quadratic form gives E(m − λu) = E(m) − 2λ u ·
Gm + λ2 u · Gu = E(m) − (u · Gm)2 /(u · Gu). No matrix inversion is needed. If G is
positive definite, then u · Gu > 0, so the energy does not increase, with equality exactly
when u · Gm = 0.

D.3.4 Poisson Gaussian photon and cell bounds (Theorems 8.10, 8.13, 8.15).
Restricting the gauge field to ker d∗1 turns the energy into the scalar graph-Laplacian
quadratic form. The external spectral homogenisation theorem gives a deterministic
quadratic form qhom . Poisson O(4) invariance reduces it to the two self-dual/anti-self-dual
coeﬀicients, and reflection symmetry identifies them. The positive bound qhom (F ) ≥ |F |2
implies S(k) ≥ c/|k|2 ; the Gaussian variance identity then gives a massless photon. The
finite d1 , d∗1 matrices, projections, and positivity certificate are the model-specific proof
data.

D.4   Electroweak and strong sectors
D.4.1 All-order fixed-background convergence (Theorem 9.4). Under the hy-
                                                ′
pothesis of Theorem 9.3, kGA (x, y)k ≤ C0 e−m |x−y| . Insert this estimate on every internal
                                                                      Q
line of an n-vertex connected graph. Local vertex bounds give C0I v |gv | times an inte-
grable product of exponentials. Summing the finitely many graphs at each order gives
        ′
Cn e−m |x−y| . This is conditional on the fixed background and uniform propagator bound.

D.4.2 Electroweak uniqueness and reduction (Theorem 9.5, Proposition 9.6).
The finite electroweak inputs and their numerical margins are stated in §9.4; they are
finite, fixed-cutoff statements. The joint gauge–Higgs norm, uniqueness, cutoff removal,
the pull-back to the unrestricted source and the 53-step composition remain open. If
                                     X
                                 sup    |z(γ)|ea|γ| ≤ η < 1,                      (D.10)
                                  x γ∋x


the Kotecký–Preiss polymer sum converges absolutely. The difference of two finite-volume
log partition functions is bounded by polymers touching the boundary, and this bound
vanishes as the boundary recedes. The 53-step reduction preserves the same inequality at
each blocking step; if the source-specific operator bounds and norm transfer are certified,
the final mass scale would supply the electroweak hypothesis. The small-field analyticity
and irrelevant-polymer contraction constants remain estimates that must be certified.

D.4.3 Higgs stability and the general Yang–Mills condition (Proposition 9.19,
Proposition 10.2). For Higgs stability, propagate outward-rounded intervals through
the three-loop beta functions and matching maps. Induction on the scales proves that the
upper interval endpoint of λSM never crosses zero; interval bisection gives the critical top
mass. If αs (Λ) = 1/45, then g 2 = 4π/45 and β = 6/g 2 = 270/(4π) ≃ 21.5. Thus the strong
sector starts in the weak-coupling lattice regime. Starting at strong coupling would make
the confinement scale of order the cutoff, so the remaining assertion is exactly the ordinary
four-dimensional SU (3) Yang–Mills mass-gap problem, not a model-specific proof of it.




                                           – 161 –
D.5   Yang–Mills sector
D.5.1 Haar Wasserstein constant and boundary absorption (Yang–Mills follow-
up lemma). Writing the Haar measure on S 3 with u1 = t reduces the Lipschitz covari-
ance optimisation to a one-dimensional cumulative-distribution problem. The extremiser is
f ∗ = arcsin t = π/2 − d(u, e), a 1-Lipschitz function of the geodesic distance to the identity,
and evaluating the covariance at f ∗ gives 8/(9π). For a boundary link e = (v, w) changed
from Ue to gUe , the internal gauge transformation hv = g and h = 1 elsewhere moves the
change to that link only. Haar invariance and gauge invariance of the observable leave the
interior law unchanged.

D.5.2 Selection structure and weak-coupling chain (Yang–Mills follow-up lem-
mas and conditional theorems). Haar orthogonality kills every non-invariant repre-
sentation, so only the stated parity/selection structures survive. Write Sj+1 = SjW +Rj and
                                         P
assume kRj k ≤ Cq j with q < 1. Then j Rj converges absolutely and loop expectations
are Cauchy. If the matching transfer operator satisfies kT∗ |Ω⊥ k ≤ e−m , functional calculus
gives H = − log T∗ and H|Ω⊥ ≥ m. The conclusion is conditional on the intermediate-
window covariance bound and the qualitative one-loop universality input.

D.5.3 Confinement and the vortex free energy (Lemmas 17.2, 17.3, 17.4, The-
orem 17.1). Internal steps: Haar invariance of the block constraint gives the exact flow
of Z[k]/Z; block alignment makes every averaged line cross a block-aligned hyperplane
once, so centre changes of variables commute with the block maps; block-local polymer
bounds are invariant under such changes of variables block by block, so they hold in
every twist sector; in the terminal polymer gas a polymer that misses some position of
the (µ, ν)-torus has its twist removed by such a change of variables, so only polymers
                  (n) (n)
with at least 12 Lµ Lν links distinguish Z[k] from Z, and the Kotecký–Preiss tail gives
                          (n)   (n)
1 − Z[k]/Z ≤ c|Λn |e−aLµ Lν /2 . External inputs: the Kotecký–Preiss criterion (§D.1.8)
and the Tomboulis–Yaffe inequality (§D.1.9). Premise: the vortex condition (VF), which
the window condition (RG) implies. The box limit p → ∞ is an explicit computation.

D.6    Homogenisation appendix
D.6.1 Locality, anchored dual, and stationary Weyl decomposition (Lem-
mas A.2, A.3, A.4). Adding one vertex appends one row and column to the boundary
matrix. Schur complementation removes the interior variable and leaves only adjacent
boundary terms, proving locality of a birth event and preservation of positive definiteness
under a fixed dual boundary. If a cycle c ⊂ Br is nonzero in H1 (K), relative duality
supplies η ∈ H 1 (K, K \ B2r ) with hη, ci 6= 0; truncating a cocycle representative gives the
anchored lemma. Under (T), (L), and (P), remove the extended H 1 component, represent
the remaining local cocycle by finitely many hole fields, and use sublinearity to show that
the residual harmonic gradient is constant. Hence every stationary harmonic one-form is
a constant field plus local hole fields.

D.6.2 Upper and high-density bounds (Theorems A.5, A.6). Split the cell energy
into independent-cell terms and boundary cross-terms. A Poincaré estimate bounds the
latter by C|∂QR |kF k2 , giving the stated subadditivity, and the Akcoglu–Krengel theorem
yields the limit of R−4 ER (Theorem A.5). In the high-density regime (Theorem A.6),
summing empty-ball and cluster tails makes the same boundary contribution finite. The
percolation and filling inputs remain explicit hypotheses.

D.7    Boundary of the derived statements
The small-field analyticity estimate, irrelevant-polymer contraction, intermediate-window
covariance bound, source-to-Wilson universality bridge and the eight source-to-continuum
conditions are not consequences of the finite derivations above. Every conclusion using one
of them is therefore conditional on the corresponding estimate; the finite calculations alone
establish only the explicitly stated algebraic identities and certified bounds.

E     Finite-model certificates and continuum boundaries

The construction used in this paper has a finite, bounded-geometry version and a more
ambitious literal-torus source interpretation. They should not be identified. The certifi-
cates below concern the finite bounded-geometry model; archive-specific file names and
calculation identifiers are kept in the reproducibility package.

E.1    Canonical finite bounded-geometry source
The declared finite source uses
       D ∗ = H∗ × E∗ ,     H∗ = {1} ∪ Trans(S4 ),       E∗ = {0, ±c2 , ±c3 , ±c5 },     (E.1)
so |H∗ | = |E∗ | = 7 and |D∗ | = 49. The symbols c2 , c3 , c5 are three independent clock-
alphabet marks; they are not the coordinate vectors ei used to write the D4 roots.

E.3   Finite results and their scope
The following statements are proved for the explicitly declared canonical finite source:

• the finite selector/measure, history retention and same-history-space Markov semigroup;

• finite reflection positivity and the finite electroweak L3 /L4 realisation;

• the finite bounded-vertex Ward tail, with

     Lean 4 counterpart of Theorem E.5 (Periodic coexact compactness): TorusModel.periodic_fourier_

• the periodic D4 coexact coercivity

                                      λmin (L) = 4 sin2 (π/L),                          (E.17)

   and the corresponding deterministic discrete Rellich compactness lemma;

• the finite Wilson quadratic photon pole and the matched Villain signed P1 realisation;
  and

• the finite metric/coframe response, U (3)×U (2)-capable link realisation, and an anomaly-
  free chiral/Higgs/Yukawa realisation.

      For the finite electroweak estimate, the interval certificate gives

          B16 ≤ 7.260041057,        sup BL ≤ 8.464,         Kworst = 0.40 < 0.75.       (E.18)
                                    L≥16

These numbers establish the stated bounds for the declared finite realisation, not the
unrestricted source-level electroweak theorem.

E.4    Continuum boundary and raw-source non-identifiability
The raw-source non-identifiability result is an information theorem, not a numerical fit. The
raw torus tuple does not specify a state measure, selector, reflection involution, blocking
map, site-link measure, state-dependent current or chiral module. Explicit completion
pairs with the same raw tuple give incompatible RP signs, metric ranks, gauge sectors and
matter sectors. Consequently

                          Sraw 6⇒ (µ∗ , Θ, ΦRG , Wyx , K[X], Fchiral , H).              (E.19)

Declaring Cfin defines the source-to-finite map of this model; it does not derive that package
from the unrestricted raw tuple.
     The complementary continuum obstruction is that the naively scaled massless clock
Dirichlet energy grows as L2 /κt , so H 1 -type tightness fails without renormalisation or a
different topology. The free/finite pinned clock is nevertheless tight in H −s for every s > 1,
with Fourier tail O(K 2−2s ). This is not yet an interacting raw-source compactness theorem.

E.5    Relation to the physical hypotheses
The certificates above concern the declared finite source. Their relation to the four physical
hypotheses is stated once in §2.3, and the source-to-continuum premises are stated in
Theorem 12.1 and Table 6. In particular, the finite reflection-positive, electroweak and
matched-Villain results do not establish the corresponding unrestricted-source statements
or the Yang–Mills continuum gap.

Boundary of the appendix. The declared package completes the canonical finite
model; it neither identifies that package with the raw torus source nor proves an interacting
all-volume continuum limit.




                                             – 168 –
F    Formal verification and Lean correspondence

The formal counterpart is checked against a Lean 4 project using mathlib. It is kept in
the public repository https://github.com/s6ygleee/torus-node-lean4 and archived
on Zenodo under doi:10.5281/zenodo.23863514 [11]. The Yang–Mills follow-up paper has its
own formalisation, released with that paper. The project verifies explicitly listed subclaims;
they are not a machine-checked proof of every statement in this paper.

                                      2. Formal objects
                                                                      3. Internal derivation
    1. Reader-facing claim             and assumptions
                                                                         definitions, alge-
      theorem, lemma, or             source, D4 /S4 data,
                                                                        braic lemmas, and
       finite calculation             fields, action, and
                                                                       finite dependencies
                                      stated hypotheses



                                                              4b. Conditional in-
               4a. Exact finite verification
                                                               terface verification
             D4 /S4 algebra, dimension, gauge
                                                       source push-forward, homogeni-
            covariance, EW masses, positivity,
                                                         sation, Ward/Hodge/Rellich
               anomalies, and enumeration
                                                          estimates, and OS premises



                                     5. Verification checks                                    independent
               Lean elaboration and build, no-placeholder and dependency/axiom                 checks, tracked
             checks, statement mapping, and separately tracked independent review              separately


                                     6. Evidence classification
                exact finite counterpart; conditional result under stated premises;
                  external theorem or certified computation; or open interface


                                     7. Reader-facing record
                 the paper gives the mathematical statement and proof; the sup-
                   plement gives the exact lookup key and reproducibility data

Figure 33: Categorised verification path. The formal work proceeds from a reader-facing mathe-
matical claim to the finite objects and assumptions on which it depends, then to internal derivations.
Exact finite statements and conditional interface statements are kept as separate branches before
the machine checks and the final evidence classification. A successful build certifies only the listed
formal statements and their dependencies; it does not convert an external theorem or an open
source–continuum interface into an internally proved result.

     The formalisation has a conditional scope: it covers the listed Lean statements and
their dependencies, but it does not formalise every assertion in the paper. Two independent
machine checks support those statements. The first is the Lean kernel itself, run during the
build. The second replays the compiled declarations outside Lean: every project module
is exported with lean4export and re-checked by the independent type checker nanoda,
in bounded batches of modules together with all of their transitive dependencies. All 84
project modules were checked in eleven batches; nanoda reported no type-checking errors.
Neither check decides whether a Lean statement says the same thing as the surrounding
prose; that correspondence is reviewed separately and still requires human approval.




                                                    – 169 –
    The formal record has the following content.

• The Lean 4.32.1 project builds without errors, and the placeholder, dependency, axiom
  and statement-mapping checks pass for all 296 inventory declarations. The inventory
  lists formal statements; it is not the count of results in the paper.

• The main paper contains 112 theorem-like environments: 4 explicit hypotheses and 108
  other environments, namely 10 definitions and 98 results, each result with a local proof.
  Of these 108 environments, 12 are formalised exactly, 54 through a finite or conditional
  core, and 38 through formalised lemmas or cited external results; the four results on
  confinement under the window hypothesis in the Yang–Mills interface section have no
  Lean counterpart, and the four hypotheses are not formalised. The classification is
  complete, so every stated result has a recorded formal status.

• The only axioms reported for the audited declarations are the standard Lean axioms
  propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound. An external mathematical theorem used
  in a proof is therefore an explicit hypothesis of the corresponding Lean statement, not
  a hidden axiom.

• The Yang–Mills follow-up is a separate project with its own build and checks; it is not
  part of the present verification count.

The formalisation therefore checks the listed formal statements and their machine depen-
dencies. The source-to-continuum bridge and the remaining certified numerical-to-analytic
interfaces are open obligations rather than hidden assumptions.
     Each row begins with a reader-facing mathematical statement. The second column
gives the exact machine lookup key used by the corresponding Lean checker. Namespaces
and qualified identifiers such as TorusModel.FiniteCompletion.* are repository-local im-
plementation names, not mathematical notation or physical model names. They are re-
tained verbatim only so that the formal correspondence can be reproduced; the first column
is the authoritative description for readers of the theory.

External results and formal boundary. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction,
Osterwalder–Seiler reflection positivity, Lovelock’s classification, Rényi’s Poisson charac-
terisation, the Poisson mapping theorem, the Kolmogorov cylinder-extension theorem,
Prokhorov tightness, Kotecký–Preiss, Fröhlich–Spencer, Akcoglu–Krengel, Armstrong–
Venkatraman, Penrose–Pisztora, Antal–Pisztora, Liggett–Schonmann–Stacey, Timár,
Dobrushin, Föllmer, Jentzsch–Perron–Frobenius and Atiyah–Singer/Riemann–Roch are
explicit external inputs [12–15, 18, 29, 31, 35, 39, 40, 42, 47, 51–53, 55, 70, 71, 87–90], never




                                            – 172 –
hidden as axiom. Their hypotheses and the conclusions imported at each use are stated in
the main paper and its proof-dependency appendix. The Yang–Mills follow-up paper gives
the paper-level proofs of the Dobrushin boundary-influence theorem and the two-point
total-variation lemma. Certified computations (interval thresholds, Sturm sequences,
integer kernels) and conditional statements are the next layers of the formalisation.
Numerical results are not formalised.




                                        – 173 –
G   Computational data and reproducibility index

This appendix lists every result of the paper that depends on a computation, together
with the computation that supports it and its evidence class. The names in the middle
column are the names of the distributed files: scripts and stored outputs are in numerics/
scripts_and_inputs/ and numerics/outputs_and_logs/, and certificate records are in
the certificates/ directory of the reproducibility package. The locked runners re-run
every script and compare the result with the stored output (Appendix H).
     The evidence classes are: exact, a computation in exact integer or rational arithmetic,
a finite exact enumeration, or a closed-form identity proved in the text, in which case a
distributed floating-point program replays the identity and is not itself the proof; interval-
certified, an outward-rounded interval computation (Arb); error-bounded, a floating-point
computation with an explicit rigorous error bound; finite counterexample, an explicit finite
witness that a stated kernel is not positive; and numerical, a floating-point computation
without a rigorous error bound. A numerical entry is evidence, not a proof.

    The matched Villain cross-check is deliberately classified as a numerical diagnostic. Its
descriptive scope and the absence of an executable script, standalone input, and execu-
tion log are recorded in supplement/reproducibility/diagnostics/matched_villain_
crosscheck.md. It is not a certificate for the integer Wilson action.

Numerical illustrations. Some statements of the paper are illustrated by numerical
calculations that the argument does not use: the averaged reflection-positivity tests, the
bounce and Page-curve models, the heat-flow negative-mode sampling, the Hartman–
Watson tail values and the weak-triangle loss estimates. They are described where they
appear and are not part of the reproducibility package.

     Of these computations, only the vacuum catalogue is an exhaustive finite integer enu-
meration. The other rows combine the catalogue with running, geometric or observational
inputs, so their values depend on those inputs as well as on the source. The searches on
the matching surfaces are numerical: the asymptotic scale is the solution of an explicit
limiting equation, but no proof is supplied that it is the global infimum; the lepton fit
uses truncated Gaussian sums at selected separations and is not a uniqueness certificate;
the quark overlap test is a pre-registered test whose failure is the reported result; and the
gravity–gauge selection is established on the sampled matching surfaces, not on the full
continuous parameter domain. The formal verification of the finite statements is described
in Appendix F.
     For the matched Villain cross-check the archive retains only a descriptive scope
note. The explicit absence of a script, standalone input, and execution log is recorded
in supplement/reproducibility/diagnostics/matched_villain_crosscheck.md; the
values are not treated as an exact certificate or as a result for the integer Wilson action.
